Commentarius in Cantica canticorum
by Ambrose of Milan
Source text: Public Domain. English translation/apparatus/encoding: CC BY-NC 4.0, Wroot Press.
https://migne.app/pl/15/commentarius-in-cantica-canticorum/
----------------------------------------

## PROLOGUE.

[1851]

[1851A] *1545* 1. *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth [n: (Cant. I, 1)]*: here it is not the incitements of foulness, but the mysteries of chastity, that are celebrated; where the matter is handled spiritually, and by a certain contemplation of the affections, concerning the marriage and the joining of Christ and the Church, of the uncreated Spirit and the created, of flesh and spirit. Let us therefore circumcise our heart: let us seek nothing base, nothing bodily; and everything earthly is base. Let us therefore set down in these heavenly utterances nothing earthly, nothing carnal, nothing worldly, nothing bodily, nothing light and changeable: for *the words of the Lord are chaste words;* that in them the spotless and modest sincerity of the heavenly mysteries may *1546* shine forth by a spiritual interpretation. Let us not mingle earthly things with divine by some adulterous notion, nor violate that inviolable mystery of prophetic vision or of the everlasting oracle by an estimate drawn from our own nature: for whether it be of kisses or of such things, the whole of it is seemly, whatever is offered to religion; so that we blush at [1852A] no service that avails toward the worship and the observance of Christ: as in that dance of David before the ark; for that dancing was no companion of delights and of wantonness, but that each man should lift up an unsluggish body, and not let his limbs lie sluggish on the ground, or grow numb with slow steps. For this mastery we did not find out, but received; thus has heavenly doctrine established the order of the mystic song. *1547*

## 2.

*Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth; for thy breasts are good above wine, and the odor of thine ointments above all spices. Thy name is an ointment emptied out [n: (Ibid.)]*. [var: Vulg. *osculo oris sui*, "with the kiss of his mouth," singular and without *ab*; *meliora sunt ubera tua vino*, "better than wine"; *fraglantia unguentis optimis*, "fragrant with the best ointments"; *oleum effusum nomen tuum*, "oil poured out"] This whole passage of delights rings of play, stirs up applause, provokes love. *Therefore,* she says, *the maidens have loved thee and have drawn thee; let us run behind the odor of thine ointments. The king has brought me into his tabernacle [n: (Ibid., 3)]*. [var: Vulg. *trahe me post te; curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum*, "draw me after thee: we will run," an imperative; and *introduxit me rex in cellaria sua*, "into his storerooms"] She began from kisses that she might arrive at the tabernacle; and she, impatient of the Lord's labor and of exercised virtue, [1853A] is driven to open the bars with her hand, to go out into the field, to abide in the villages; yet at the beginning she runs behind the odor of the ointment, and when she has come into the tabernacle she will exchange the ointment for the villages. See, then, where she comes out.

## 3.

*If she is a wall,* he says, *let us build upon her towers of silver [n: (Cant. VIII, 9)]*. [var: Vulg. *propugnacula argentea*, "bulwarks of silver"] She who was playing with kisses now raises up towers, so that, turreted with the precious pinnacles of the saints, she may not only frustrate the assaults of enemies, but may also build safe bulwarks of good merits. Even in these temporal marriages the bride is applauded before she is commanded, lest harsh commands give offense before love, warmed by caresses, has grown into her. The heifer learns to love the sound of a certain clapping, that she may not refuse the yoke upon her neck; and so she is first accustomed to the word *1548* of playfulness before the lash of discipline. But when she has put her neck [1853B] under the yoke, the rein also constrains her, and the goad presses, and her yokemates draw, and the yoke-fellow invites. So too our Virgin ought first to hammer with holy love; to marvel at the golden supports of the heavenly couch, and in the very porch [1854A] of the wedding to behold the doorposts crowned with garlands of leafage, and to drink in the delights of the choir sounding within; lest, made afraid, she withdraw herself from the Lord's yoke before, being called, she bow to it. For we must know certain degrees of advancement, and their order — what ought to come first and what after: for to know what thou shouldst do, and not to know the order of doing it, is not of perfect knowledge; for things done back to front most often give offense. And so, since there are as many opinions as there are men, if anything in our discourse has been cleared, let all read it; if anything is boiled down, let the riper approve it; if anything is modest, let it cling to their breasts and paint their cheeks; if anything is flowery, let the flowering age not disapprove it. We ought to have stirred up the love of the bride; for it is written: *Thou shalt love the Lord thy God [n: (Deut. VI, 5)]*: [1854B] we ought to have adorned the bride's hair at the wedding with certain curling-irons of prayer; for it is written: *Clap with the hand, and strike with the foot [n: (Ezech. XXI, 14)]*: we ought to have strewn the everlasting bridal chambers with roses.

## HERE BEGINS THE TEXT.



## CHAPTER ONE.

[1853B] *1547*

## 1.

Life is to be sought before doctrine; for a good life has grace even without doctrine, while doctrine without life has not wholeness. *For wisdom does not fall into an ill-willing soul [n: (Sap. I, 4)]*: [var: Vulg. *non introibit sapientia*, "will not enter in"] and therefore he says: *The wicked shall seek me, and shall not find [n: (Prov. I, 28)]*: [var: Vulg. *tunc invocabunt me et non exaudiam; mane consurgent et non invenient me*, "then shall they call upon me, and I will not hear"] because the eye of the mind is blinded by wickedness, and, with iniquity darkening it, it cannot find the deep mysteries. First, therefore, the soldiery of life must be exercised, [1853C] and morals corrected. When we have set these things toward their due course, so that there may be correction of offense and the grace of purity, then let us come to the pursuits of taking in knowledge, in their own order and way. First, then, come the moral matters, second the mystical: in the former is life, in the latter knowledge; so that, if thou seek perfection, there be neither life without knowledge nor knowledge without life, but each bear witness with the other. Following this arrangement, Solomon wrote the book of Proverbs, in which he set forth the moral topic more richly, the natural in Ecclesiastes, the mystical in the Song of Songs: although, if thou examine diligently, thou wouldst find very many mystical things in Proverbs too, and in the Song the sweetness of moral matters. For surely this is mystical: *Wisdom has built herself a house, and has set seven columns beneath it; she has slain her victims, [1853D]* etc. [n: (Pov. IX, 1)]. [var: Vulg. *excidit columnas septem; immolavit victimas suas*, "hewed out seven columns; sacrificed her victims"] For in the Song that shines as mystical which is moral, wherein the sweetness of endearments and the affection of a lover is expressed.

## 2.

*[n: (Vers. 1, 2.)] Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth; since thy breasts are best above wine, and the odor of thine ointments above all spices.* [var: Vulg. *meliora sunt ubera tua vino*; the plate has printed the same lemma with *bona*, "good," above] Set before thee, then, a Virgin betrothed a long while, and burning with a just love, who has come to know many glorious works of her beloved by the assertion of approved witnesses; often put off, her desires held in suspense, bearing delays no longer, who would have done all things so that she might [sic: *deret*] the bridegroom: [1854B] who at length, having obtained her vows, troubled with joy at the unforeseen coming of the bridegroom, does not seek the first beginnings of greeting, nor the exchanges of words, but at once demands what she had desired. So then also the holy Church, betrothed in the first beginnings of the world in paradise, prefigured in the flood, announced through the Law, called through the prophets, when she had long awaited the redemption of men, the beauty of the Gospel, the coming of her beloved, impatient of delay, rushes into kisses, saying: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth;* and, delighted with the kisses, [1854C] she adds: *since thy breasts are best above wine.*

## 3.

And to speak more morally, I understand for myself that flesh which had been steeped in Adam with the serpent's venom, which was withering with the stench of crimes, which went forth among the daughters of Sion with neck held high and with beckonings of the eyes, trailing her tunics in the going of her feet and playing with her own feet, with ringlets of hair and composed countenances and headbands, and in every affected comeliness the more unseemly; and yet that same flesh, taught by very many *1548* oracles that he would come who, when the serpent's enticements had been shut out, would pour in the grace of the Holy Spirit, so that all flesh might see the salvation of God, that all flesh might come to God — that it had withered with longing: but fearing lest, as impatient, as wanton, as luxurious, as querulous, it should displease as it had been before, [1854D] although it was shaken by an expectation, longer than it could now bear, of the Lord's delaying coming: yet not murmuring nor transgressing, but lifting up pure hands in every place, without wrath and dispute, in adorned habit with modesty and sobriety, adorning herself not with plaited hair or gold or pearls or costly raiment, but with those things that befit the grace of chastity and of good conversation, praying and saying: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth; since thy breasts are best above wine.* Now the flesh wished to cleave to Christ, now she hastened to be wedded; [1855A] that she might be one spirit, and that she might become the flesh of Christ who before was a harlot's. *Let him kiss me,* she says: the Word of God kisses us when the spirit of knowledge illumines our understanding.

## 4.

Therefore it is either the Church, held in suspense through many seasons by the Lord's coming long since promised to her through the prophets, or the soul which, raising herself from the body, luxury and delights and carnal pleasures being renounced, stripped also of the anxiety of worldly vanities, has this long while yearned for the inpouring of the divine presence into her and for the grace of the saving Word: that she is worn away because he comes late, and is afflicted; and therefore, as one wounded with charity, since she cannot bear his delays, turning to the Father, that she asks God to send her the Word, and declares the cause why she is so impatient, saying: *Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth.*



## 5.

She does not seek one kiss, but many kisses, that she may be able to fulfil her desire; for she who loves is [1855B] not content with the scantness of a single kiss, but demands very many, claims more, and has been wont thus to commend herself the more to her beloved. And so she in the Gospel was thus approved: *Because she has not ceased,* says the Lord, *to kiss my feet; and therefore many sins are forgiven her, because she has loved much [n: (Luc. VII, 46, 47)]*. [var: Vulg. *remittuntur ei peccata multa quoniam dilexit multum*, a present "are forgiven"] So this soul also desires many kisses of the Word, that she may be enlightened with the light of divine knowledge; for this is the kiss of the Word, namely the light of sacred knowledge. For God the Word kisses us when the Spirit of divine knowledge illumines our heart and the very governing part of man; and the soul, endowed with this pledge of nuptial charity, glad and exulting, says: *I opened my mouth and drew forth the spirit [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 131)]*. [var: Vulg. *adtraxi spiritum*, "drew in the spirit"] For a kiss is that whereby lovers cleave to one another [1855C] and, as it were, take possession of the sweetness of an inward grace. Through this kiss the soul cleaves to the Word of God, in that the spirit of the one kissing is poured over into her: just as they also who kiss one another are not content with a foretaste of the lips, but seem to pour their own spirit into one another. *1549*

## 6.

*Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth.* Wilt thou fit this to Christ? Nothing is more welcome. Wilt thou fit it to thy own soul? Nothing is more delightful. *Let him kiss me*: he sees thee to be clean from every sin, since offenses are of the earth; therefore he judges thee worthy of the heavenly sacraments, and therefore invites thee to the heavenly banquet, that *he may kiss thee with the kisses of his mouth.* Yet, because of what follows, thy soul, or the human condition, or the Church, has seen herself cleansed from all sins, worthy to be able to approach [1855D] the altar of God. For what is the altar but the form of the body of Christ? She has seen the wondrous sacraments, and says: *Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth;* that is, let Christ fix a kiss upon me. Why? Because:

## 7.

*Thy breasts are better above wine,* [var: Vulg. *meliora sunt ubera tua vino*, "better than wine"; the plate has printed the same lemma with *bona* and with *optima* above] that is, thy senses are better, thy sacraments are better above wine — above that wine which, though it has sweetness, [1856A] though it has gladness, though it has grace, yet in it the gladness is worldly, while in thee the delight is spiritual. Even then, therefore, Solomon was introducing the marriage either of Christ and the Church, or of spirit and flesh and soul. And as though despising all her own delights and pleasures, desiring [sic: *cupieos*] to cleave to the heavenly commandments, she says: since the precepts of thy testaments are best above every appetite of the flesh and every pleasure of the world; for she remembered that she had thus fallen before, in Eve, while she set the pleasure of the body before the heavenly commandments. And so, showing that she loves not only the appearance of the Word and a certain countenance, but all his inward parts, she adds to the grace of the kisses: *Because thy breasts are good,* she says, *above wine, and the odor of thine ointments above all spices.* She asked for a kiss; God the Word poured himself wholly into her, and bared his breasts to her, that is, his doctrines and the disciplines of his inward wisdom, and was fragrant with the sweet odor of his ointments: [1856B] and, taken captive by these, she says that the delight of divine knowledge is more abundant than the gladness of every bodily pleasure.

## 8.

*Thy name is an ointment emptied out, or oil poured forth;* that is: this world was wholly stinking with the unclean impurities of divers crimes; now everywhere the sweetness of modesty breathes, the ointment of faith, the flower of integrity. There breathes also in the Word the odor of grace and the remission of sins, which, being poured out over the whole world, has filled all things as with an ointment emptied out, because through all men the heavy filth of vices has been wiped away. This ointment was emptied out above the Jews, and was gathered up for those acting: emptied out in Judaea, and it gave forth its scent in all lands. With this ointment Mary was anointed, and the Virgin conceived, the Virgin bore the good odor, the Son of God: this ointment was poured out upon [1856C] the waters, and sanctified the waters: with this ointment the three young men were anointed, and the flame bedewed them with moisture; with this ointment Daniel was anointed, and he softened the mouths of the lions and stroked down their wildness. This ointment flows daily, and never fails. Take thy vessel, O virgin, and draw near, that thou mayest be able to be filled with this ointment: take the ointment valued at three hundred pence, but given freely, not sold, that all might have it freely. O virgin, be anointed; be not made sorrowful as Judas was, because this ointment is poured out, but bury Christ together with thee: shut thy vessel indeed, lest the ointment flow away: shut it with the key of integrity, with modesty in speaking, with abstinence from boasting; she who has this ointment receives Christ: *Thy name is an ointment emptied out.* [1856D]

## 9.

*[n: (Vers. 2.)] Therefore the maidens have loved thee.* Who are these maidens, but the souls of individual men, who have laid aside the old age of this body, being renewed through the Holy Spirit? *Thy name is an ointment poured forth,* than the power of which utterance nothing can be more excellent. For just as ointment shut up in some vessel holds in its own odor — which odor, so long as it is confined within the narrows of that vessel, keeps its force, even though it [1857A] cannot reach many — but when the ointment has been poured out of the vessel in which it was shut, it is said to be spread far and wide: so also the name of Christ, before his coming, was shut up in the people of Israel, as it were in some vessel of the minds of the Jews: for *God is known in Judaea, in Israel his name is great. [n: (Psal. LXXV, I)]*. This name, therefore, which the vessels of the Jews, confined within their own narrows, contained. A great name indeed even then, when it clung within the narrows of the weak and the few, but it had not yet poured out its greatness through the hearts of the nations and unto the ends of the whole world: *1550* but after he shone forth by his coming through the whole world, he stretched out that divine name of his through the whole creation, not filled up by any addition — for fulness knows no increase — but filling the empty places, that his name might be wonderful in [1857B] all the earth. The pouring forth of this name, therefore, signifies a certain abounding overflow of graces and a plenty of heavenly goods; for whatever is poured out overflows out of abundance. *Therefore the maidens have loved thee.* Good, she says, is prudence, but sweet is mercy; for the first few attain, while the second reaches to all: for the sake of this thy indulgence, she says, the souls renewed in spirit love thee.

## 10.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] Draw us after thee; we will run in the odor of thine ointments.* [var: Vulg. *trahe me post te*, "draw me," singular; the exposition that follows turns on the plural "us"] The holy soul, having now kissed the Word of God, takes no measure and is not satisfied, saying: Sweet art thou, O Lord, and in thy delightfulness teach me thy justifications [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 12)]. Having kissed the Word of God, she desires him above all beauty, loves him above all gladness, delights in him above all [1857C] spices, longs to see him often, to gaze on him frequently, longs to be drawn that she may be able to follow: *Thy name,* she says, *is an ointment emptied out; therefore we maidens love thee;* therefore we strive, but we cannot lay hold: draw us, that we may be able to run; that from the odor of thine ointments we may receive strength to follow. She hastens also to see the inward mysteries, the very rest of the Word, the very dwelling-place of that highest good, and his light and brightness in that fatherly bosom and retreat; she hastens to hear his discourses, and when she has heard she receives him above all sweetness.

## 11.

Let the prophet teach thee who has tasted, and says: *How sweet are thy words to my throat, above honey and the honeycomb to my mouth [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 103)]*! [var: Vulg. *quam dulcia faucibus meis eloquia tua, super mel ori meo*, without "and the honeycomb"] For what else does the soul desire [1857D] that has once tasted the sweetness of the Word, that has once seen his brightness? Moses, set on the mountain for forty days receiving the Law, sought no food for the body: Elijah, hastening to that rest, prayed that his soul might be taken from him: Peter, himself also beholding on the mountain the glory of the Lord's resurrection, was unwilling to come down, saying: *Lord, it is good for us to be here [n: (Matth. XVII, 4)]*. How great, then, is that glory of the divine substance, how great the gifts of the Word, upon which the angels also desire to look!



## 12.

The soul, therefore, that sees that, does not seek this body, and understands that there ought to be [1858A] the least possible familiarity with it; she renounces the world, leads herself away from the bonds of the flesh, and strips herself of all the entanglements of these pleasures. And so Stephen was seeing Jesus, and did not dread to be stoned; nay, when he was being stoned, he prayed not for himself but for those by whom he was being destroyed. Paul too, caught up as far as the third heaven, did not know whether he was with the body or without the body; caught up, I say, into paradise, he no longer felt the use of his own body, and, hearing the words of God, blushed at how he should come down to the infirmities of the body. And so, knowing what he had seen in paradise, or what he had heard, he cried out, saying: *Why do you still, as though living out of this world, make decrees? Touch not, handle not, taste not, which things are all unto corruption by the very use [n: (Coloss. II, 20 et 21)]*. [var: Vulg. *ne tetigeritis neque gustaveritis neque contrectaveritis, quae sunt omnia in interitu ipso usu*; the three prohibitions stand in a different order, and "unto destruction"] For he wished us to be in the figure of this world, not in possession and use, [1858B] so that we might so use this world as though we used it not, as those passing by, not as those who settle; walking as in the image of the age, not in covetousness, that by a most swift reckoning we may pass beyond the very image of this world. And so he himself, walking by faith, not by sight, was a pilgrim away from the body and present with God: and when he was upon the earth, he had his conversation not in earthly but in heavenly things. Therefore let our soul, which wishes to draw near to God, lift herself up from the body, let her always cleave to that highest good, to that good which is divine, which is always, and which was from the beginning, and which was with God [n: (I Joan. I)], that is, the Word of God: this is that divine thing, *in which we live, and are, and are moved [n: (Act. XVII, 28)]*. [var: Vulg. *in ipso enim vivimus et movemur et sumus*, "live and are moved and are," the last two members in the other order]

## 13.

*We will run in the odor of thine ointments.* This [1858C] odor is breathed by the soul that begins to open to Christ, that she may first receive the odor of the Lord's burial, and may believe that his flesh saw no corruption, nor withered with any odor of death, but rose again, embalmed with the odor of that eternal and ever-flourishing flower. For how could he wither even in the flesh, whose name *is* *1551* *an ointment emptied out?* He emptied himself that he might breathe upon thee; this ointment always was, but it was with the Father, it was in the Father, and gave forth its scent only to angels and archangels, as within the vessel of heaven. The Father opened his mouth, saying: *Behold, I have set thee for a covenant of my race, for a light of the nations, that thou mayest be for salvation unto the utmost part of the earth [n: (Esai. XLIX, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *dedi te in lucem gentium, ut sis salus mea usque ad extremum terrae*, without the covenant clause and with "my salvation"] The Son came down, all things were filled with the new odor of the Word, the heart of the Father uttered forth a good Word, the Son was fragrant, the Holy [1858D] Spirit breathed out, and poured himself abroad through the hearts of all: *for the charity of God has been poured abroad in our hearts by the Holy Spirit [n: (Rom. V, 5)]*. The Son of God himself at first held in his own odor within the body as within a vessel, awaiting the time, as he says: *The Lord gives me the tongue of learning, that I may know when it behoves me to speak a word.* [var: Vulg. Isa. 50:4 *Dominus dedit mihi linguam eruditam, ut sciam sustentare eum qui lassus est verbo*, "to sustain him that is weary with a word"] The hour came, he opened his mouth, the ointment went forth, when virtue was going out from him. And therefore she hastens to the Word, and asks to be drawn, lest perchance she be left behind; because the Word of God runs, and is not bound. And so *he exults as a giant to run [1859A] the way [n: (Psal. XVIII, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *exultavit ut gigans ad currendam viam*, a past "he exulted"] And because *his going forth is from the highest heaven, and his meeting even unto the highest thereof [n: (Ibid., 7)]*, seeing herself unequal to so great a swiftness, she says: *Draw us.*

## 14.

Good is the soul that asks not for herself alone, but for all: *Draw,* I say, *us;* for we have the desire of following, which the grace of thine ointments breathes into us: but because we cannot match thy courses, *Draw us;* that, propped by thy help, we may be able to press upon thy footsteps. For if thou shalt draw, we too shall run, and shall catch the spiritual breezes of swiftness. For the burden is laid down for those to whom thy hand is a prop: and thine oil is poured in, by which he was healed who had been wounded by robbers.

## 15.

But lest what she says seem to thee shameless — *Draw us* — hear him who says: *Come to me, all you that [1859B] labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you [n: (Matth. XI, 28)]*. Thou seest that he draws us willingly, lest we be left behind as we follow: but let him who wishes to be drawn run that he may lay hold, and let him run forgetting the things behind and reaching after the things that are before; for thus will he be able to lay hold of Christ; whence the Apostle also says: *So run that you may lay hold.* She too wishes to come to the prize, who desires to lay hold. Prudently, therefore, she asks that she may be drawn [sic: *attrabatur*], because not all are able to follow. And so, when Peter said: *Whither goest thou?* the Word of God answered: *Thou canst not follow me now, but thou shalt follow hereafter [n: (Joan. XIII, 36; Matth. XIV, 30)]*. He had committed to him the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and yet judged him unequal to himself in following. This soul, however, he did not put off, because she was not presuming, but asking. And so, [1859C]

## 16.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] He has brought me into his chamber.* [var: Vulg. *introduxit me rex in cellaria sua*, "into his storerooms," plural] Blessed is the soul that enters the inner chambers of the Word; for she, rising up out of the body, becomes more remote from all things, and searches and seeks after that which is above her very self, that divine thing, whether by any means she may attain it: and when she has been able to grasp it, having passed beyond the things that are intelligible, she is confirmed in it and is fed upon it. Such was Paul, who knew that he had been caught up into paradise, but did not know whether outside the body or in the body. For his soul had risen up out of the body, and had led and lifted itself away from the inward parts and the bonds of the flesh; and, made a stranger to himself, he held within himself the unspeakable words which he heard and could not publish abroad, having marked that it is not lawful for a man to utter them. The good soul, therefore, despises the visible [1859D] and the sensible, and does not settle in them, nor tarry and sit down in the beholding of them: but she ascends to those things that are eternal and invisible and full of wonders, raising herself with the pure perception of a devout mind. For indeed, being zealous for perfection, she reaches toward that good of the Godhead alone, and thinks that nothing else is to be sought, because she holds what is highest.

## 17.

*The king has brought me into his chamber.* The kiss is simple, but the secret of the chamber is full of business; for in this place she testifies that she wishes to know the mysteries more fully, that she may enter the inner shrines of the heavenly mysteries, [1860A] and that the treasures of wisdom and knowledge hidden in Christ may be opened to her: and that she may be able to do this, perhaps she says that which was said above: *Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth.* This signifies the grace of the Holy Spirit coming upon her, as the Angel said to Mary: *The Holy Spirit shall come upon thee, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow thee [n: (Luc. I, 35)]*. But when the king brought her into his chamber, the time of the passion declares it, the piercing of the side, the shedding of blood, the ointment of the burial, the mystery *1552* of the resurrection; so that she received the kiss as a betrothed, but into the chamber of Christ the Church was brought in, no longer as merely betrothed, but also as wedded: nor did she only enter the bridal chamber, but also obtained the keys of the lawful union. And therefore, set as it were in the bridal chamber, she says: *A binding of the drop is my brother to me; between [1860B] the midst of my breasts he rests [n: (Vers. 12)]*. [var: Vulg. *fasciculus murrae dilectus meus mihi, inter ubera mea commorabitur*, "a bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me… he shall abide"]

## 18.

But if we seek the chamber, he himself teaches us who says: but thou, when thou shalt pray, *enter into thy chamber, and, the door being shut, pray to thy Father in secret [n: (Matth. VI, 6)]*. The chamber of the Church is the body of Christ. The king brought her into all the inward mysteries, gave her the keys that she might open for herself the treasures of knowledge, might spread open the doors of the sacraments that were closed before, might know the grace of him who rested, the sleep of him who died, the power of him who rose again. In that chamber the bride finds the justices of the Lord Jesus. What are those justices? Surely the sacraments of baptism, as we read: for when John said to him as he came to baptism, *I ought to be baptized by thee, and comest thou to me?* Jesus answered: *Suffer it now, for so it becomes us to fulfil all [1860C] justice [n: (Matth. III, 15)]*. In that chamber she learns the justifications of the Lord, she has come to know the counsel of God, than which nothing can be more sublime; for this is the divine thing, in which remission of sins is made.



## 19.

She seeks to learn from the Lord those things which men could not teach unless God had taught them first: and after the apostles had learned them from Christ, the Church says: *The king has brought me into his chamber,* that is, into that secret place in which are all the treasures of his knowledge and understanding. And therefore it is said to thee: *When thou prayest, enter into thy chamber [n: (Matth. VI, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *cum orabis*, a future "when thou shalt pray"] which signifies the secret place of mind and soul. Into this chamber of justices the bride asked to be led, when she had run out after the odor of the ointment, which flows always and never fails. [1860D]

## 20.

*The king has brought me into his chamber.* The Greek has "into his storehouse" and "into his cellar": where are the good libations, where are the good odors, sweet honeys, where are the divers fruits, where are the varied dishes, that thy meal may be seasoned with very many dishes. And so, having been brought into that secret place of the divine bridegroom:

## 21.

*Let us exult,* she says, *and be glad in thee; let us love thy breasts above wine;* [var: Vulg. *exultabimus et laetabimur in te, memores uberum tuorum super vinum*, a future "we will exult" and "mindful of thy breasts"] for she exults not in riches and in treasures of gold and silver, not in the fruits of possessions, not in powers, not in banquets, but in God alone, saying to him: *Let us love [1861A] thy breasts above wine.* Yet she so drank that she was not swallowed up by the wine, but drew the gladness of the heart from his grace, and did not stagger with the drunkenness of the body. And so, drinking this wine, she opened her eyes and saw the straight way, and left the crooked ways, saying: *Equity has loved thee,* [var: Vulg. *recti diligunt te*, "the upright love thee"] that is: winding journeys do not follow thee, but the path of justice alone can come to thee; for he who loves justice does not turn himself away from Christ. For how should an innocent conscience fear the judge of justice and the rewarder of merits? Yet the same soul, knowing herself darkened by the fellowship of the body, says to other souls, or to other heavenly powers set beside the sacred mystery: *Do not look upon me, since I have been darkened, because the sun has not looked upon me.*

## 22.

*[n: (Vers. 4.)] I am dusky and comely, O daughters of Jerusalem.* [1861B] [var: Vulg. *nigra sum sed formosa*, "I am black but beautiful," and *decoloravit me sol*, "the sun has discolored me," where the plate prints the negative "the sun has not looked upon me"] Set before thee the Lord Jesus reclining at the banquet, and John leaning himself upon his breast, and the others marvelling that a servant should lean upon his Lord, that that sinful flesh should recline upon the temple of the Word, that that soul, bound in by the chains of the flesh, should search out the court of the divine fulness. To these others, therefore, as they marvelled, the soul of John answered: *I am dusky and comely, O daughters of Jerusalem;* dusky through fault, comely through grace. The flesh says it too, *I am dusky and comely;* dusky with the dust of the world, which she gathered in her striving, comely with the spiritual eye, wherewith she wiped away the dust and squalor of this world: dusky through vice, but comely now through the washing, which cleanses away every offense: I am dusky because I have sinned, comely because Christ now loves me; for her whom he had banished in Eve, he takes back in [1861C] the virgin, he receives from Mary.

## 23.

The Synagogue says it too, whose mysteries very many think to be expressed in this place; who, when she saw herself cast off on account of the impiety of the whole people, yet consoled herself, *1553* because she saw Christ himself out of her own people, and not him only, but Peter, John, and James cleaving to Christ, and because she had the oracles entrusted to her. And therefore she said: *I am dusky and comely, O daughters of Jerusalem:* dusky through unbelief, comely through the law: dusky through the fall, comely because the sun has loved me, and I was made the first congregation of God. *Do not flee from me because I am dusky;* I am dusky for this reason, that the sun of justice has forsaken me, who before was wont to give me light. I have lost the color of my countenance, the keenness of my eyes has been dulled, wherewith before I looked upon the sun: [1861D] I walk in darkness, I who know not the day of Christ. Yet do not despise me, since he who forsook me can look upon me again. He is wont to have mercy again, to gather the scattered, to seek out the forsaken, to collect the destitute. All ye tribes of Israel, come together, look upon me as I was comely before my offenses; *do not look upon me, since I have been darkened;* that is, do not look at this alone, that I have been darkened: the sun has not looked upon me, and therefore I have been darkened. But the sun shines upon the just and the unjust, upon the just for grace, upon the unjust for [1862A] mercy; giving to the one the reward of their merits, forgiving the others their sins. And upon the nations he did not shine before; now he shines, now he rises upon them who once rose upon me; he who has forgiven them will forgive me also. Do not judge, because I have been darkened, that the sun has utterly forsaken me and no longer looks upon me, nor seeks me out in my sickness. He has hidden himself from me because I have not kept his commandments: he will be reconciled when he shall have seen the repentance of my offenses. The sun has not seen me, because I did not receive him when he came, I did not open the windows that the light of life might come in: when I shall have opened them, he will enlighten my eyes, who came that he might enlighten the whole world, and that even those who do not see may see.

## 24.

Or the Church of the nations says: *Do not look upon me, since I am dusky, because the sun has not regarded me;* in that the sun of justice long judged the congregation of the nations, bound as it were with the winter's frosts and the ice [1862B] of gentile error, unworthy to be surveyed by the serene light of his countenance. Did not the harshness of a wintry season seem to thee to be there, when God was known in Judaea only? But now the fulness of the summer light shines out, when Christ is all things and in all. Is not *the earth the Lord's, and the fulness thereof [n: (Psalm. XXIII, 1)]*? And truly the whole round world is in the Church, because *there is not Jew only or Greek, not Barbarian or Scythian, not slave or free: but we are all one in Christ [n: (Coloss. III, 11)]*. [var: Vulg. *sed omnia et in omnibus Christus*, "but Christ is all and in all"] The sun shines upon all, the day gives light to all. *I am black, and comely.* She has put "black" first, that she might increase the comeliness: she did not say, I am black comely, lest what is black should be thought comely: but she says, I am black by the former sin, but comely [1862C] by the confession of sin, and by zeal for amendment, and by love of virtue. Although therefore *And* is a syllable which, as the grammarians call it, is conjunctive, yet it has a distinguishing force, whereby confusion is distinguished and separated; as, if thou shouldst say that Ambrose Bassus is within, one man is thought of; but if thou assert that Ambrose *and* Bassus are within, two are certainly understood.

## 25.

*As the tabernacles of Cedar, as the skins of Solomon;* for tents are made from the hides of dead animals. Therefore she who said this was dead to sin and living to God: and let us too, then, die to sin, that we may live to God. Filled with the spirit, with the delight and sweetness of gladness, we shall be spiritual hides, lacking bodily infirmity, and keeping in the whole bosom of the mind the grace of the divine mysteries poured into us. [1862D] But that the tabernacles of Cedar, or the skins of Solomon, mean this body is clear from many passages: yet understand it also from this, that when Adam and Eve laid aside the image of the heavenly man which they bore before, and put on the image of the earthly man, they are said to have been clothed with tunics of skin; for the fault they had committed had made them bodily out of spiritual.

## 26.

Nor is that absurd to understand, that the bride says she has been made like skins scorched by the burnings of the sun; in that, though she was set in the harshness of tribulations, [1863A] she yet mortified her body, that it might not be able to feel that harshness: for as the skin of a tabernacle does not feel the burning of the sun, in that it is the skin of a dead animal, so she, being dead to sin, could not feel the burning of sin: either because she was not broken by necessities, or because we are a body *1554* of affliction, and ought always to carry about the death of the Lord Jesus in our body.

## 27.

*[n: (Vers. 5.)] The sons of my mother have fought against me;* that is, the passions of the body have assailed me, the enticements of the flesh have discolored me: therefore the sun of justice has not shone again upon me, because, widowed of my defense, I could not keep my devotion and my full observance; for this is what is meant by *I have not kept my own vineyard,* because I brought forth thorns and not the grape, that is, doing sins instead of fruits. And when she speaks of the Word, [1863B] the splendor of the Word shining upon her, she turns to him and says:

## 28.

*[n: (Vers. 6.)] Where dost thou feed, where dost thou abide at midday?* [var: Vulg. *ubi pascas, ubi cubes in meridie*, subjunctive and "where thou liest down"] Rightly she says, *where dost thou feed,* because the Word of God is royal: *where dost thou abide,* because he is moral: *at midday,* because he is mystical: since indeed at midday Joseph, set at the banquet with his brothers, was revealing the mysteries of the times to come. But David also says: *Reveal thy life to the Lord and hope in him, and he himself will do it, and will lead forth thy justice as the light, and thy judgment as the noonday [n: (Psalm. XXXVI, 5)]*. [var: Vulg. *revela Domino viam tuam*, "reveal thy way to the Lord"] And Paul himself asserted that a light shone about him at midday, when he was turned from persecution to grace.



## 29.

She complains, therefore, that she has been forsaken, that she has been left destitute, poor out of rich; for she abounded in the gift [1863C] of graces, but she began to be in want when the abundance of the divine presence was denied her. And therefore she asks even to be held as a hireling, she who before claimed for herself the grace of a more precious union.

## 30.

*They have set me as a keeper in the vineyards; my own vineyard I have not kept [n: (Vers. 5)]*. Some think that this is said of those who bind the Synagogue to the precepts of the law, that she may keep the law and guard her own vineyard, which she could not keep. And so she was keeping it that she might make grapes, but she made thorns. What, then, the vineyard is, David comes to testify to us, saying: *Thou hast brought a vineyard out of Egypt, and hast cast out the nations, and hast planted it [n: (Psalm. LXXIX, 9)]*. The apostles too may be the sons of her mother, who truly assailed the Synagogue, saying: *Behold, we turn to the nations:* and they began to sow the word [1863D] of God among the peoples. The prophets too may be understood, who, by warning and denouncing that the Synagogue should keep her own vineyard, accomplished nothing; for she made the thorns of wickedness, who ought to have brought forth the rich fruits of virtue: and therefore she confesses that she could not keep her own people, and deservedly seeks too late to hold him whom, while she held him, she lost: far unlike her who says: *I have found him whom my soul loves; I held him, and I will not let him go [n: (Cant. III, 4)]*. [var: Vulg. *tenui eum, nec dimittam*, "I held him, nor will I let him go"] And so that one invites the bridegroom into her garden; this one, asking where he feeds, says to him: [1864A]

## 31.

*Tell me, whom my soul has loved.* [var: Vulg. *indica mihi quem diligit anima mea*, "show me, whom my soul loves," in the present; the gloss that follows turns on the past tense] Why dost thou say *whom my soul has loved,* and not "whom she loves"? Why hast thou let go him whom thou didst hold? Thou didst love him by the faith of the fathers, thou hast lost him by unbelief: thou didst hold him by the bonds of charity, thou hast lost him by the long rope of faithlessness. Therefore thou knowest not where he feeds, where he abides; for if thou didst know, thou wouldst not seek. For thou sayest: *Where dost thou feed, where dost thou abide at midday?* Thou knowest that the midday is the Church which holds Christ, and thou seekest him in the nights. Say to me, she says, O Christ, answer me at least for this cause, that thou wast dear to me before and beloved; therefore, though I have lost the privilege of so great a charity, yet, as one who art merciful, answer me, *Where dost thou feed, where dost thou abide?* Thou hast forsaken me, thou hast gone away to the nations, thou hast withdrawn far from me, and hast been made nearer to those from whom thou wast far. But thou hast been made near, because they have believed in thy blood. Thou hast withdrawn from me, because I did not receive the cross [1864B] as the redemption of the world, but as the condemnation [sic: *damnanationem*] of a guilty man. But they who received it as the author of salvation are at midday; for them thou shinest, for them thou blazest out, for them thy grace is warm as the noonday. To me thou wast the morning, when as yet I believed, but I did not believe fully, because I have not been found at midday, like Joseph with his brothers, who held the midday: for them thou hast been made noonday, who are fed in thy riches and hope in thee. Therefore, as David said: *Thou shalt lead forth their justice as the light, and their judgment as the noonday [n: (Psalm. XXXVI, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *educet quasi lumen iustitiam tuam, et iudicium tuum tamquam meridiem*, "he will lead forth thy justice… and thy judgment"]

## 32.

Thou seekest, then, as a stranger, thou who wast a neighbor; as a poor woman, thou who wast rich: thou wishest to follow, thou who didst go before; and would that thou mightest at least follow those whom thou oughtest to have gone before! Thou desirest to be a hireling, thou who [1864C] before didst gather hirelings; and so this is the voice of a hireling, saying: *Lest* *1555* *perchance I become one wrapped about, beside the flocks of thy companions.* [var: Vulg. *ne vagari incipiam post greges sodalium tuorum*, "lest I begin to wander after the flocks"] Thou who before didst receive proselytes out of the nations, now thou thyself among the peoples wishest to be received in the place of proselytes, and to be gathered as a stranger. The Word of God answered the longing soul:

## 33.

*[n: (Vers. 7.)] Unless thou know thyself, O comely one among women.* [var: Vulg. *si ignoras te, o pulcherrima inter mulieres, egredere*, "if thou know not thyself, O fairest among women, go forth"] Know thyself, O man: it is said to thy soul, unless thou hast known thyself comely among women. Know thyself, O soul, that thou art not of earth, not of clay, because God breathed into thee and made thee into a living soul. Thou art a magnificent work, formed by the inbreathing of God: *Attend to thyself,* as the Law says, that is, to thy soul: let not the things of the world and of the age hold thee, let not earthly things [1864D] delay thee. Hasten with thy whole intention to him by whose inbreathing thou standest: *A great thing,* he says, *is man, and precious is a merciful man: but to find a faithful man is a labor [n: (Prov. XX, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *multi homines misericordes vocantur; virum autem fidelem quis inveniet?*, "many men are called merciful; but who shall find a faithful man?"] Learn, O man, wherein thou art great and precious. The earth shows thee to be vile, but virtue makes thee glorious, faith makes thee rare, the image makes thee precious. For where is anything so precious as the image of God? which ought first to pour faith into thee, so that some likeness of its author may shine again in thy heart; lest he who questions thy mind should not recognize the author. Or is anything so precious as humility; [1865A] that, looking upon the nature of body and of soul, by the one thou shouldst subject thyself, by the other recognize thyself. The enticement of the flesh, prone to vice, urges mercy; because what thou hast bestowed upon another, thou payest to thyself: whatever proceeds from thee runs back into thee, and whatever thou shalt do, thou payest to thyself: and whatever profit thou shalt bring, it profits thee. Let the vigor of the living soul, the perception of reason and the mind capable of understanding and of judgment, so that it may be seen a worthy house for so great a dweller, not lose the privilege of its own nature. *Unless thou know thyself, O comely one among women.* Thou who complainest that thou hast been forsaken: unless thou know thyself, unless thou repent of thy fall, unless thou approve the intention of thy devotion, unless thy faith and sincerity be increased, thy complaint will profit nothing. Or thus: unless thou know thyself, that thou art comely; unless thou keep the beauty of thy nature, and the enticements of the body do not sink thee down, [1865B] nor hindrances hold thee back, the nobility of a better creature will avail thee nothing.

## 34.

Know thyself, therefore, and the comeliness of thy nature; and go forth as one stripped of the bonds of the feet, and thrust out with bare footprint, that thou mayest not feel the coverings of the flesh: let not bodily chains entangle the footstep of thy mind, that thy foot may appear shapely. For such are they who are chosen by the Lord to announce the kingdom of heaven, of whom it is said: *How shapely are the feet of them that preach the gospel of peace [n: (Esai. LII, 7)]*. Such was Moses, to whom it is said: *Loose the shoe of thy feet [n: (Exod. III, 5)]*; [var: Vulg. Exod. 3:5 *solve calciamentum de pedibus tuis*, "from thy feet"] so that he who was to go forth to call the people to the kingdom of God should first cast off the hides of the flesh and walk with bare spirit and with the footstep of the mind; this, then, is what he says: *Go thou forth in the sandals of the flocks, and feed [1865C] thy kids in the tabernacles of the shepherds.* [var: Vulg. *egredere et abi post vestigia gregum, et pasce hedos tuos iuxta tabernacula pastorum*, "go after the footsteps of the flocks… beside the tabernacles"]

## 35.

By the flocks we understand a kingdom, in that it belongs to power to preside over flocks. And every man presides over himself by a certain royal power, if he restrains in himself the excesses of the body and reduces his flesh to servitude; and therefore it is said: *The kingdom of God is within you.* Whence beautifully he says to the soul: *Go forth,* that is, go forth from servitude, go forth from the command and dominion of the flesh, and go forth not in the flesh but in the spirit, go forth to the governance of power. And therefore he added: *Feed thy kids,* that is, rule those things that are on thy left hand; for if they are not ruled, they easily slip away. Restrain the wantonness, the playfulness of thy body and its unreasoning excess, tame its light motions: feed them not in bodily tabernacles, but in the tabernacles of the shepherds who know how to rule a flock; for the tabernacles of Israel are lovely, like groves overshadowing a river, in which the soul, set as it were [1865D] in battle array, exercises a good soldiery and searches out the assaults of the enemy.

## 36.

*Unless thou know thyself, O comely one among women.* What is it to know oneself, but that each man should know that he was made to the image and likeness of God, capable of reason, who ought to till his own earth like a good husbandman with a certain plough and sickle of wisdom, so that either hard things may be split or luxuriant things cut back, and who ought to govern the inward portion of himself by the command of the mind? [1866A] Whence also it is written in the Law: *Attend to thyself, lest a hidden word be made in thy heart [n: (Deut. XV, 9)]*. [var: Vulg. Deut. 15:9 *cave ne forte subrepat tibi impia cogitatio*, "beware lest perchance an impious thought creep upon thee"] Attend to thyself, he says: he certainly does not say *1556* to thy money, not to thy possessions, not to the strength of the body, but to thy spirit and to thy mind, whence all counsels, deeds, and thoughts flow. Attend to thyself, therefore, there, where thou knowest thyself to be the stronger. Know thyself: which the men of the nations assign to Pythian Apollo, as though he were the author of this maxim; whereas they carry over to their own what has been usurped from ours, and Moses, who wrote the book of Deuteronomy, was far earlier than the philosophers who invented these things.



## 37.

Whence Solomon also, following the divine oracle, wrote in the Song of Songs: *Unless thou know thyself, thou comely one among women* [var: Vulg. *Si ignoras te, o pulcherrima inter mulieres*, "If thou know not thyself, O fairest among women"]; that is, unless thou know thyself to be mortal [1866B] and rational, and quickly confess thy sins, quickly tell thy iniquities that thou mayest be justified — unless thou be converted, and be the first to accuse thy own faults, the day of death comes and there is now no remedy of conversion; thou art forestalled while thou art thinking of it. Kindle thy torch before the bridegroom's door be shut against thee, for he is not wont to wait long for the negligent. *Unless thou know thyself,* he says, *thou comely one among women;* and say thou: *I am swarthy and comely* [var: Vulg. Cant. 1:4 *Nigra sum, sed formosa*, "I am black, but beautiful"]*: I am swarthy,* because I have sinned, *comely,* because I am loved, because I am of the stock of Abraham, a chosen stock, beloved of God. The grace of the fathers will profit thee nothing: *For God is able of these stones to raise up sons to Abraham [n: (Matth. III, 9)]*. And elsewhere in the Gospel thou hast read that the devil bound a daughter of Abraham with the chains of his own wickedness, whom the Lord loosed on the sabbath day. Unless therefore thou know thyself, it will profit thee nothing [1866C]. Even if thou say, I am a daughter of Abraham — thou who believest not, who correctest not thine error: Abraham indeed is saved, but nobility of stock will not help thee, unless faith have saved thee. Let not the promise given to the fathers deceive thee; I accept not the person of a man, I accept not the prerogative of stock, unless I have seen a nobility of manners answering to the stock, so that there be an election of a just stock.

## 38.

But if thou know thyself, and know that thou art liable to sin, thou must go out among the heels of the flocks. Go out therefore with bare foot, and feed thy kids in the tents of the shepherds. He who is without sin feeds lambs, feeds sheep; but she who is liable to sin, let her feed the kids which are on the left; for she cannot be at the right hand of the good shepherd. Let her rather follow him who says to Peter: *Come behind me, Satan [n: (Marc. VIII, 33)]* [var: Vulg. *Vade retro me, Satana*, "Go behind me"]. By following, Peter deserved to be set at the right hand; and therefore [1866D] it is said to him: *Feed my little lambs [n: (Joan. XXI, 16)]* [var: Vulg. *agnos*, "lambs" — without the diminutive]. But hear whither she is to go out: *To the tents,* he says, *of the shepherds,* that is, to the nations, to the dispersion. And therefore he said: *I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock shall be scattered [n: (Zach. XIII, 7)]* [var: Vulg. Zach. *Percute pastorem, et dispergentur oves*, "Strike thou the shepherd, and the sheep shall be scattered"]; that the whole round of the earth might be filled with the flock of Christ — signifying that the Synagogue would then be safe, when she too shall have joined herself to this dispersion which has filled this world; for the tents of the shepherds are the kingdoms of the earth. [1867A]

## 39.

*[n: (Vers. 8.)] To my mare among the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee, my neighbor* [var: Vulg. *Equitatui meo in curribus Pharaonis assimilavi te, amica mea*, "To my cavalry… my friend"]. In that the likeness of the Church seems here to be compared to the running of swift horses, the richness of her grace is reckoned. Do not therefore understand one mare of the rich king, but a word put absolutely for the cavalry, as we have often said — of what sort that sheep of his is, put for the flock of sheep. Pharaoh therefore, as a most powerful and most wealthy king, had powerful horses; whence Scripture also says: *The chariots of Pharaoh [n: (Exod. XIV, 9)]*, as being a most powerful kind for the use of wars and a support in fighting: and indeed with these chariots Pharaoh easily overtook the fleeing Hebrews. As therefore horses yoked together easily draw the chariot, and patiently take up the yoke, and bear it with grace, and by the taking up of that yoke grow gentle: so also the congregation of the nations, [1867B] untamed in its heathen manners, once vaunted itself. But when it took up the yoke of him who says: *Take my yoke upon you, for it is light; and my burden, for it is sweet [n: (Matth. XI, 29 et 30)]* [var: Vulg. *jugum enim meum suave est, et onus meum leve*, "my yoke is sweet and my burden light" — the two epithets exchanged]; and the bride of Christ began to be exalted by the concord and gentleness of the peoples, and to be carried about the whole world, then, like a chariot caught up above the world by swift horses, she went up to the bridegroom.

## 40.

For Christ has his own horses, of whom the prophet says: *Thou hast sent thy horses into the sea, troubling many waters [n: (Habac. III)]* [var: Vulg. Hab. 3:15 *Viam fecisti in mari equis tuis, in luto aquarum multarum*, "Thou madest a way in the sea for thy horses"]; in that the apostles, by preaching the Gospel, stirred up the peoples of the nations, who are moved like many waters and are roused into billows of waters, so that, rising up from the earthly ceremonies of idols, they might believe in Christ. And above he says: *Thou hast mounted [1867C] upon thy horses, thy cavalry is health* [var: Vulg. Hab. 3:8 *quadrigae tuae salvatio*, "thy chariots are salvation"]. O marvellous [sic: *duo decajugum*] of good horses, for whom the bits are of peace, *1557* the reins are of charity: bound fast among themselves by the bonds of concord, and subjected to the yoke of faith, carrying in upon four wheels the mystery of the Gospel to the bounds of the whole world, bearing the good charioteer, the Word of God: by whose whip the enticements of the world were put to flight, the prince of this world driven out, the course of the just fulfilled. O great contest of rational horses! O mystery to be wondered at! A wheel ran within a wheel, and was not hindered: the New Testament within the Old Testament, it ran within that through which it was announced. Into four parts the wheels went, and they turned not backward; because the spirit of life was in them, which ran into the four parts [1867D] of the whole world: and it ran without stumbling, because the good life of the horses squared with it. The horses therefore ran, because he who had mounted the horses was not sleeping.

## 41.

The charioteer of our souls, therefore, is Jesus; who wills that we too should mount our own horses, that is, our bodies, and always be watchful, lest it be said of us: *They have slumbered who mounted horses [n: (Psalm. LXXV, 7)]*. This sea must be crossed briskly; it is scarcely crossed even by the watchful. But if any man shall have slept, he will not be able to cross, but is drowned, like the Egyptian, whose soul and body alike perished. For *the horse* and *the rider he cast [1868A] into the sea [n: (Exod. XV, 1)]* [var: Vulg. *dejecit*, "cast down"], who did not follow the law, but [sic: *per sequebantur*].

## 42.

*To my mare among the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee.* The labour of virtue seeks victory, so that she cannot be compared to that mare which is Solomon's, swift for running, apt for bearing young; since the fruitfulness of the soul is desired and sought. This mare therefore is precious, and the chariots of Pharaoh are swift. But in this place we have undertaken to speak of the soul, of this mare who is reckoned like to this soul, that is, of prophetic or apostolic virtue: that she be numbered in the flock of those who by the fruitfulness of their preaching have filled the spaces of the whole round of the earth. And although set in the body, yet they felt no losses of their spiritual course. And therefore she is praised, that, the heavenly precept enlightening her, [1868B] she is now shapely, now beautiful, who bears in her countenance the grace of chastity, and lifts up the necklaces of her neck, in which are the marks of patience and humility. And on this account, praising his bride, he said:

## 43.

*[n: (Vers. 9.)] How shapely are thy cheeks become, as a turtle-dove's, thy neck as necklaces* [var: Vulg. *Pulchrae sunt genae tuae sicut turturis; collum tuum sicut monilia*, "Beautiful are thy cheeks… thy neck as jewels"]. The countenance is freer where there is the consciousness of charity; and to bear the yoke of Christ is sweet, if thou reckon them to be the ornaments of thy neck, not its burdens. Lift up therefore thine eyes always to thy Lord, and seek God, that thou mayest find him. Raise up thy neck: thou wearest necklaces, not chains: even dumb beasts rejoice in trappings, and seem to themselves rather to be adorned than to be tied. *Cheeks as a turtle-dove's:* let them bear the marks of modesty; *the necklaces of the neck:* let them lift up the confidence of freedom; for the yoke of Christ is light, [1868C] and therefore the neck is not pressed down by it, but lifted up.

## 44.

*[n: (Vers. 10, 11.)] Likenesses,* he says, *of gold will we make for thee, out of distinctions of silver, until the king shall be in his reclining* [var: Vulg. *Murenulas aureas faciemus tibi, vermiculatas argento. Dum esset rex in accubitu suo*, "Golden chains will we make thee, inlaid with silver. While the king was at his couch"]. For out of those who are of the Law and of the prophets, they had before believed but moderately in the glory of the Lord Jesus; and his inheritance, spread abroad through the peoples, the more often it was tried, so much the more was it approved. For the frequent persecutions of the Church have brought forth for us the titles of the just, the victories of martyrdom. And so, like good gold: when the Church makes use of wrath, it feels no losses, but rather its brightness is increased, until Christ come into his kingdom, and lay back his head upon the faith of the Church. He, when he had come to the lost sheep of the house of Israel, had not where to lay back his head: but now faith is already fragrant, and therefore [1868D] the Church says: *My spikenard has given its odour;* and she says it, awaiting her recompense with confidence. The ointment of grace breathes forth, in which the Virgin brought forth, and the Lord Jesus took up the sacrament of the incarnation.



## 45.

*[n: (Vers. 12, 13.)] A binding-together of the drop is my brother to me; in the midst of my breasts he shall remain,* or *shall dwell* [var: Vulg. *Fasciculus myrrhae dilectus meus mihi, inter ubera mea commorabitur*, "A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me; he shall abide between my breasts"]. She had indeed said before that her spikenard had given her the odour of the bridegroom, and that through the ointment with which she had anointed him she had received the fragrance of his odour: but now she says: My brother smells to me of the drop, and this not poured abroad nor scattered as it may chance, but [1869A] bound together and drawn tight, so namely that the odour of its own sweetness may be rendered denser and stronger. And he, she says, *1558* being such as he is, dwells and abides in the midst of my breasts, and makes his rest and his mansion in the place of my bosom. *A binding-together of the drop is my cousin, a cluster of cypress is my brother to me; between the midst of my breasts he shall rest.* For the Lord Jesus, taking a body, bound himself with the chains of charity, and joined himself not only to our members and to natural sufferings, but even to the cross; and therefore, like a cluster, he rests in the faith of the Church and in her moral grace.

## 46.

*Spikenard of cypress is my cousin in the vineyards of Engaddi* [var: Vulg. *Botrus cypri dilectus meus mihi, in vineis Engaddi*, "A cluster of cypress is my beloved to me"]. If we seek the place of a certain region which is in Judaea, that place is so called in which the balsam-sap is produced: if we seek the interpretation, in Latin it signifies temptation. [1869B] But in those vineyards there is a wood which, if one prick it, sends forth ointment; this is the fruit of the wood. If the wood be not cut, it does not so give fragrance and odour; but when it has been pricked by the craftsman's hand, then it distils a tear. So also Christ, crucified upon that wood of temptation, wept over the people, that he might wash away our sins, and out of the bowels of his mercy poured forth ointment, saying: *Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do [n: (Luc. XXIII, 34)]* [var: Vulg. *non enim sciunt quid faciunt*, "for they know not"]. Then therefore, pricked upon the wood with the lance, there went out from him blood and water, sweeter than any ointment, a victim acceptable to God, pouring out through the whole world the odour of sanctification. And as balsam out of the tree, so virtue went forth out of his body, whence he says: *I perceive that virtue has gone out from me* [1869C] [n: (Luc. VIII, 46)] [var: Vulg. *nam ego novi virtutem de me exiisse*, "for I know that virtue has gone out from me"]. Thence it is called more expressly *opobalsamum*, in that by the pricking of the wood the balsam bursts out through the hollow of the puncture.

## 47.

Jesus therefore, being pricked, poured forth the odour of the remission of sins and of redemption. For he was also drawn tight, when the Word was made man: and he was made poor, when he was rich, that we might be enriched by his want: he was powerful, and offered himself to be despised, so that Herod scorned him and mocked him: he moved the earth, and clung to the wood: he covered heaven with darkness, and crucified the world, and was crucified: he bowed his head, and the Word went forth: he was emptied out, and filled all things: God descended, man ascended: the Word was made flesh, that the flesh might claim for itself the throne of the Word at the right hand of God: he was a wound, and [1869D] ointment flowed: he was heard as a beetle, and was acknowledged as God. And Christ answered.

## 48.

*[n: (Vers. 14.)] Behold thou art good, my neighbor, behold thou art good: thine eyes are a dove's* [var: Vulg. *Ecce tu pulchra es, amica mea, ecce tu pulchra es; oculi tui columbarum*, "Behold thou art beautiful, my friend… thine eyes are doves' eyes"]. Because the Church had known the mystery, and was preaching the Lord Jesus crucified for the redemption of the whole world, she deserves to hear: *Behold thou art good,* thou who callest me good; *Behold thou art good,* thou who hast seen the comeliness of my glory, and art thyself shapely and comely. But what is it for Christ to say: *Thou art good,* or *Thou art comely,* if not that word of the Gospel: *Be steadfast, daughter, thy sins are forgiven thee [n: (Matth. IX, 22)]* [var: Vulg. Matt. 9:22 *Confide, filia, fides tua te salvam fecit*, "Have confidence, daughter, thy faith hath made thee whole"; the clause on forgiveness stands at Matt. 9:2]? Therefore he to whom Christ has forgiven sins [1870A] rightly says: *Reward thy servant, that I may live and keep thy commandments [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 146)]* [var: Vulg. Ps. 118:17 *Retribue servo tuo, vivifica me, et custodiam sermones tuos*, "quicken me, and I will keep thy words"]. He has nothing in the recompense whereof to despair; because the Lord Jesus came to save the world, not to destroy it. Therefore he is unmindful of injury, mindful of grace, as he himself bears witness in the prophetic book, saying: *I am, I am he who blot out thine iniquities, and I will not be mindful of them: but be thou mindful, and let us be judged; tell thou first thine iniquities, that thou mayest be justified [n: (Esai. XLIII, 25 et 26)]* [var: Vulg. *Ego sum, ego sum ipse qui deleo iniquitates tuas propter me, et peccatorum tuorum non recordabor. Reduc me in memoriam, et judicemur simul: narra si quid habes ut justificeris*]. Whosoever therefore tells his iniquities to God is justified: and whosoever is justified fears not the recompense, but demands it: he who fears not the recompense, lives.

## 49.

*[n: (Vers. 15, 16.)] Behold,* she says, *my cousin is well-formed, and indeed beautiful. Our reclining-place is shady, the beams of our houses are cedars, our panelled ceilings [1870B] cypresses* [var: Vulg. *Ecce tu pulcher es, dilecte mi, et decorus. Lectulus noster floridus; tigna domorum nostrarum cedrina, laquearia nostra cypressina*, "Behold thou art beautiful, my beloved, and comely. Our little bed is flowery"]. The Church praises the comeliness of the bridegroom, whom each one praises the more with silent affection; and the faithful interpreter of the mysteries preaches the more by keeping silence. For she who divulges secret things diminishes the comeliness of Christ; therefore let no one cast his pearls before swine, lest he cause precious ornaments to be trodden down. Not then in the tent of the talkative and the chattering — because by much speaking sin is admitted — but in that of a serious man, who is sparing of address, and not intemperate of speech, and by the sobriety of his words avoids the drunkenness of talkativeness, does Christ lay back his head.

## 50.

And with reason does she signify a shady reclining-place; because the virtue of the Most High overshadows those who are set in the Church. In this shadow David asked to be protected, lest the sun burn him by day, or the moon by night *(Psalm. [1870C] CXX, 6)*. This shadow spiritual grace *1559* ministers to those who flee the parched and summer heats of this age and of the world. Shady therefore is the reclining-place of Christ and of the Church, upon whom that eternal rest of God the Father breathes. In this shadow therefore let us rest, wearied by the heats of our sins: whomsoever lust has scorched, let the cross of the Lord refresh them, upon which he laid himself back, that he might take up our debts. Whomsoever guilt has wearied, let Jesus receive them in his bosom, and cherish them with a soft embrace; whence I dare to say that the flesh of Christ is the reclining-place of the Church.

## 51.

*The beams of our houses are cedars.* Under the figure of the cedar the glory of those elders who were just is designated. *For the just shall flourish like the palm tree, and like the cedar which is in Lebanon he shall be multiplied [n: (Psalm. XCI, 13)]* [var: Vulg. *sicut cedrus Libani multiplicabitur*, "like the cedar of Lebanon"]; for as the cedar [1870D] does not rot, so neither is the glory of the elders corrupted by any old age.

## 52.

*Our panelled ceilings cypresses.* This kind of tree never loses its greenness; in winter as in summer it feeds its foliage, nor is it changed with a different colour. This tree alone the wind never despoils of its honour; it alone is never stripped of its old cloak, nor clothed with a new blossom. So too apostolic grace knows no failing, but flourishes by its own age. The soul therefore knows not how to be corrupted, which is vigorous with flourishing members, and always upholds with patient greatness of mind the summits of justice and of the other virtues; [1871A] and therefore it neither flows away nor fails, because there is nothing in it chinked or slack, nothing shifting, nothing slippery, nothing which could be poured out of it by a fault of speech. There follows the Church, or the faithful soul — the soul, I say, because man is nothing so much as soul.

## CHAPTER THE SECOND.



## 1.

*[n: (Vers. 1, 2.)] I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys, as a lily in the midst of thorns* [var: Vulg. *Sicut lilium inter spinas*, "As a lily among thorns"]; for into the whole earth the faith of the believing people has gone out, and Christ has set his feet in a spacious place; and therefore he beautifully says that he is the flower of the field. A flower too was Paul, who said: *We are the good odour of Christ unto God [n: (II Cor. II, 15)]*. And truly a flower, who could bring forth new things and old out of the treasure of his own heart. *I am the flower of the field.* And well is [1871B] the Church a flower, who announces the fruit, that is, the Lord Jesus Christ, of whom it was said to Mary: *Blessed art thou among women, and blessed is the fruit of thy womb [n: (Luc. I, 42)]*.

## 2.

In another sense also I show thee a flower to be plucked, that one surely who said: *I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys.* Very many think this comparison more apt according to the nature of the flower and the use of the shoot set before us; in that lilies require no tending of any yearly use, nor, as in the case of the yield of the other fruits, does the labour of the year come round again in its circuit for anxious husbandmen with regard to the generation of this flower; for whatever the droughts of the field, all that which is nourished into flower is quickened out of itself by a certain generative power, and in itself by a sap always green; so that when [1871C] thou seest the stalk of its grown leaves to have withered, yet the nature of the flower is green; for the greenness is hidden, not lost. But when it has been called forth by the caresses of spring, it takes up again the clothing of the shoot, the foliage of the flower, or the adornments of the lily.



## 3.

It delights one also to notice this, that lilies are not begotten in the roughnesses of mountains and the uncultivated places of woods, but in the pleasantness of gardens. For there are certain gardens fruitful with divers virtues, according to that which is written: *A garden enclosed,* etc. [n: (Cant. IV, 12)], in that where there is integrity, where chastity, where religion, where the faithful silences of secret things, there is the brightness of angels, there are the violets of confessors, the lilies of virgins, the roses of martyrs. Well is Christ a lily, because where the blood of the martyrs is, there is Christ, who is the flower on high, [1871D] unspotted, harmless, in whom the roughness of thorns gives no offence, but the grace poured about him was shining. For the thorns of the roses are the torments of the martyrs; the unoffended Godhead, which felt no torments, has no thorns.

## 4.

*As a lily in the midst of thorns.* Which is of plain evidence, that virtue is beset by the brambles of spiritual wickednesses, whence no man may carry off fruit save he who comes warily. Take wings therefore, O virgin, but wings of the spirit, that thou mayest fly above the vices, if thou desirest to touch Christ, [1872A] *Who dwelleth on high, and beholdeth lowly things [n: (Psalm. CXII, 5)]*. And his form is like the cedar of Lebanon, which sets its foliage in the clouds, its root in the earth; for his beginning is from heaven, his latter parts on earth, *1560* and they have brought forth fruits nearest to heaven. Search out more diligently so good a flower, to see whether thou mayest find him anywhere in the valley of thy breast; for he is often breathed upon lowly things. He loves to be begotten in gardens, in which Susanna, while she walked, found him: ready rather to die than to be violated.

## 5.

Otherwise: *I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys, as a lily in the midst of thorns.* Behold another place in which the Lord is wont to abide — nay, not in one, but in many: *I,* he says, *am the flower of the field;* because he frequents the open simplicity of a pure mind. [1872B] *And the lily of the valleys;* for Christ is the flower of humility, not of luxury, not of pleasures and wantonness, but the flower of simplicity, the flower of humility. A flower keeps its odour even when cut down, and heaps it up when bruised, nor does it lose it when plucked away; so also the Lord Jesus, upon that gibbet of the cross, neither withered when bruised, nor vanished when plucked away, and, cut by that pricking of the lance, he blossomed the more shapely with the sacred colour of the blood poured out, himself not knowing how to die, and breathing out upon the dead the gift of eternal life.

## 6.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] As a lily in the midst of thorns* — does not the flower of good odour rise up amid the roughnesses of labours and the contritions of minds? For by a contrite heart God is appeased. This is the desert, O daughters, which leads to the kingdom; this is also the desert which [1872C] flowers as a lily, according to that which is written: *Rejoice, thou barren one, and exult, thou desert, and let it flower as a lily [n: (Esai. XXXV, 1)]* [var: Vulg. *Laetabitur deserta et invia, et exsultabit solitudo, et florebit quasi lilium*, "The desert and the pathless land shall be glad… and shall flower like a lily" — indicative, not imperative]. In this desert, O daughters, that good fruitful tree, which makes good fruits, begins to spread abroad the arms of its own deeds, to lift up the crown of its divinity, beside which the trees of our wood may put forth their shoots; because, as the apple tree among the trees of the wood, so is my brother in the midst of the sons; so that the Church, seeing this, and already glad at the success of our faith, may say: *In his shadow I have desired and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws* [var: Vulg. *Sicut malus inter ligna silvarum, sic dilectus meus inter filios. Sub umbra illius quem desideraveram sedi, et fructus ejus dulcis gutturi meo*, "Under the shadow of him whom I had desired I sat, and his fruit was sweet to my throat"].

## 7.

[n: (Vers. 3, 4.)] Note that often in this book of Songs Solomon has plainly expressed that threefold wisdom, namely the moral, the natural, and the mystical, as he had said in the Proverbs; that he might write in threefold fashion [1872D] for him who should wish to hear his wisdom. He says therefore in the Songs, the bride concerning the bridegroom: *Behold thou art beautiful, my cousin, behold thou art beautiful and comely; our reclining-place is shady, the beams of our houses are cedars, our panelled ceilings cypresses [n: (Cant. I, 15 et 16)]*. We can take this of moral things; for where does Christ rest, and the Church, if not in the works of his own people? And indeed where there was unchastity, where pride, where iniquity, there the Lord Jesus says: *But the Son of man has not where to lay back his head [n: (Matth. VIII, 20)]*. But what shall we take [1873A] of natural things? *In his shadow,* she says, *I have desired and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws;* for he who steps over earthly things, and to whom worldly things die (since the world is crucified to him, and he to the world), flees and despises all things which are under the sun. Of mystical things also she says: *Bring me into the house of wine, set charity in me.* For as the vine embraces its own vineyard, so the Lord Jesus, as an eternal vine, embraces his own people with certain arms of charity [var: Vulg. Cant. 2:4 *Introduxit me in cellam vinariam; ordinavit in me caritatem*, "He brought me into the wine cellar; he set charity in order in me" — indicative, not imperative].

## 8.

Consider them one by one: in moral things the flower among thorns is the lily, as he himself says: *I am the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleys [n: (Sup., v. 1)]*. In moral things therefore he is a flower, as in natural things the sun of justice, who, rising and rising again, gives light, and setting, overshadows. Beware lest he set upon thee, for it is written: *Let not the sun go down upon [1873B] your wrath [n: (Ephes. IV, 26)]*. In mystical things he is charity, because Christ is the fullness of the law. And therefore the Church, which loves Christ, is wounded with charity.

## 9.

Beautifully also is the Church called a lily; for as the lily shines, so also do the works of the saints shine forth. Most beautifully also was it said: *The lily of the valleys;* because in lowly things grace shines out the more.
