But this lily is in the midst of thorns, that is, among the Jews and the heretics, that is, among the anxieties of this age, which prick the mind and the spirit of man. We can take it also otherwise: that as the lily stands out among thorns, so the Church of God shines above all assemblies.

## 10.

It is worth considering also that this lily [1873C] is compassed about with splendour, but that what it has within is red, and this too of good odour; in that the flesh of Christ, hedged about as it were with the moral brightness of the Godhead, had the protection of heavenly grace. And indeed in the latter parts he says: *My kinsman is white and red [n: (Cant. V, 10)]* [var: Vulg. *Dilectus meus candidus et rubicundus*, "My beloved is white and ruddy"]: white with the brightness *1561* divine, red with the appearance of human colour, which he took up in the sacrament of the incarnation. And deservedly that also which is red smells well, because the flesh of Christ is without sin — which the faithless, in handling, defiled their own hands; the saints, in venerating, were fragrant with the odour of piety.

## 11.

The Lord Jesus received this odour of his fragrant Church, and says: *Behold my neighbor is good.* And the Church says to Christ: *Behold thou art good, my kinsman, behold thou art good, as an apple among the trees of the wood.* [1873D] A most beautiful comparison! For what could be said more beautifully or more aptly than that she should compare the bridegroom to an apple tree? For a fruit of this kind has a pleasing odour, so that it surpasses the fragrance of the other fruits. And Christ, fastened to the wood, like an apple hanging on the tree, poured out the good odour of the world's redemption, which wiped away the heavy stench of sin, and poured forth the ointment of a life-giving draught.

## 12.

*As an apple,* he says, *among the trees of the wood, so is my cousin in the midst of the sons;* in that above [1874A] the prophets and apostles he soothed the inmost hearts of men with the sweetness of his words. But not only odour, but also sweet food is in the apple: therefore Christ is sweet food.

## 13.

*In his shadow I have desired, and sat down;* for she had received the law, this she followed, this way she ran: resting therefore in the law, she was resting in the shadow of Christ. Good is the shadow which defends us from the sun of iniquity! And to whom is it a doubt that the law of God is the shadow of Christ? What is the law of the feast day, of the new moon, of the sabbaths, if not a shadow of things to come? What days are they according to the law of Moses: That I may see six days, and again the seventh day, and so perpetual ages? What month is there according to the law of Moses, that, knowing the month, I may know the first new moon of the month, and the things truly holy which are to be offered on the new moons? What year is there according to the law of Moses, [1874B] consisting of those six days of which he says: *Six years shall the Hebrew serve, and in the seventh year he shall be set free [n: (Deut. XV, 12)]* [var: Vulg. *serviet tibi sex annis: in anno septimo dimittes eum liberum*, "he shall serve thee six years: in the seventh year thou shalt let him go free"]; so that there be found six years in which a man works the land, but in the seventh year he releases it to the strangers, and to the poor, and to the beasts of the earth? What is the seventh year, in which all debts are remitted to the Hebrews?



## 14.

In this shadow rested the faith of the Fathers, the holy devotion of the prophets. Say therefore, O religious congregation or holy soul: *In his shadow I have desired, and sat down.* A shadow is the jubilee year: for who sees its mystical things, so as to know face to face those things which were prescribed by the Law to be fulfilled in the five hundredth year? Shadows are the feast days according to the Law, from the first month, from the tenth day of the month unto the fourteenth, [1874C] and thence unto the twenty-first of the month. What shadow is that celebration between the first and the seventh month, at that nameless season? A shadow is the sanctified liberty on the sabbath. A shadow is the feast day of the new moon of the seventh month, and the memorial of the trumpets, the tenth day of the seventh month, the day of propitiation. Thou seest that the monad and the decad are two feast days. Likewise the fifteenth day, the day of the Tabernacles through eight days: in shadow are all these things. In shadow is the first day so called, and the eighth day so called is in shadow, which things are prescribed for the feasts of tabernacles according to the Law.

## 15.

Set before me some of those who believe out of the circumcision, learned in the Law, men of careful observance, and now enlightened by the acknowledgment of the Gospel, fattened with spiritual grace: in these the Church speaks, which sees Christ, [1874D] which receives the bridegroom, which is fed with his food: *In his shadow I have desired, and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws.* What is his sweet fruit, if not the preaching of the Lord's passion? as he himself says: *Behold the inheritance of the Lord, sons; the reward, the fruit of the womb [n: (Psal. CXXVI, 3)]*. For what fruit can be sweeter in our jaws than the remission of sins?

## 16.

*And his fruit is sweet to my [sic: *gutluri*].* There is a spiritual manna, that is, a rain of spiritual wisdom, which is poured from heaven upon the ingenious and upon those who seek, [1875A] and bedews the minds of the pious, and sweetens their jaws: he therefore who has understood the infusion of divine wisdom is delighted, and seeks no other food, nor lives on bread alone, but on every word of God. He who is more curious asks what that is which is sweeter than honey: the minister of God answers him: This is the bread which the Lord has given you to eat. What this bread is, hear: *The Word,* he says, *which God ordained.* *1562* This ordinance of God therefore, this nourishment, feeds the soul of the wise man, and enlightens and sweetens it, shining with the flash of truth, and soothing as with a certain honeycomb, so by the sweetness of divers virtues and by the word of wisdom. For *the honeycombs of honey are good words,* as it is written in the Proverbs [var: Vulg. Prov. 16:24 *Favus mellis composita verba*, "Well-ordered words are a honeycomb"]. Therefore, when it has tasted the fruit of sweetness, impatient, it hastens on to more perfect things, saying: [1875B]

## 17.

*[n: (Vers. 4-7.)] Bring me into the house of wine, set love in me, strengthen me with ointments, hedge me about with apples; for I am of wounded love. His left hand is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me. I have adjured you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the powers and strengths of the field, if ye shall rouse and rouse up again love, until she will* [var: Vulg. Cant. 2:4–7 *Introduxit me in cellam vinariam, ordinavit in me caritatem. Fulcite me floribus, stipate me malis, quia amore langueo… Adjuro vos, filiae Jerusalem, per capreas cervosque camporum, ne suscitetis, neque evigilare faciatis dilectam, quoadusque ipsa velit*, "He brought me into the wine cellar… Prop me up with flowers… because I languish with love… I adjure you… by the roes and harts of the fields, that ye stir not up, nor awake the beloved, till she please"]. Rightly does she seek him, rightly does she desire him; because our commander has well disposed all things in this course and journey. First of all he judged that this tent must be strengthened upon the foundation of faith. Then, if any lodging of ours is rough and dry and steep, yet that Commander of ours parts asunder the troubled things, waters the dry, makes fruitful the deserts. If there is anything of bitterness, anything of temptation, anything of weakness, our leader both tempers the bitter things, [1875C] and mitigates the anxious; he loosens hard things and strengthens them into strong.

## 18.

Wherever the king of the earth wills his army to rest, he appoints no ignoble village, none lacking in supplies, no sandy and bare places of growth: but a city noble in buildings, restored and rich in stores, or a pleasant field, or one green with pastures, or wooded and level places convenient for a standing camp. If therefore the kings of the earth know how to provide advantages for those who follow them, how much more does God, who is good, know how to dispose things profitable for those who love him? And first, if an unknown road must be taken, guides of the ways are chosen, who run before the column; but these commanders reckon this an injury to themselves: God, however, went before, when the Hebrews made their journey; and indeed he spoke to them in the pillar of cloud. And that thou mayest know [1875D] that he went before, *God,* he says, *went before them: by day indeed in a pillar of cloud, showing them the way; but by night in a pillar of fire, and the pillar of cloud did not fail by day [n: (Exod. XIII, 21 et 22)]* [var: Vulg. Exod. 13:21–22 *Dominus autem praecedebat eos ad ostendendam viam… Numquam defuit columna nubis per diem*, "And the Lord went before them… Never did the pillar of cloud fail by day"]. Following this pillar going before us, which does not fail lest she herself should fail, the Church was refreshed by its shadow; and therefore she says: *In his shadow I have desired, and sat down, and his fruit is sweet in my jaws [n: (Sup., v. 3)]*; because she was fed by the Lord, and led into the place of pastures and the water of refreshment. [1876A]

## 19.

*Bring me into the house of wine.* Out of the supplies made ready, she seeks to go forward to another lodging, in which she may take the grace of the mysteries and the sweetness of gladness. Hence too, advancing on the true way, she says: *Order love in me.* Good standing camps, where there is the fullness of charity. Charity cannot be without faith; for there are as it were three sureties of the Church: hope, faith, charity; when hope has gone before, when faith has been founded, charity is set in order, the Church is joined.

## 20.

*Strengthen me,* she says, *with ointments, hedge me about with apples.* Thou hast other lodgings, to which the Church succeeds with delight. These lodgings are of the cross of Christ and of the burial, in which the Church is wounded, but with the wound of charity; for it is a wound that Christ received, but it is ointment that he poured out. [1876B] It is an apple that hung; this apple the Church has tasted, and says: *And his fruit is sweet in my jaws.* And that thou mayest know that the Lord is an apple, thou hast read above: *As an apple among the trees of the wood, so is my cousin.* We too confess a wound, when we preach Christ crucified, but we are a good odour to God; because the cross of Christ is to the Jews a scandal, to the Greeks foolishness, but to us the power of God and wisdom. With this wound the Church is wounded, when she preaches the death of her Saviour, but this wound is of charity. And indeed he who believes not, denies: he who loves, confesses. The Manichean denies, the Christian confesses; and therefore it is written: *More useful are the wounds of a friend than the willing kisses of an enemy [n: (Prov. XXVII, 6)]* [var: Vulg. *Meliora sunt vulnera diligentis quam fraudulenta oscula odientis*, "Better are the wounds of one who loves than the deceitful kisses of one who hates"]. Beautifully therefore the Church says: *For wounded with charity [1876C] am I.*

## 21.

Let us bare our members to the good wound, let us bare them to the chosen arrow: this arrow is Christ, who says: *He has set me as a chosen arrow [n: (Esai. XLIX, 2)]*; it is good therefore to be wounded with this arrow. This advance is not of a middling lodging; not all can say that they are wounded with love. *1563* The apostles said it, when they were stoned for Christ and preached Christ: Paul said it, when he was beaten thrice with rods, and disputed day and night that Christ was to be adored by the nations: the martyrs say this, who are wounded for Christ; and because they have deserved to be wounded for his name, they love the more. Coming therefore to this lodging, to this advance, that she may offer her sons for Christ, that she may take upon her the wounds [1876D] of charity, the Church, finding as it were the good nourishments of faith, has herself tasted the fruits of piety, and has begun to exhort the rest, saying: *Taste and see that the Lord is sweet.* And the Church added, saying:



## 22.

*His left hand is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me.* And these lodgings belong to the Church as she walks upon the royal way; *For there are many mansions with my Father [n: (Joan. XIV, 2)]*. The Lord Jesus bears witness: *And behold I come, and summon you [n: (Ibid.)]*. Good is the lodging of wisdom, and whether in her left hand or in her right, it is a good lodging; because it is the royal way. And therefore [1877A] the wise messengers say: *By the royal way will we go; we will not turn aside to the right hand, nor to the left, until we pass thy borders [n: (Num. XX, 17)]*. This the messengers sent by Moses said to the king of Edom, that is, to the earthly king; because all earthly things, whether on the right or on the left, are evil. An evil lodging is folly, an evil lodging is intemperance; and therefore the Hebrew passes through these lodgings: he does not turn aside to them, but passes through, that he may come to the left hand of wisdom and to her right, and abide in these, where are the riches of simplicity, where glory, where length of life is. *For length of life is in her right hand,* as Solomon says: *and in her left hand riches and glory [n: (Prov. III, 16)]* [var: Vulg. *Longitudo dierum in dextera ejus*, "Length of days is in her right hand"]: in the right hand is life, in the left rest. Would that I might rest upon her left hand, and not seek pillows! *For woe to them who [1877B] sew pillows,* Ezekiel says [n: (Ezech. XIII, 18)] [var: Vulg. *Vae quae consuunt pulvillos sub omni cubito manus*, "Woe to them that sew cushions under every elbow"]. These lodgings the good leader prepares for his Church, and by the ways of wisdom he directs her journey. And indeed the wise man praises her ways: *Her ways are good ways, and all her paths are in peace [n: (Prov. III, 17)]* [var: Vulg. *Viae ejus viae pulchrae, et omnes semitae illius pacificae*, "Her ways are beautiful ways, and all her paths peaceable"].

## 23.

*His left hand under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me.* These things the bride speaks concerning Christ, the soul concerning the Word of God: but Christ is the same as the Word of God and wisdom. Blessed therefore is the soul whom wisdom embraces. Great is the hand of wisdom, great her right hand; she embraces the whole soul; for she is wholly fortified who is betrothed to the Word of God. For the fullness of wisdom is to fear God: the soul therefore which fears God fortifies itself with a full defence. Wisdom sends her left hand under her neck, [1877C] and her right hand into her embrace. Both her arms indeed are stretched out unto useful things; yet the several hands of wisdom have their own proper gifts: *In the right hand is length of life,* *in the left hand riches and glory.* With good gifts assuredly is her hand endowed on both sides; yet they have herein a variety of their own office, that they comprehend both present and future times; so that the left hand is the rewarder of present things, the right of things to come.

## 24.

We can know this also from the prophecy of the holy patriarch Israel; for when he had set his right hand upon Ephraim, and his left upon Manasseh, and Joseph had wished to change them out of consideration of their age, so that he might place the father's right hand upon the head of Manasseh the elder son, he would not, and said: *I know, son, and this one also shall be for a people, and this one shall be exalted, [1877D] but the younger brother shall be greater than he [n: (Genes. XLVIII, 19)]* [var: Vulg. *Scio, fili mi, scio: et iste quidem erit in populos, et multiplicabitur; sed frater ejus minor major erit illo*, "I know, my son, I know: and this one indeed shall be for peoples, and shall be multiplied"]. In what greater? In this surely, that he was preferred in the blessing, since those who came after said of Ephraim: *God make you as he made Ephraim and Manasseh [n: (Ibid., 20)]* [var: Vulg. *Faciat tibi Deus sicut Ephraim et sicut Manasse*, "God make thee as Ephraim and as Manasses" — singular]: or because his seed is a multitude of nations, which in this world chose labour, believing in the Lord Jesus, that it might have the consolation of things to come. But the seed of Manasseh is the people of forgetfulness, which, having forgotten its own author, has been exalted for a time in this world, and shall pay heavy penalties for the rest; because it denied its own God and Lord. [1878A]

## 25.

We have said this in order to prove that those things are preferable which are to come. And indeed the left hand of wisdom is under the head of the bride, but the right hand is higher, which embraces the whole bride. And so that hand is as it were the prop of present quiet, upon which the soul, leaning, rests. She has where to lay back her head, because the left hand of wisdom is riches and glory. For that season these things soothe, and are in part a solace; and therefore: *The Son of man had not where to lay back his* *1564* *head [n: (Matth. VIII, 20)]*; *because, when he was rich, he was made poor [n: (II Cor. VIII, 9)]* [var: Vulg. *quoniam propter vos egenus factus est, cum esset dives*, "because for your sakes he became needy, whereas he was rich"]. Nor did he seek any glory of this world; because he had come, not to succour a portion, but the whole human race, saying: *Ye are angry with me because I made a whole man sound on the sabbath [n: (Joan. VII, 23)]*. that is, wholly — not that he might enrich him [1878B] by the bestowal of substance, not with the marks of honours, not that he might heap him up with an increase of worldly glory; for these things have not the fullness of blessedness and of grace: but wholly, that is, so as to embrace the length of eternal life. For the life which is in the right hand of wisdom is not like the common life, but is length of life, so that he who receives life from wisdom may obtain not scantiness of life, but perpetuity and the length of eternity.

## 26.

A good garment is the eternity of life. And so the bride, upheld by the riches of pomegranates, that is, of divers and innumerable fruits, and chiefly by the odours of faith, of grace, of wisdom and of glory and of eternal life, which are about the left hand and the right hand of the bridegroom, rouses Christ through holy souls by the applause of the congregations that praise him, [1878C] saying:

## 27.

*I have adjured you, daughters of Jerusalem, by the powers* from *the strengths of the field, if ye shall rouse and rouse up again love, until she will.* That field which the Lord blessed is not this earthly one, either bristling with woods, or broken with torrents, or useless for vines, or stony with barren gravel, or gaping and dry with drought, or wet with blood, or uncultivated with brambles and thorns; but that field of which the Lord says: *And the beauty of the field is with me [n: (Psalm. XLIX, 19)]*. In this field is found that grape which, being pressed, poured out blood and washed the world: in this field is that fig tree under which the saints shall rest, refreshed by the sweetness of spiritual grace: in this field is that fruitful olive tree, flowing with the ointment of the Lord's peace: in this field flower [1878D] the pomegranates, which cover very many fruits under one defence of faith, and cherish them with a certain embrace of charity.

## 28.

Since therefore wisdom and honourable brightness are so far perfect, if they have charity (for the fullness of the law [n: (Rom. XIII, 10)] is charity), she wills charity to be roused and roused up again: to be roused in the Old Testament, to be roused up again in the New. Charity is God, as we read; charity is Christ [n: (I Joan. IV, 8)] [var: Vulg. *Deus caritas est*, "God is charity"]. He is roused *as a lion, and a lion's whelp; that he may go up out of the seed of Judah [n: (Gen. XLIX, 9)]* [var: Vulg. *Catulus leonis Juda: ad praedam, fili mi, ascendisti*, "Juda is a lion's whelp: to the prey, my son, thou art gone up"]: he is roused up again as [1879A] one sleeping, as one reclining; because he was raised from the dead not by a human majesty, but by his own and his Father's. Whence Scripture says: *Who shall rouse him up?* For neither an angel nor an alien Power could rouse him, since he himself was rousing others. Therefore when he here says: *If ye shall rouse and rouse up again love, until she will;* he speaks of those who can fittingly preach his resurrection, so as to kindle in their hearers the ardour of faith and of devotion: or Christ is roused in those who first come to him, and roused up again in those who, after they have come, have slept. Christ therefore sleeps for the negligent, and is roused in the saints.

## 29.

This charity therefore she rouses and rouses up again, until she receive his voice and summon his presence: because, being sought, he not only comes, but even [1879B] comes leaping. But by now the bridegroom was at hand, so as to prove that which, while we are yet speaking, he says: *I am here.* He is present, because he is roused by the daughters of Judah, as it were roused up again by the daughters of Jerusalem. The Church hears the sound of his voice, and says:

## 30.

*[n: (Vers. 8.)] The voice of my cousin: behold, here he comes, leaping over the mountains, bounding over the hills* [var: Vulg. *Vox dilecti mei; ecce iste venit saliens in montibus, transiliens colles*, "The voice of my beloved; behold he cometh leaping upon the mountains"]. This book is like a maniple, having many noble persons, as it were a triumphal maniple, and signifying many acts, which we can rather understand than hold expressed. For, as though she had heard the bridegroom coming and [sic: *collonquentem*] with certain who were journeying along with him, the bride says: *The voice of my cousin.* While she speaks with the daughters of Jerusalem, and asks that they stir up and rouse up again the bridegroom, suddenly, perceiving as it were the sound of a voice sent [1879C] from afar, she says: *The voice of my cousin,* announcing him whom before she was seeking to have announced to her. Him whom she desired to be roused by others, believing him now, roused up again by her own prayer, to be coming from the Father, she gladly says: *The voice of my cousin.* Well did she add, *my cousin;* that no other women, but she alone, his cousin, might claim his coming for herself. *1565*



## 31.

*Behold*, she says, *he is come* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:8 *ecce iste venit*, "behold, he cometh"]: still am I seeking him, and he has already come; still am I catching at intercessions that he may come, and he is already near at hand; I long for charity to be roused up in me, I deem myself wounded of charity, and charity herself hastens to me the more. I said, Come; he leaps and leaps over. I ask him to come with grace; he works increases of graces; and while he comes, he carries increases of grace with him, [1879D] and by coming he acquires, because he too is eager to please his own beloved. He leaps upon the high places, that he may ascend to the bride; for the bride's marriage-chamber is the tribunal of Christ. He leaps upon Adam, he leaps over the Synagogue; he leaps upon the Gentiles, he leaps over the Jews.

## 32.

Let us see him leaping He leaps from heaven into the Virgin, from the womb into the manger, from the Jordan into the cross, from the cross into the tomb, into heaven from the sepulchre. Prove to me, David, that he leaps; prove that he runs. For thou hast said: *He hath rejoiced as a giant to run the way. His going forth is from the height of heaven, and his meeting even to the height thereof; and there is none that can hide himself from his heat [n: (Psal. XVIII, 6 et 7)]*. And now he leaps, and now he runs, from [1880A] the heart of the Father upon his saints, from the East upon the West, from the North upon the South. This is he who ascends above the sunset, he himself above the heavens of heavens toward the East, he himself ascends above the mountains, he himself above the hills.

## 33.

Would that I, wretched man, might say it, and that my soul might say: *Behold, he is come*, and he is come not upon earthly things, not upon the valleys, but he is come *Leaping upon the mountains* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:8 *saliens in montibus*, "leaping in the mountains"]; for God is the God of mountains, not of valleys: where therefore he leaps, he leaps upon mountains. If thou be a mountain, he leaps upon thee; he leaps upon Isaiah, he leaps upon Jeremiah, he leaps upon Peter, John, James. *The mountains are round about him [n: (Psalm. CXXIV, 2)]*: if one cannot be a mountain and thou dost not prevail, be at least a hill, that Christ may ascend upon thee; and if he leaps over, that his passing may guard thee. [1880B]

## 34.

We have spoken of Christ and the Church; let us speak of the soul and the Word. The soul of the just man is the bride of the Word. If she should desire, if she should long, if she should pray and pray unceasingly, and pray without any disputing, and stretch herself wholly toward the Word: suddenly she seems to hear the voice of him whom she does not see, and in the fear of her senses she recognizes the odor of his divinity, which those who believe well for the most part experience. Suddenly the nostrils of the soul are filled with spiritual grace, and she feels the breath of his presence breathe upon her, of him whom she seeks, and she says: Behold, this is the very one whom I seek, the very one whom I desire.

## 35.

Is it not so, that when we ponder something out of the Scriptures and cannot find the explanation of it, while we doubt, while we seek, suddenly he seems to us to ascend, as it were, upon the mountains, that is, upon the loftiest doctrines. [1880C] Then, appearing to us as it were upon the hills, he enlightens the mind, that he may pour into our senses what seemed difficult to be found: and so the Word becomes, as it were from absent, present in our hearts. And again, when something is somewhat obscure to us, the Word is as it were withdrawn, and we long for the coming of him as though he were absent; and again he appears, he shows himself to us as though he were present with us in the knowing of those things which we seek.

## 36.

He leaps, then, often in the heart of each one; he leaps over and goes out and returns: if thou shouldst follow, if thou shouldst seek, if by the welcome disputation of faithful teachers thou shouldst beg that the Word which had gone out and had passed over be raised up again for thee. Like her who sought and found, who said: *My brother has passed by, my soul went out at his word [n: (Cant. V, 6)]* [var: Vulg. *at ille declinaverat atque transierat; anima mea liquefacta est, ut locutus est*, "but he had turned aside and passed on; my soul melted when he spoke"]. Therefore even if he leaps upon the mountains, follow; even if [1880D] upon the hills, follow. For there are found on the mountains and on the hills the hunters of the Lord, who track out those who are taken for life; for thus God said through Jeremiah: *Behold, I send many fishers and many hunters, and they shall hunt them upon every mountain and upon every hill [n: (Jerem. XVI, 16)]* [var: Vulg. *de omni monte et de omni colle*, "from every mountain and from every hill"]. There, then, let the people of God be sought and found in the doctrine, the grace, the discipline of Peter and of Paul; that they be not in the deep valley, where there is weeping, but on the mountains from which Christ enlightens each man. And when we read Peter, Christ enlightens; and when we read Paul, Christ enlightens. Paul healed, Christ enlightened; for when the name of the Lord Jesus had been invoked, he rose again, by whose gift he was made [1881A] whole. Peter raised up a dead woman, Christ enlightened; and therefore he says: *Thou dost enlighten wonderfully from the everlasting mountains [n: (Psal. LXXV, 5)]*.

## 37.

We therefore who cannot be mountains, let us stand *1566* on the mountains or on the hills; so that when the Lord shall have sent his fishers and hunters to hunt those who are upon every mountain, or upon every hill, that is, in the precepts of the law and of the prophets, and who have all their conversation in the knowledge of the New and the Old Testament, they may find us ready and may gather us like good ears of corn, reapers sent at the opportune season; because if anyone shall have been found outside the mountain or the hill, he will not be able to be gathered as a good ear by those who, that we may make mention of another comparison, shall be sent to separate the wheat [1881B] from the chaff. The Lord therefore has skilled ministers of very many operations: the same are fishers, the same also are hunters, and reapers. If in the time of harvest, at which these are directed to reap, thou shouldst wait ripe, thou wilt be able to behold him leaping upon the mountains, and thou wilt see the Lord Jesus like a roe or a young hart upon the mountains of Bethel. For he leaps upon the Church, which is the house of bread, in that he strengthens the hearts of the faithful; whence the bride also says of him:

## 38.

*[n: (Vers. 9.)] My cousin is like a roe or a young hart, upon the mountains of Bethel;* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:9 *similis est dilectus meus capreae hinnuloque cervorum*, "my beloved is like a roe and a young hart," with no mountains of Bethel] for he leaps upon the Church, which is the house of God, in that he strengthens the hearts of the faithful. Rightly, like a little roe, because the goat is pastured in high places. She is called *dorcas* from seeing, [1881C] for the *dorcadion* is of keener sight. What is fitter to Christ than this, who saw the Father whom no one has seen, or, if anyone has seen him in Christ, the Son himself revealed him? Rightly *like a young hart*: the fawn is as it were a son, in whom the force of the paternal nature has grown, so that hidden things do not lie concealed from him, serpents flee, poisons do not harm. Accordingly the serpent, led forth out of his lurking-places, who was being led forth out of the man, said: *Why hast thou come before the time to torment us [n: (Matth. VIII, 29)]*. Let us therefore gaze upon this leaping fawn, that we may not be able to fear the serpent.

## 39.

*Behold, he himself stands behind our wall, looking through the windows, peering through the nets.* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:9 *prospiciens per cancellos*, "looking through the lattices"] He comes, then, and first is behind the wall, that he may loose the enmities of soul and body, the wall being taken away which seemed to bring a hindrance [1881D] to concord. Then he peers through the windows: what the windows are, hear the Prophet saying: *The windows are opened from heaven [n: (Esai. XXIV, 18)]* [var: Vulg. Isa 24:18 *cataractae de excelsis apertae sunt*, "the floodgates from on high are opened"]. He signifies therefore the prophets, through whom the Lord looked upon the human race, before he himself came down to the earth. And today too, if any soul seeks him much, she will merit much of mercy; since to him who seeks much, much is owed. If therefore any soul should seek him more earnestly, she hears his voice from afar: and although she seeks him from others, she hears his voice before those very ones from whom she seeks. She sees him coming to her with a leap, that is, hastening and running [1882A] and leaping over those who with feeble heart cannot take in his power; then peering through the riddles of the prophets, by reading them and holding fast their words; for she sees him peering, but as it were through a window, not as though he were yet present. She sees him raised above the nets. What is this? Unless perhaps because those nets are nets to us, they are not nets to him, in that that soul is still within things sensible and worldly, which are wont to catch the mind of man and to wrap it in a certain fold of their own.



## 40.

*Behold, he himself stands behind our wall, looking through the windows, peering through the nets.* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:9 *prospiciens per cancellos*, "looking through the lattices"] For the Church, or the soul of the just man, had seen him leaping like a fawn upon the mountains, and, first catching sight of him suddenly behind the wall of her house, peering through the windows, [1882B] raised above the nets, she exults and rejoices, because she too is loved by the Bridegroom. And he, as though he himself also were wounded by the beauty of charity, when at first he was away from his beloved, was present at her asking for kisses (for he is asked when it is said [n: (Cant. I, 1)]: *Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth* ); then did not spurn the prayers and blandishments of the bride as she offered him her breasts; and kindly led her into the inner parts of the house; then, playing as with a wanton love, because he wished to try the senses of her who loved him, going out often that he might be sought by the Bride, returning often that he might be invited to kisses, stood behind the wall, peered through the windows, raised above the nets; that he might neither be wholly absent, nor as it were wholly enter in, and that he himself might call the bride to himself; that the exchanges of coming to one another might become the more welcome, and that they might kindle love [1882C] with mutual words. *1567*

## 41.

*[n: (Vers. 10.)] Rise up,* he says, *come, my neighbor, my well-formed one, my dove;* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:10 *surge, propera, amica mea, columba mea, formosa mea, et veni*, "arise, make haste, my love, my dove, my beautiful one, and come"] and thou too, if thou hast a wall well founded, not that middle wall which divides the members of one house, but one built upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets, so that its framed structure may grow into a temple, and may not sever its inner parts but fortify them. If therefore thou hast in thee the building of God, and thy windows stand ever open toward the East, the Word comes, *He stands behind thy wall [n: (Cant. II, 9)]*; for *the eyes of the Lord are upon the just [n: (Psal. XXXIII, 16)]*; *he peers through thy windows [n: (Cant. II, 9)]*.

## 42.

What are these windows? We read of the windows of which Jeremiah says: *Death has entered through the windows* [1882D] [n: (Jerem. IX, 21)] [var: Vulg. *quia ascendit mors per fenestras nostras*, "for death is come up through our windows"], through which avarice entered, lust entered. Thine eye is a window. If thou hast looked upon a woman to lust after her, death has entered through thine eye: if thou hast looked upon a widow's estate, or the treasures of a minor, and hast stirred up thy covetings, *Death has entered through the window.* As therefore death enters through these, life enters too: if, looking upon the comeliness of a consecrated girl, thou reverencest the Lord Jesus, in that the age of old age has come into her tender years, a life unspotted, and thou thyself offerest thy daughter, that she may be consecrated with the holy veil; if thou lookest upon a minor's estate not like an anxious invader, but guardest it like a diligent parent with religious [1883A] affection: through these windows Christ peers, that he may call the bride.

## 43.

*Raised above the nets:* rightly *raised;* because he alone is the one whom the nets of sins have not enwrapped. We were all within the nets, nay rather we are still within the nets; because no one is without sin save Jesus alone, whom, knowing no sin, the Father made sin for us; for indeed he delivered him to the snares, delivered him to the nets: sending him not in the sin in which all men were, but in the likeness of sinful flesh, that of sin he might condemn sin in the flesh. The flesh was sin, according to that saying, because it had been condemned by a hereditary curse; sin was the enticement and the handmaid of sin. The Lord Jesus came, and in flesh liable to sin he practiced the warfare of virtue: our members [1883B] have been made now no longer weapons of lust, but weapons of virtue; for where the incitements of lust were, there now are the dwellings of chastity. Here therefore he came to the snares, but willingly: he came to the nets, but secure. All things were full of nets, crammed with snares; hear him who says: *In this way wherein I walked, the proud have hidden a snare for me [n: (Psal. CXLI, 4)]* [var: Vulg. Ps 141:4 *in via hac qua ambulabam absconderunt laqueum mihi*, without *superbi*, "the proud"]. And in the book of the wisdom of Sirach thou art admonished to know that thou walkest in the midst of snares [n: (Eccli. IX, 20)]. As many vices, so many nets: as many sins, so many snares.

## 44.

Already the hereditary bonds held thee; Jesus came to the snares, that he might loose Adam: he came to set free what had perished. We were all held by the nets, no one could rescue another, since he could not rescue himself. Such a one therefore was needful, whom the chains of human generation, liable to offenses, [1883C] would not hold, whom avarice had not taken, whom guile had not bound. That one alone was Jesus, who, though he had surrounded himself with the bonds of this flesh, was not captured, was not fettered: but bursting them and dissolving them, he called the Church the more to himself, *Peering through the snares, raised above the nets;* that she too might learn not to be held by chains. In fact he was so far from being far from the chains, that for us he underwent even death, yet not made a slave of death, but free among the dead; for he was free who had the power of loosing death. In fine, let him himself teach thee who says: *Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up again [n: (Joan. II, 19)]* [var: Vulg. John 2:19 *et in tribus diebus excitabo illud*, "and in three days I will raise it up"]. Let us hear, then, what he says:

## 45.

[n: (Vers. 11 - 13)]. *Rise up, come, my neighbor;* that is, rise from the dead, rise up from the chains by which thou wast held [1883D] encompassed. Rise up, for I have risen again for thee: loose the bonds of iniquity, for I have already loosed them for thee: come, for already the nets are loosed, the Virgin has borne, a child is born of a virgin, he owes nothing to the inheritance of woman, as the son of a woman he is not held: behold the middle wall of the enclosure now loosed, which divided the concord of the inward affections, and stirred up in diverse directions the dissensions of bodily passions. Come therefore secure; I long to see thy face and to hear thy voice: come, that thou mayest no longer see me through nets, but, beloved, mayest enjoy face to face the countenance of thy lover.

## 46.

And let these things be run through thus far in the moral sense. But those [1884A] mystical things, if we can, let us behold at least in their outermost line. The Bride was sitting within the house, devoted to the Lord, within the wall of the Law and *1568* of the prophets, founded upon a building of spiritual stones, which shut in and walled about the royal house, full of delight and gladness. She marveled at the royal treasures, and gazed upon them anxiously, longing to take to herself the wisdom which might show her these riches. She was in secret, but she was seeking the interpreter of that very secret. The Lord Jesus came, leaping upon the mountains. To us he seemed to come late, but he was hastening. He was leaping, in fine, and leaping over, that he might leap over the bodily and stony doctrines of the Jews. He stood behind the wall of the house which was in the Old Testament, peering through the window of the Law and the caverns of the prophets. [1884B]

## 47.

Not yet were the vestibules of that house open, not yet had the keys of wisdom laid open the barriers of the gates by which the inner parts of the Law were shut; yet from the higher part, that is, the spiritual part, looking forth he calls the Church, that she, rising sublime through the Law and the prophets to the summit of the Gospel, may tread with fearless foot upon the nets and the knots of Jewish interpretation. And therefore she is called *Neighbor*, that she may cleave to Christ and not seek worldly things: therefore *Well-formed*, that she may bear the shapely feet of those who preach the Gospel: therefore *Dove*, that she may seek spiritual things and leave earthly things behind.

## 48.

*Behold,* he says, *the winter is past, the rain is gone, it has departed to itself, flowers have been seen in the earth, the time of cutting is come.* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:11-12 *jam enim hiems transiit; imber abiit, et recessit. Flores apparuerunt in terra nostra; tempus putationis advenit*, "for now the winter is past; the rain is gone and departed. The flowers have appeared in our land; the time of pruning is come"] Before the coming of Christ it was winter: Christ came, he made summer. Then all things were destitute of flowers, [1884C] bare of virtues: Christ suffered, and all things began to be made fruitful with the buds of a new grace. The rain of overflowing wantonness is gone, and the clouds risen from foul disgraces are now dispersed in the summer serenity of a pure conscience. Therefore they do not escape whose flight is made in winter; because they do not follow the passion of the Lord, they do not take up their cross, and they follow Christ.

## 49.

Rain hinders flowers: but now flowers are seen in the earth. Good flowers are the apostles, who poured out the fragrance of their diverse writings and works. *The time of cutting is come,* when ripe grain is stored in the barns; and he who reaps receives a reward. *The voice of the turtledove has been heard,* because she has found a nest for herself; for the Church is the house of chastity. *The fig tree,* which for its unfruitfulness was ordered to be cut down, *has now begun to bear fruit.* [1884D] The vine carried over out of Egypt, destroyed by the casting down of its walls, is now not assailed by beasts; nor is it unshapely with brambles, but now fragrant with flowers.



## 50.

*Rise up,* he says, *come, my neighbor, my well-formed one, my dove, my perfect one; neighbor* indeed by the desire of faith, *well-formed* by the comeliness of virtue, *dove* by spiritual grace; for *the wings of the dove covered with silver* signify that eternal power, and the flight of the dove declared the presence of the Holy Spirit. Christ therefore calls her to himself that she may come, since she was already coming endowed with spiritual dowries. *For behold,* he says, *the winter is past, the rain is gone, it has departed [1885A] to itself, flowers seen in the earth.* See in what manner the holy Church is invited. *The winter,* he says, *has departed to itself,* lest, being naked, she dread the winter, a winter not of the season but of infirmity, which strips the fruitful field of the soul of every flower. For there is a winter not of the earthly sun, there is a winter of the mind, when cold glides into the soul, when the warmth of the spirit fades away, when the vigor of sense is loosened, when excessive humour overflows and weighs down the mind, when the inward sight grows dark. And therefore the Lord says: *See that your flight be not in winter or on the sabbath [n: (Matth. XXIV, 20)]* [var: Vulg. Matt 24:20 *orate autem ut non fiat fuga vestra in hieme vel sabbato*, "but pray that your flight be not in winter or on the sabbath"]; for it is good that the day of judgment or of death should come then, when the kindly temperateness of the soul is in vigor, when the heavenly mystery shines back with serene light, when our heart burns within us. For then Christ is present, as Ammaon and Cleophas testify [1885B] in the Gospel, saying: *Was not our heart burning within us in the way, when he had opened the Scriptures in us [n: (Luc. XXIV, 32)]*. And the soul is in vigor when the flower too is seen in the earth. Who is this flower of good fragrance, save he who said: *I am the flower of the field and the lily of the valleys [n: (Supra, vers. 1)]*: of whom also it is written in Isaiah: *A rod shall go forth from the root of Jesse, and a rod shall ascend from the root, and a flower shall go forth from the rod [n: (Esai. XI, 1)]* [var: Vulg. Isa 11:1 *et egredietur virga de radice Jesse, et flos de radice ejus ascendet*, "and there shall come forth a rod out of the root of Jesse, and a flower shall rise up out of his root," with no middle member]. The root indeed is the family of the Jews, the rod is Mary, the flower of Mary is Christ: and when he shines forth in our earth, and is fragrant in the field of the soul, or blooms in his own Church, we can neither fear cold nor dread rain, but may await the day of judgment. And therefore the Church, that she might see this flower, was hastening with all zeal, as she herself testifies *1569* [1885C] in what follows, saying: *In the bed by nights I sought him whom my soul loves [n: (Cant. III, 1)]* [var: Vulg. Cant 3:1 *in lectulo meo per noctes quaesivi quem diligit anima mea*, "in my little bed by night I sought him whom my soul loveth"].

## 50.

*The fig tree has put forth its green figs.* The comparison of that tree is apt for the Synagogue, because just as that tree, overflowing with flowing leaves, disappoints the hope of its owner by an empty expectation of the yields that were hoped for: so also in the Synagogue, while its teachers, unfruitful in works, glory only in words as in overflowing leaves, the empty shade of the Law prevails; and the hope of a false yield, once awaited, mocks the prayers of the believing people.

## 51.

There is also in the nature of the tree that whereby thou mayest the more believe that the likeness of the Synagogue is expressed by comparison with it. For indeed, if thou inquire diligently, thou wilt find the habit of this tree's nature distinct from the practice of the rest; [1885D] for others bear the flower before the fruit, and mark out the apples that are to come by the flower that heralds them: this one alone from the beginning buds forth apples in place of flowers; in the others the flower is shaken off and the apples are born, in this one the apples fall off, that apples may succeed them. Therefore those earlier ones, void of fruit, come forth in the place of the flower: thus, by a certain premature habit of being born, they that know not the order of nature cannot keep the benefit of nature. For indeed where the bud is wont to thrust itself out from the middle of the bark, there each of the tiniest apples of this kind breaks forth: concerning which we read in the Song [1886A] of Songs: *The fig tree has put forth its green figs.* And so, while the other shoots whiten in the first spring, the fig alone knows not how to grow white with a flower of its own; perhaps because there is for these no riper use in their apples. For when others succeed, which are spat out as though degenerate, and the weak root being dry, they are stripped away, those being renewed whose sap is more serviceable. Yet some very few remain, and do not fall, upon which this yield has smiled, so that they push forth from the middle of two twigs by a short tendril breaking out, whereby, covered by twin defenses, as though in the lap of nature their parent, they grow up by the fostering of a fuller sap. These, called forth by the mildness of a gentler air, and grown to maturity through longer seasons, when they have put off the wild temper of their former sap, are preferred to the rest in appearance and in the grace of ripeness. [1886B]

## 52.

Look now upon the observances and the minds of the Jews, who, as it were the first fruits of the ill-bearing Synagogue, fell after the likeness of what is slipping away, that apples of our kind, destined to abide beyond the age, might succeed them. For indeed the first people of the Synagogue, weak as it were with a dry root of works, could not draw in the richness of natural wisdom. And therefore it fell like useless fruit, that as it were from the same tendrils of a fruitful tree, out of the richness of the ancient religion, the new people of the Church might come forth. Therefore he who was ceased to be, that this one might begin who was not: yet the first out of Israel, whom the branch of a stronger nature had lifted up, under the shadow of the Law and of the cross, colored in the bosom of each by a twin sap, after the pattern of the ripening green fig, surpassed the rest in the grace of most beautiful fruits, to whom [1886C] it is said: *You shall sit upon twelve thrones, judging the twelve tribes of Israel [n: (Matth. XIX, 28)]* [var: Vulg. Matt 19:28 *sedebitis et vos super sedes duodecim*, "you also shall sit upon twelve seats"].

## 53.

*The vines in flower have given their fragrance [n: (Sup., v. 5)]*. Well is the Church designated by the vine; for as the vine is wedded to trees, so the body is joined to the soul, and the soul also to the body: as the vine, while it is bound, is raised up, and when cut back is not diminished but increased; so the holy people, while it is bound, is stripped: while it is humbled, it is lifted up: while it is cut back, it is crowned. Nay more, as a tender shoot, cut off from the old tree, is grafted into the offspring of another root: so this holy people, the scars of the old shoot being smoothed away, grows up cherished in that new sign of the cross as in the lap of a devoted parent; and the Holy Spirit, poured into this prison-like body, as though let down into deep [1886D] trenches of the earth, washes away by the watering of the water of salvation whatever is foul, and raises the bearing of our members into heavenly discipline.

## 54.

This vine the diligent husbandman is wont to dig, to water, to strive over; and, the mounds of earthy mass being cleared away, he is accustomed now to scorch the works of our body with the sun, now to drench them with rain, and to hem the field close, lest the bud be hurt by brambles, lest the shade of the leaves run riot, and lest the unfruitful boasting of words, by overshadowing the virtues, hinder the ripeness of the natural disposition. [1887A]

## 55.

*[n: (Vers. 14.)] And come,* he says, *my dove, in the covering of the rock, beside the outwork;* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:14 *columba mea in foraminibus petrae, in caverna maceriae*, "my dove in the clefts of the rock, in the hollow of the wall"] that is, come beside the Gospel: the bulwarks of thy faith are the deeds of Christ; the supports of thy wall *1570* are the words of the Lord; the passion of the Lord's body is thy strength.

## 56.

*Show me thy face, and make thy voice known; for thy voice is sweet, and thy face comely.* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:14 *sonet vox tua in auribus meis: vox enim tua dulcis*, "let thy voice sound in mine ears: for thy voice is sweet"] Sweet is thy voice; *for with the mouth confession is made unto salvation:* and comely the face; because she who is not ashamed of her author is not confounded at her Redeemer. She shows therefore her face, bearing before her the seal of the cross; and she makes her voice known, taking up the authority of preaching. For by the covering of the body of Christ, whereby he was redeemed from sin, he finds the defense of spiritual grace, so that he may both perceive and speak the things that are for his salvation. Sweet [1887B] therefore is the voice, which spoke in the divine testimonies: *Comely the face,* which was not confounded in the sight of kings.

## 57.

*Rise up,* he says, *come, my neighbor;* that is, rise up from the delights of the world, rise up from earthly things, and come to me, thou who still labourest and art laden, thou who art anxious for the things that are of the world: come above the world, come to me, for I have overcome the world: come near to me, now beautiful with the comeliness of eternal life, now a dove, that is, gentle and meek, now wholly full of spiritual grace. Rightly therefore she ought no longer to fear the nets, since he calls the soul to himself who could not be caught by the temptations and the nets of the world. For since we men walk in the midst of snares, through the appetite for food we are liable at once to nets and to snares. [1887C] He, set in a body, did not fear the nets, but was raised above the nets, that is, above the temptations of the world and the passions of the body; nay, he made others also to be raised above them.

## 58.

Rise up, come, my neighbor, fear not the nets, already the winter is past. The passover is come, indulgence is come, the remission of sins is come, temptation has ceased, the rain is gone, the storm and the buffeting are gone. Before the coming of Christ it was winter; after the coming of Christ there are flowers, whence he says: *Flowers have been seen in the earth.* Where before there were thorns, there now are flowers. *The time,* he says, *of cutting is come.* Where before there was desert, there is harvest. *The voice of the turtledove has been heard in our land:* well did the prophet add *our*, as though marveling, that where before there was shamelessness, there is chastity. *The fig tree has put forth its green figs.* [1887D] She who as unfruitful was ordered to be cut down has now begun to bring forth fruit. But why dost thou doubt, because he said green figs? He shakes off the earlier ones, that he may bring forth later and better ones: as the fruit of the Synagogue is cast away, but that of the Church is renewed.



## 59.

And although there is full tranquillity, and the mysteries have grown to their strength, yet again he says: *Rise up secure in the covering of the rock,* that is, safe by the protection of my passion and by the defense of faith. For they sucked honey out of the rock and oil out of the firm rock. Clothed with this covering, the souls of the pious are no longer naked, and this is their [1888A] outwork. And therefore to this soul also he says: *And come thou, my dove, in the covering of the rock beside the outwork: show me thy face, and make thy voice known.* He exhorts her to confidence, that she be not ashamed of the cross of Christ, nor of its seal. He exhorts her to confession; he wills that all snares be removed, that the good fragrance of faith may breathe, that the day may shine, that the shadow of the hostile night may not harm; since he who is beside Christ says: *The night is passed, and the day has drawn near [n: (Rom. XXIII, 12)]*. There is also a shadow of worldly things which passes away, and the day of heavenly things is Christ, who shines for his saints.

## 60.

*[n: (Vers. 15.)] Take for us the little foxes that destroy the vines, that our vines may flower.* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:15 *capite nobis vulpes parvulas quae demoliuntur vineas: nam vinea nostra floruit*, "catch us the little foxes that destroy the vines: for our vineyard hath flourished"] Scripture calls him a man who is after the image and likeness [1888B] of God; but the sinner it is wont to call not a man, but either a serpent, or a horse neighing after females, or a little fox, or a beast of burden. Of one of these the Lord says: *Go, tell that fox [n: (Luc. XIII, 32)]*, that is, of Herod. And elsewhere, when he perceived that he was being questioned deceitfully, he said: *The foxes have dens, and the birds of heaven nests, where they may rest [n: (Matth. VIII, 20)]* [var: Vulg. Matt 8:20 *vulpes foveas habent, et volucres caeli nidos; Filius autem hominis non habet ubi caput reclinet*, "the foxes have holes, and the birds of the air nests; but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head"]; in that they linger deceitfully in certain lurking-places of earthly passions.

## 61.

Whence Samson too bound foxes to himself two by two, to whose tails he tied torches, and sent them out through the harvests of the *Allophyli* [n: (Judic. XV)]: signifying by this figure that wicked and deceitful men, and most of all heretics, have a tongue free for barking, but that their outcomes are hindered, or their beginnings quarrelsome, [1888C] while the end of their fraud is assigned to burning. And therefore he sent out three hundred foxes also, in that the faithless do indeed desire to commend themselves by the preaching of the cross, but cannot hold its mystery: they who, by this *1571* preaching made up, and false and feigned, attempt rather to burn up the fruits of others, whereas assuredly the true cross of the Lord does not burn up the merits of others, but makes them fruitful.

## 62.

And deservedly is it written: *Take for us the little foxes that destroy the vines, that our vines may flower.* By which it is shown that either the Lord Jesus or the Church commands that the wiles of the deceitful be driven out from his vines, lest they harm the tiny vines, since they cannot harm vines already grown up. For a heretic can tempt the imperfect; he cannot supplant [1888D] the perfect.

## 63.

*[n: (Vers. 16.)] My brother is mine, and I am his.* [var: Vulg. Cant 2:16 *dilectus meus mihi, et ego illi*, "my beloved to me, and I to him"] This is the voice of a virgin; for to keep the body unspotted from mingling with a man, and, in a body of clay, to bear up the palm of chastity by the guardianship of integrity even to the converse of angels, is a private benefit and a public praise. How rare on earth is he who can say this, how estranged from vices, how set apart from every stain of sin, who has nothing in common with the world, saying: *My beloved is mine and I am his, he shall abide in the midst of my breasts.* [var: Vulg. Cant 1:12 *inter ubera mea commorabitur*, "he shall abide between my breasts" — the second member of the printed lemma follows this verse rather than 2:16] That is to say, let him claim nothing [1889A] of this world for himself, one who has no possession of bodily desires, whom lust does not inflame, avarice does not goad, wantonness does not unman, excess does not discolor, ambition does not lay low, envy does not waste, no care of secular business makes anxious: a true minister of the altar, born for God, not for himself; for Levi, as the interpretation has it, signifies: He himself taken up for me; it signifies: And he himself is mine; it signifies also merely: Taken up; it signifies: And taken up for me.

## 64.

The very same man is Levi both for me and for God. How the same is a priest both for me and for God. And he is both my advocate and the Lord's intercessor: offering sacrifice for me, and the same man offering himself to the Lord. In fine, elsewhere too by the interpretation of Levi it is said, *For me;* for indeed if Levi be named by me, Levi is for me: if he offer for me, [1889B] he is for me: if he intercede for me, he is for me: but if he be called by the Lord, he is said to be mine: that is, tributary to no other, tithe-paying to no other, bountiful to no other out of a possession. He himself is mine, that is, as one abounding in place of all his own goods. From him I seek no tithes, no fruits, no gifts, no offerings; he himself is to me in place of an offering, he himself in place of tribute. Not bountiful to me in his own possession, but he himself is my possession, he himself my fruit, he himself my understanding. Taken up by me, or taken up for me: this cannot be without divine grace. For as a possession cannot be mine unless I have bought it, so he cannot be Levi unless he has been taken up by the Lord; for when he has been taken up, rightly is it said, he himself is mine. In fine, when it had been said to Moses that he should divide the dwelling-places among the people of the Jews tribe by tribe, [1889C] and should distribute a portion to each several tribe, God excepted the Levitical tribe, saying to the sons of Levi: *He shall have no portion, nor lot in the midst of his brethren; because the Lord God is their part [n: (Deut. X, 9)]* [var: Vulg. Deut 10:9 *non habuit Levi partem neque possessionem cum fratribus suis, quia ipse Dominus possessio ejus est*]: and elsewhere: *I the Lord am their portion [n: (Num. XVIII, 20)]* [var: Vulg. Num 18:20 *ego pars et hereditas tua in medio filiorum Israel*, "I am thy part and inheritance in the midst of the children of Israel"].

## 65.

An earthly division is denied to these, that they themselves, while they claim no secular portion for themselves, may become the possession of the heavenly one; or that they may know how to possess this only, that is, the obedience of faith and of devotion, being far richer than those who spread out the ample expanses of their possessions. For however far they extend their boundaries, both the land fails and the sea shuts in their mad desires, and they pay tributes greater than their fruits are. But this man, possessing nothing, serves as a soldier to none but God; his portion is above the earth, [1889D] it does not fail with the earth, it is not shut in by the sea. He whose portion is God is possessor of the whole of nature. In place of fields, therefore, he is enough for himself, having a good fruit which can never perish. In place of houses it is enough for him to be the dwelling of the Lord and the temple of God, than which nothing can be more precious. For what is more precious than God? Or what is lacking to this man who can say: *Let it not befall me to glory save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, through whom the world is crucified to me, and I to the world [n: (Galat. VI, 4)]* [var: Vulg. Gal 6:14 *mihi autem absit gloriari nisi in cruce*, "but God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross"] ?

## 66.

The prince of this world cannot claim [1890A] fellowship for himself in this man, in whom he finds nothing of his own. And therefore the Lord, who came to teach that God should become our portion, says: *For the prince of this world comes, and in me he finds nothing [n: (Joan. XIV, 30)]* [var: Vulg. John 14:30 *et in me non habet quidquam*, "and in me he hath not any thing"]. And deservedly, willing them to be imitators of himself, he says: *Do not possess gold, nor silver, nor money [n: (Matth. X, 9)]*. Whence Peter, showing that his portion was in God, not in the world, says: *Silver and gold I have not, but what I have,* *1572* *this I give thee: in the name of Jesus Christ of Nazareth rise up and walk [n: (Act. III, 6)]* [var: Vulg. Acts 3:6 *argentum et aurum non est mihi*, "silver and gold I have none"]; that is, this is my portion, my portion is Christ: *In the name therefore of Christ Jesus rise up and walk (Ibid)*. That is, in my portion I am rich, in my portion I am powerful. Deservedly do I look for such fruits of that portion, that health and life may be given; because this is the patrimony of that portion which I have chosen for myself: *My beloved is mine, [1890B] and I am his.*

## 67.

*Who is pastured among the lilies.* The lily signifies chastity. The good shepherd therefore knows where he may pasture his flock, what fodder is fit for his sheep. What are our pastures, that is, the pastures of the faithful, save Christ? In whose pastures the Prophet rejoiced that he was set, saying: *In a place of pasture, there he has set me [n: (Psal. XXII, 2)]*; for there he pastures and refreshes us. Good pastures are the divine sacraments. There thou pluckest the new flower, which has given the good fragrance of the resurrection: thou pluckest the lily, in which is the splendor of eternity: thou pluckest the rose, that is, the blood of the Lord's body. Good pastures too are the books of the heavenly Scriptures, in which we are fed by daily reading, in which we are restored and refreshed; when we taste the things that are written, [1890C] or more often chew over what we have sipped with the edge of the lips. By these pastures the flock of the Lord is fattened. Good pastures of Christ too are the mountains of the valleys; for in these also Christ pastures like a roe or a young hart, that is, in the splendors of the saints.



## 68.

*[n: (Vers. 17.)] Until the day breathe, and the shadows decline.* Zacchaeus climbed up into a sycamore tree that he might see Christ: for us too Jesus spread out his hands, that he might overshadow the whole world. How are we not in the shadow, we who are protected by the veil of his cross? How are we not in the shadow, whom the crucified defends from the malice of the world and from the burning of the body? Or do we not know that the Word of God, coming into this world, did not come as the Word. Such as it was in the beginning, such as it was with God: *but he emptied [1890D] himself, taking the form of a servant [n: (Philip. II, 7)]*. He came in a light cloud, and since he was the power of the Most High, he overshadowed Mary; that he might transfigure the body of our lowliness, to be made conformable to the body of his glory. As he therefore changed his form when he was born of the Virgin, so to us also the words of God seem to be transfigured when they are read in the Gospel, when their appearance is seen in the Scriptures as through a mirror; because the whole truth cannot be grasped here, but when that which is perfect shall have come, they shine forth no longer through examination, not through appearance as transfigured, but in whole and expressed truth. [1891A]

## 69.

Let him therefore bend the soul with the yoke of the Word, and rule it with his reins, that by the will of God it may be led away from vices and take in the fragrance of eternal life; for this life is not the perfect life, but this life is in shadow. Therefore, that Christ Jesus might be born a man of the Virgin, the power of the Most High overshadowed her who was to be his mother; because he came down in shadow, beginning from shadow to work the salvation of man, and about to complete it in the brightness of the eternal sun. This life, then, is a shadow. Hasten to the sun, that he may defend thee from the cold of this shadow, and pour over thee the warmth of summer. And therefore he urges that prayer must be made that our flight be not in winter or on the sabbath, not signifying a season and a day, but forbidding us to be cold in merits and to be fasting from good works.

## CAPUT TERTIUM.



## 1.

[1891B] *[n: (Vers. 1.)] In my bed, in the night I sought him whom my soul loved: I sought him and I found him not. I will rise up therefore, and will seek in the city, in the forum, in the streets, him whom my soul loved.* [var: Vulg. Cant 3:1-2 *in lectulo meo per noctes quaesivi quem diligit anima mea… surgam, et circuibo civitatem: per vicos et plateas quaeram*, "in my bed by night I sought him whom my soul loveth… I will rise, and will go about the city: in the streets and the broad ways I will seek," with no forum] This soul had already received the good pledges of charity; but because we ought always to be anxious, always intent, and because the Word of God leaps forth like a little roe, or like a young hart, let the soul be always watchful, and hold out before her him whom she seeks and whom she longs to hold. Therefore, as though lamenting that he had slipped away from her, *In my bed,* she says, *by nights I sought him whom my soul loved.* Let him who seeks well seek in the bed, seek by night: let there be neither a day of rest nor nights of it; let no time be empty of the duty of piety; and even if he has not found at the first, let him persevere in seeking. And therefore she says:

[1891C]

## 2.

*[n: (Vers. 2.)] I will rise up* therefore, *and will seek in the city, in the forum, in the streets.* And perhaps she has not yet found him for this reason, that she sought in the forum, where lawsuits are; in the streets, where the markets of things *1573* for sale are; for Christ is not purchased with money. Let holy devotion suffer no time of preaching to pass by empty. If those who study secular teachings indulge very little in sleep, how much more ought those who long to know God not to be hindered by the sleep of the body, save so far as is enough for nature? David washed his bed with tears through the several [sic: *nocctes*], and rose also in the middle of the night that he might confess to the Lord: and dost thou think that the whole night is to be assigned to slumber? Then the more must the Lord be prayed to by thee, then must protection be sought and fault be guarded against, when it seems to have secrecy. [1891D] Then most of all, when darkness is round about me and walls cover me, must it be considered that God beholds all hidden things. Say not therefore, wrapped about with darkness: Who sees me? And whom do I fear, hedged in and shut up by walls? Because *The face of the Lord is upon them that do evil [n: (Psalm. XXXIII, 17)]*. In fine, if thou seest no witness, dost thou not see thyself? Dost thou not fear the testimony of thine own conscience? Dost thou not know that that gloom of night is not a covering but an incitement of sin? When bodies grow hot with sleep and food, then too the vigor of the mind is loosened by slumber, is dissolved by sleep. Then [1892A] the lust of impure embrace creeps in, then the heart is troubled with the squalor of uncleanness, chastity is not seen, purity is not considered, the glory of modesty is not reckoned.

## 3.

It was night when Judas betrayed, when Peter denied. At that time therefore especially must the justifications of God be woven over again in the mind, and the exhorting commandments be read again: let those precepts concerning chastity not be absent, so that the mind, occupied with these, may quench the blaze of lust, the burning of the flesh. Hold that in mind: *I will wash my bed every night [n: (Psal. VI, 7)]*. For who, given over to debauchery, wrapped up in disgraces, washes his bed every night? He knows not how to weep who commits what must be wept for; and though he himself is a thing to be wept over, he has no tears for his own punishment. But he who chastises his body, and is an anxious governor of himself, and, groaning and [1892B] grieving over the offense of a former fall, seeks how he may wash it away with the weeping of penitence, this man washes his bed every night. Let us not therefore sleep whole nights through, but let us assign the greatest part of them to reading and to prayers. Hear the voice of the Church seeking Christ even by night: *In my bed,* she says, *by nights I sought him whom my soul loved.*

## 4.

Take it according to the letter, that she sought by night by praying, by beseeching, and also by lamenting with tears. She sought by night; because *He made darkness his hiding-place [n: (Psal. XVII, 12)]*, that we might learn to seek him more anxiously. The Church gathered out of the nations therefore sought him in the prophets, and therefore believed. In fine, the Evangelists and Paul set down testimonies of the Lord's coming out of the prophets, whence it is written [1892C]: *For night unto night showeth knowledge [n: (Psal. XVIII, 3)]*. She sought by night, in persecutions and adversities, in tribulations and hard sorrows. It is night for all for whom there is no perfect security; whence the Lord says: *The night will come when no man can work. While I am in this world, I am the light of this world [n: (Joan. IX, 4)]* [var: Vulg. John 9:4-5 *venit nox, quando nemo potest operari. Quamdiu in mundo sum, lux sum mundi*, "the night cometh, when no man can work. As long as I am in the world, I am the light of the world"]. Let us not therefore work in darkness; for if our works shine, we do not work in darkness, but in light.

## 5.

It is day for those to whom Christ is present, it is night for those to whom he denies himself. It is no great thing for thee if thou give thanks to the Lord then, when thou art in prosperity and in favorable things: but if thou cleave to Christ then, when a persecutor harries thee, when some storm troubles thee. Hast thou lost a son? In that grief, in that night, in that destitution, remember the Lord thy God; lest, as one not heard, [1892D] thou be ungrateful and transgress in thine affliction. Hast thou been driven into exile? Remember the Lord thy God, lest thou prefer the love of a fatherland forbidden thee before God. Crushed by the power of some rich man, hast thou lost thine own means, dost thou lack the wherewithal to live? Remember the Lord thy God, lest the night of want lead thee away from the affection of devotion. For this is the commandment of the Law, that thou seek the more by night, when thou art the more heard by the Lord, and mayest be able to say: *In tribulation I called upon the Lord, and the Lord heard me in a broad place [n: (Psal. CXVII, 5)]*.

## 6.

But neither does it suffice for grace to seek perfunctorily, [1893A] but that thou press on and bend thyself to the task. In fine, this Church or soul, who sought in the bed, sought by night, did not find at the first; because perhaps she sought in the bed. *1574* But after she rose and went away into the city (see whether it be into that city in which the Lord, about to celebrate the passover, sent his disciples, saying [n: (Matth. XXVI, 18)]: *Go into the city to a certain man* ), after she sought, as I have said, in the forum, where oil is sold, which those awaiting the Bridegroom are wont to buy; where right is, where the laws are. For if the law is spiritual, the forum too is assuredly spiritual, where those skilled in the eternal law dispute. Which forum is not tumultuous with lawsuits, but glorious with the tribunals of Christ. Where did she seek, I say? In the forum, and afterward she sought in the streets, out of which were gathered those who were to come together to the banquet of the householder of [1893B] the Gospel: who, being called, did not think that they should excuse themselves from such a feast. After she sought, I say, in the forum and in the streets, she fell in with those who go about the city, and then at last she was able to find what she was seeking; perhaps because, set in tribulation and fear, she the more finds grace. In fine, she says afterward:



## 7.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] They found me who go about the city; they took away my cloak.* [var: Vulg. Cant. III, 3 *Invenerunt me vigiles qui custodiunt civitatem*; the taking of the cloak stands at Cant. V, 7] She had a struggle, then: but by what reasoning they did not take away her cloak here, I am at a loss to find out; unless perhaps because there she said that she had asked: *Have you seen him whom my soul has loved?* She who was speaking Christ, [sic: *nex*] was not stripped of her cloak, and she found him whom she was seeking. Learn in what manner Christ is sought: by those, surely, who do not seek him perfunctorily, but hold him fast [1893C] by force, as she does who says: *I held him and I will not let him go,* who found him with faith and bound him fast with meditation.

## 8.

If we take them for good guards, they are certainly angels. But he who passed beyond the angels. Found the Word; and therefore there was not much of an interval, when he passed beyond these and found Jesus. How then did they take away her cloak below, unless perhaps because, by the advance of an offense against faith, the Church, while she is stripped bare, is the more loved by the Lord, putting off from herself the old man that she may put on the new, whom sins do not cover but the hidden things of the mind make bright; or because, being about to come to Christ, she laid aside the mantle of worldly wisdom? And he was not stripped of his cloak, after he had begun to be more perfect.

## 9.

Let us therefore be mindful of the heavenly ordinances of justification; that while we sing them with the hidden voice of the mind, we may be mindful [1893D] in the night of the name of the Lord, and may say as it is written: *This was made mine, because I sought out thy justifications [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 56)]*; that is, this remembrance was made mine, that I might be mindful even in the night of thy name, not lulled asleep by drunkenness, not loosed into slumber by feasting, not taken up with worldly cares, lest forgetfulness of the reverence owed to thee should creep upon me: but chastening my members by daily meditation, and exercising the intention of the mind; so that by constancy this course may become a solemn one for us, so that we may reverence the Lord Jesus with our whole affection even in the nights.

## 10.

*In my chamber, in the nights I sought him whom [1894A] my soul has loved.* [var: Vulg. *In lectulo meo per noctes quaesivi quem diligit anima mea*] We can understand it in this way as well. He seeks Christ in his bed who seeks him with tranquillity, with peace. He seeks in the nights, since he was speaking in parables; for *He hath set* indeed *darkness for his covert, and night sheweth knowledge unto night [n: (Psal. XVII, 12)]*. Then, because the things which we say in our heart ought to prick us in our beds.

## 11.

But even so she did not find him, and therefore she says: *I will rise up,* that is, I will raise and lift up my intention, that I may seek untiringly, may seek anxiously: I will go into the city. There is also a soul which says: *I am a fortified city, I am a besieged city [n: (Esai. XXVII, 3)]*. [var: Vulg. Isa. XXVII, 3 *Ego Dominus qui servo eam*] There is a city fortified through Christ; there is that city Jerusalem in heaven, in which the interpreters of the divine law abound, and men skilled in the disciplines, through whom the Word of God is sought. [1894B] I will seek, she says, in the forum of that city, in that forum where those learned in the law handle rights, where the oil is sold which the Gospel virgins buy, that their torches may always shine, and that the smoke of iniquity may not put them out. I will seek, she says, in the streets in which the waters overflow, bursting out from those fountains of which Solomon says that one must drink.

## 12.

*They found me,* she says, *the guards who go about the city.* While therefore she seeks Christ, she finds the guards who are in ministry; from these she asks: *Have you seen him whom my soul loves?* But the soul which seeks God passes even the guards by. For there are mysteries which even the angels long to see, whence Peter says: *They were announced,* he says, *to you by those to whom they preached the gospel, the Holy* *1575* *Spirit having been sent from heaven, [1894C] upon whom the angels long to look [n: (I Pet. I, 12)]*. [var: Vulg. *per eos qui evangelizaverunt vobis* … *in quem desiderant angeli prospicere*]

## 13.

*[n: (Vers. 4.)] How little a while after I had passed beyond them, I found him, I held him, and I did not let him go.* [var: Vulg. Cant. III, 4 *Paululum cum pertransissem eos, inveni quem diligit anima mea*] Therefore he who has passed beyond the guards has found the Word. John passed beyond them, who found the Word with the Father. There are also many others who seek Christ in leisure and do not find him; and they seek him in persecutions, and they find him. And therefore, as if after temptations — because he is present in the perils of his faithful ones — *How little a while,* he says, *after I had passed beyond them, I found him, and I held him, and I did not let him go.* For everyone who seeks, finds; and he who has found ought to cleave to him, lest he be able to lose him.

## 14.

And since through the Gospel we see the heavenly mysteries figured upon earth, let us come to Magdalene, [1894D] let us come to the other Mary.
