Let us consider in what manner they sought Christ in the nights, in the chamber of his body in which he lay dead, when the angel said to them: *You seek Jesus who was crucified: he is not here, for he is risen: why then do you seek the living with the dead [n: (Matth. XXVIII, 5 et seq.)]*? [var: Vulg. Matth. XXVIII, 6 ends *surrexit enim, sicut dixit*; *Quid quaeritis viventem cum mortuis* stands at Luc. XXIV, 5] Why do you seek in the sepulchre him who is already in heaven? Why do you seek in the bonds of burial him who looses the bonds of all men? Not the sepulchre but heaven is his seat; therefore one of them said: *I sought him, and I did not find him [n: (Cant. III, 1)]*. Yet while they go to bring word to the apostles, Jesus, having pity on those who sought him, met them, saying: [1895A] *Hail.* But they came near, and held his feet, and adored him.

## 15.

Jesus therefore is held, and is delighted to be held so, because he is held by faith. In short, that woman also delighted him who touched him and was healed of the flow of blood, of whom he said: *Someone has touched me; for I feel that power has gone out from me [n: (Luc. VIII, 46)]*. [var: Vulg. *nam ego novi virtutem de me exiisse*, "for I know"] Touch him therefore, and hold him by faith, and clasp his feet faithfully; that power may go out from him to heal thy soul. And if he say: *Do not touch me;* do thou hold him. *For I have not yet ascended to my Father,* he said [n: (Joan. XX, 17)]. Once he said: *Do not touch me,* when he had risen: or perhaps he said it to her who thought that he had been carried off by theft and not raised up again by his own power. In short, in another book thou hast it that to those holding his feet and adoring him he said: *Do not [1895B] be afraid [n: (Matth. XXVIII, 10)]*.

## 16.

Hold him therefore, thou also, O soul, as Mary too was holding him, and say: *I held him, and I will not let him go:* to whom both of them were saying, we hold thee. Go to the Father, but do not forsake me, lest I slip again; and with Mary, who does not stray but holds the tree of life, snatch up with thee her who clings to thy feet. I will ascend with thee, do not let me go, lest again the serpent pour out his venom, lest again he be able to bite the woman's heel, that he may supplant Adam. Let thy soul therefore say: *I hold thee, and I will bring thee into the house of my mother, and into the secret place of her who conceived me [n: (Cant. III, 4)]*; that I may know thy mysteries, that I may drink in thy sacraments.

## 17.

*I held him, nor will I leave him, until I bring him into the house of my mother, and into the chamber of her [1895C] who conceived me.* [var: Vulg. Cant. III, 4 *in cubiculum genitricis meae*, "into the chamber of her that bore me"] What is the house of my mother and her chamber, but the inward and hidden part of thy nature? Guard this house, cleanse the inner rooms of this house, so that when the house has been unstained, and defiled by the filth of no adulterous conscience, a spiritual house may rise up, framed together upon the corner stone into a holy priesthood, and the Holy Spirit may dwell in it. She who seeks Christ thus, who thus entreats Christ, is not left by him: nay rather, she is even visited again and again.

## 18.

Or the house of the mother is perhaps the house in which the discipline of morals shines out: while that secret place is the one in which are the higher mysteries, in which the honey of divine grace gives out its fragrance. Or is not the remission of sins sweeter than all honey? Is not the resurrection of the dead [sic: *floregratior*] than every one? Receive then Eve, no [1895D] longer covered with the leaves of the fig tree, but clothed with the Holy Spirit, and glorious with new grace; because now she is not hidden away as one stripped bare, but comes to meet us as one surrounded with the splendour of a shining garment, because grace clothes her: yet neither was Adam naked at first, when innocence clothed him. And so the daughters of Jerusalem, seeing her clinging to Christ and still ascending with him (for he deigns often to meet those who seek him, and to come down, that he may lift them up), say:



## 19.

*[n: (Vers. 6.)] Who is this that ascends from the desert?* [1896A] Be thou like that soul which stirs up in itself the charity of Christ, at whose ascent the powers of the heavens marvel; that she ascends without stumbling *1576* and ascends out of this world with gladness and delight, like the shoot of a vine, and lifts herself like smoke to the things above, fragrant with the odour of a holy resurrection and with the sweetness of faith, as thou hast it written: *Who is this that ascends from the desert, like the shoot of a vine, made odorous with the smoke of incense, with myrrh and frankincense, out of all the powders of the ointment.* [var: Vulg. *sicut virgula fumi ex aromatibus myrrhae et thuris et universi pulveris pigmentarii*] Beautifully has he expressed her fineness by the comparison of powder and by the mention of ointment; because in Exodus we read of a fine incense compounded of many things, that this is the prophetic incense which is the prayer of the saints, that it may be directed in the sight of the Lord, as David also says: *Let my prayer be directed as incense in [1896B] thy sight [n: (Psalm. CXL, 2)]*.

## 20.

*Who is she that ascends from the desert?* This desert appears as a place of earth and untilled, overgrown with the briars and thorns of our offenses. They marvel, plainly, how the soul which before was being left in hell should cleave to the Word of God, and should have ascended like the shoot of a vine, raising herself into the higher regions, like smoke born of fire and seeking the heights, and should moreover be fragrant with good odours. And that odour of holy prayer breathes out a sweetness which is directed *as incense in the sight of the Lord [n: (Psal. CXL, 2)]*. And in the Apocalypse we read that *The smoke of the incense went up from the prayers of the saints [n: (Apoc. VIII, 4)]*. which are offered as incense through the prayers of his angels, namely upon that golden altar which is before the seat of God. And it is fragrant like the sweet [1896C] ointment of holy entreaty; because it is compounded out of the asking for eternal and invisible things, not for bodily ones: yet chiefly it smells of myrrh and frankincense, in that she is dead to sin and lives to God. Whence also in what follows: *I will go,* he says, *for myself to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense.* There follows:

## 21.

*[n: (Vers. 7.)] Behold, sixty mighty men round about her shoot, armed with drawn swords, and trained in the disciplines of war.* [var: Vulg. Cant. III, 7–8 *En lectulum Salomonis sexaginta fortes ambiunt ex fortissimis Israel*, "sixty valiant men surround Solomon's bed"] Consider who these strong men are, and trained in the disciplines of war. It belongs to a strong and churchly man not to dissemble when something threatens, but to look out ahead and, as it were, to scout from some watchtower of the mind, and to meet with provident thought the things that are to come; lest afterwards he say: I fell into this because I did not think it could happen. In short, unless adverse things are scouted out, [1896D] they quickly overtake us. As in war an unforeseen enemy is scarcely withstood, and if he finds men unprepared he easily overwhelms them: so evils unexplored break the mind the more. In these two things, then, lies that excellence of mind: first, that thy mind, exercised in good thoughts, may see with a clean heart what is true and honourable: for *Blessed are the clean of heart, because they also shall see God:* and that it may judge that alone to be good which is honourable. Then, that it be disturbed by no occupations, that it waver with no covetous desires. [1897A]

## 22.

Nor indeed does anyone do this easily: for what is so difficult as to look down, as from some citadel of wisdom, upon riches and all the other things which seem to most men great and lofty? Then, that thou shouldst confirm thy judgment by settled reason, and despise the things thou hast judged trifling as things that will profit nothing? Then, that if anything adverse should befall, and be thought grievous and bitter, thou shouldst bear it in such a way as to think that nothing has happened contrary to nature, since thou hast read: Naked was I born, naked shall I go out: the things which the Lord gave, the Lord has also taken away [n: (Job. I, 21)]; [var: Vulg. Job I, 21 *Nudus egressus sum de utero matris meae, et nudus revertar illuc*] and he had indeed lost both his sons and his goods: and that in all things thou shouldst keep the character of a wise and just man, as he kept it who said: *As it pleased the Lord, so it has been done, blessed be the name of the Lord [n: (Ibid.)]*. And below, *Thou hast spoken as one of the foolish women: if we have received good things [1897B] from the hand of the Lord, shall we not bear the things that are evil [n: (Ibid., 2)]* ? [var: Vulg. Job II, 10 *si bona suscepimus de manu Dei, mala quare non suscipiamus?*]

## 23.

The fortitude of the mind, therefore, is no middling thing, nor one set apart from the rest, that it should wage war with the virtues; but it is that which alone defends the ornaments of all the virtues, and guards their judgments, and which contends in inexpiable battle against all vices, unconquered in labours, brave in dangers, more rigid against pleasures, hard against enticements, to which it knows not how to lend an ear, nor (as the saying is) to bid them hail; which neglects money, flees avarice as a kind of stain that unmans virtue; for nothing is so contrary to fortitude as to be overcome by gain. Often, when the enemy has been driven back and the line of the adversaries turned to flight, while the fighter is taken up with the spoils of the slain, he has fallen, a man to be pitied, among the very men [1897C] whom he laid low: and legions cast down by their own triumphs, while they are busied with the *1577* spoils, have called back upon themselves the enemy who had fled.

## 24.

Let fortitude therefore drive back and trample down so monstrous a pest, and let it not be tempted by covetous desires, nor broken by fear; because virtue is consistent with itself, so that it pursues all vices bravely, as the poisons of virtue. Let it drive off anger as with certain weapons, since anger takes away counsel, and let it shun it as a sickness. Let it beware also of the appetite for glory, which has often done harm when sought too immoderately, but always when usurped. For to be softened by flattery seems not only not to belong to fortitude, but even to belong to cowardice. Seeing in the spirit the camp of these strong men, Jacob said: *These are the camps of God [n: (Gen. XXXII, 2)]*. [1897D]

## 25.

But what are the sixty? We know that the hebdomad belongs to the Old Testament, and the ogdoad to the New — namely when Christ rose again, and the day of the new salvation shone upon all: on which day the brightness of the full and perfect circumcision poured itself into human breasts. Now seven and eight make fifteen. These, then, are the seven and the eight, that is, the fifteen, for which Hosea hired the harlot whom the Lord had commanded him to hire. And so the hebdomad and the eighth signify the consummation of the two Testaments [1898A] and the fullness of faith: while the number fifteen taken four times, which is sixty, signifies the perfection of the Gospel.

## 26.

But what is the shoot round about which these mighty men are? *Nephthalim,* he says, *a vine let loose, putting forth beauty in its bud.* [var: Vulg. Gen. XLIX, 21 *Nephthali, cervus emissus*, "a hind let loose"] One branch of the vine is cut away which seems useless, lest the vine run to waste in an empty exultation of branches, and another is pruned back a little and let loose, that it may bear fruit, whose beauty is put forth in its bearing; because while it lifts itself to the things above it embraces the vine, and climbing to the top it clothes the necks of the vines' yoke-poles with a certain necklace of precious shoot. That one too is comely in its bearing, because with full shoots it pours out many fruits. This is comely, but that other is far more beautiful, which signifies the shoot [1898B] clinging to the spiritual vine, of which we are the branch, and we can bear fruit if we remain in the vine; but if not, we are cut off. The holy patriarch Nephthalim was an abounding shoot, whence Moses also says: *Nephthalim, the satisfying of those who receive, shall be filled with blessing from the Lord: he shall possess the sea and the south-west wind [n: (Deut. XXXIII, 23)]*; [var: Vulg. Deut. XXXIII, 23 *mare et meridiem possidebit*, "shall possess the sea and the south"] explaining what Jacob had said, what *a vine let loose* is, that is, one stripped of the bonds of death through the grace of faith: in which is signified the people of God, called to the liberty of faith and the richness of grace, spread through the whole world, which clothes the yoke of Christ with good fruit, and encircles the wood of that true vine, that is, the mysteries of the Lord's cross, and does not fear the peril of confessing him: but rather, even when set in persecutions, glories in the name of Christ. [1898C]

## 27.

Now these strong men hold swords in their hands, which they do not refuse for the name of Christ, or by which they distinguish bodily things from spiritual, the shadow from the truth. *For the word of God is living and effectual, and more piercing than any two-edged sword, and reaching unto the division of the soul and the spirit, of the joints also and of the marrow, and a discerner of the thoughts and intentions of the heart: and there is no creature invisible in his sight, but all things are naked and open to his eyes [n: (Hebr. IV, 12 et 13)]*. For the word of God is a sword twice sharpened. There are also two other swords in the Church, of which it is said: *Lord, behold, here are two swords,* namely of the churchly power and of the worldly, against which a false mercy fights.



## 28.

There is a just mercy: but there is also an unjust [1898D] mercy. In short, it is written in the Law concerning a certain man: *Thou shalt not have mercy upon him [n: (Deut. XIX, 13)]*. And in the books of Kingdoms thou readest that Saul incurred offense for this reason, that he had mercy on Agag the king of the enemy, whom the divine sentence had forbidden to be kept alive [n: (I Reg. XV, 9)]. So that if anyone, moved by the pleading of a robber's children and bent by the tears of his wife, should think that he ought to be acquitted, while the desire of robbing still pants within him: will he not hand over the innocent to destruction, who sets free a man who is plotting the destruction of many? Assuredly, if he holds back the sword, looses the chains, [1899A] relaxes the exile; [sic: *car*] why does he not take away, by the gentler road that lies open to him, the power of robbing, he who could not wrest away the will? Then, between two men, that is, an accuser and a defendant contending at equal peril of life — the one if he did not prove his case, the other if he were not convicted by the accuser — let not the judge follow that *1578* which belongs to justice; but while he has mercy on the defendant, let him condemn the man who proves the charge: or while he favours the accuser who cannot prove it, let him cast off the guiltless. This therefore cannot be called a just mercy.

## 29.

In the Church itself, where it is most fitting to show mercy, the form of justice ought to be kept as strictly as possible, lest anyone who has been kept back from the fellowship of communion should, by a brief little tear made ready for the moment, or even by more abundant weeping, wring out through the easiness of the priest [1899B] the communion which he ought to ask for over a very long time. Does he not, when he indulges one unworthy man, cause very many to be drawn on to the contagion of falling? For easiness of pardon supplies an incentive to offending. This has been said for this reason, that we may know that mercy is to be dispensed to debtors according to the word of God, according to reason: but otherwise let the sword carry out what belongs to it.

## 30.

*[n: (Vers. 9, 10.)] King Solomon made himself a bed out of the woods of Lebanon; he made its pillars of silver, its headrest of gold, its ascent of purple, its back strewn with jewels.* [var: Vulg. Cant. III, 9–10 *Ferculum fecit sibi rex Salomon*, "a litter"; and Vulg. has nothing answering to *dorsum ejus gemmis stratum*] What is this bed, but the appearance of our body? For in jewels the air is displayed under the appearance of brightness, in gold fire, water in silver, earth by wood; of which four elements the human body consists, in which our soul reclines, [1899C] if it be not without rest upon the roughness of the mountains or upon the dry ground; but, raised on high above the vices, may rest propped upon the wood. Whence David also says: *May the Lord bring help to him upon the bed of his sorrow [n: (Psal. XL, 4)]*; for how can there be a bed of sorrow, when that which has no feeling cannot feel sorrow? Now a body of sorrow is like a body of death: *Unhappy man that I am! who will free me from the body of that death [n: (Rom. VII, 24)]*? [var: Vulg. Rom. VII, 24 *de corpore mortis hujus*, "of this death"]

## 31.

And since we have brought in a verse in which we have made mention of the Lord's body, lest perhaps it disturb any reader that the Lord took upon himself a body of sorrow, let him remember that he both sorrowed over the death of Lazarus and wept, and in the passion was wounded, and from the wound blood and water came out, and he breathed out his spirit [n: (Joan. XI, 35)]. Water for the washing, blood for [1899D] drink, spirit for the resurrection. For Christ is to us one hope, faith, charity: hope in the resurrection, faith in the washing, charity in the sacrament. Yet as he took upon himself a body of sorrow, so also he turned the couch in infirmity; because he turned it to the advantage of human flesh: for by the passion infirmity was loosed, death by the resurrection. And nevertheless you ought to mourn for the world and to rejoice in the Lord: sorrowful unto penitence, eager unto grace — although the Doctor of the Gentiles has laid it down by a saving precept that we must both weep with them that weep and rejoice with them that rejoice [n: (Rom. XII, 15)]. [1900A]

## 32.

*He strewed the midst with charity, for the daughters of Jerusalem.* Who are these daughters? It ought not to be hidden that those seem superior who are [sic: *generali*] beforehand, and weaker those who come afterwards. For the birth-throes of this world are compared to the womb of a woman; since those are stronger who were born in the strength of youth, weaker those born in the time of old age. For this world has failed in the multitude of its begetting, like the womb of one giving birth, and like an aging creature it lays down the strength of its youth, as though the vigour of its powers were now withering. These, then, are the daughters of Jerusalem, that is, the more tender and weaker souls: to these he shows himself, that his image may shine in their confession, may shine in their love, may shine in their works; and, if it can be done, that his whole form may be expressed in them.

## 33.

[1900B]These daughters of Jerusalem, therefore, seeing the bride ascending and not resisting, and delighted by the odours of her merits, recognizing also that she is the bride of that peaceable Solomon, follow after her with sedulous escort as far as the bed of Solomon: because true rest in Christ is due to her. For the bed of the saints is Christ, in which the hearts of all men, wearied by the battles of the world, find rest. In this bed Isaac rested, and blessed his younger son, saying: *The greater shall serve the lesser:* reclining in this bed Jacob blessed the twelve patriarchs: reclining in this bed the daughter of the ruler of the synagogue rose from death: lying in this bed the widow's only son, who had died, loosed the bonds of death when called by the voice of Christ. The bride, therefore, having been brought as far as the rest of the bridegroom, they sing the marriage [1900C] song, saying to the daughters of Jerusalem:

## 34.

*[n: (Vers. 11.)] Go forth and see upon king Solomon the crown with which his mother crowned him, in the day of his espousals.* [var: Vulg. Cant. III, 11 *in diademate quo coronavit illum mater sua in die desponsationis illius*] They sing the epithalamium, and call the other powers of the heavens, or *1579* the souls, to see the charity which Christ has toward the daughters of Jerusalem. Whence he also deserved to be crowned by his mother as the son of charity, as Paul shows, saying: *Because God has rescued us from the power of darkness, and has carried us over into the kingdom of the son of his charity [n: (Coloss. I, 13)]*. [var: Vulg. Col. I, 13 *in regnum filii dilectionis suae*, "of the son of his love"] And so the son of charity is himself also charity: not having charity from accidents, but having it by his own substance, like the kingdom of which he says: *For this was I born [n: (Joan. XVIII, 17)]*.

## 35.

And therefore he says, *Go forth,* that is, go out from the [1900D] anxieties and thoughts of the world: go out from bodily straits: go out from the vanities of the world, and see what charity the peaceable king has in the day of his espousals: how glorious he is, because he gave resurrection to bodies, and joined souls to himself. This is the crown of a great contest, this the illustrious gift of Christ's espousals, his blood and his passion. For who could give more, he who did not even spare himself, and laid down his soul unto death for us?

## 36.

But what is the crown of glory, but the Church which [1901A] crowns Christ her head? What is the delight of the whole earth, but the house of the Christian people, the court of the saints, of whom it is written: *Their sound has gone out into all the earth [n: (Psal. XVIII, 5)]*? Therefore, because the Church is the crown of glory, it is said: *Go forth, daughters of Sion, and see king Solomon, in the crown with which his mother crowned him, in the day of his espousals, and in the day of the gladness of his heart.*

## 37.

It is said to souls that they should go out from the hovels and prisons of the body, that they should go out beyond the thoughts of the body, that they should depart from covetous desires, and from cares, and from the other passions of this flesh and of slippery affection: that they should climb above the world, go out from this world, meet Christ, be present and ready, shining with burning torches. As though the angels of Christ were speaking, they say these things: You [1901B] cannot see his brightness, his glory, unless you go out from the cares of human frailty, O daughters of Jerusalem. As if they said: *Why do you seek the living among the dead [n: (Luc. XXIV, 5)]*? [var: Vulg. Luc. XXIV, 5 *Quid quaeritis viventem cum mortuis?*] Christ is not sought within this world, who willed his disciples to be above the world.

## 38.

What is the crown with which Christ is crowned, but the crown of glory? Joseph had the crown of chastity, Paul of justice, Peter of faith. There are crowns of the several virtues. Christ alone has the crown of glory, with which the Church crowned him. In this crown are all the crowns; because glory is not the portion of one crown, but the reward of all the crowns.

## CHAPTER FOUR.



## 1.

[1901C] [n: (Vers. 1.)] But now the Bridegroom, turned to the praise of the bride, says: *How beautiful thou art, my love, how beautiful thou art! thine eyes are as doves, apart from silence.* [var: Vulg. Cant. IV, 1 *Oculi tui columbarum, absque eo quod intrinsecus latet*] Beautiful in preaching, beautiful in her manner of life. *Her eyes also,* he says, *are as doves.* They are the eyes of a man, plainly adorned with spiritual senses, which are keen for seeing the mysteries, and ready for penetrating the secret things of divine Scripture, shining with reasonable milk, in which there is no spotted confusion of guile, but the pure and unstained simplicity of a single-hearted affection. Therefore he afterwards mentioned these doves as washed in milk, in an abundance of waters.



## 2.

Now as for the rest — the teeth and the cheeks, and the lips of the bride like a scarlet cord — we understand them to be the virtues of the soul and the diversities of teachers: who either supply spiritual [1901D] nourishment to the mind by sedulous dispensation, or, by the preaching of the Lord's cross, bind the hearer as with a certain cord of the Word: or who are modest in their bashfulness and most pleasing in the flower of youth. And though they be called back from the touch by shame, yet let the odour of Christ be fragrant in them: and just as the ointment runs down upon the cheeks, as from the priestly head, so also let the beauty of doctrine shine out in them.

## 3.

*Thine eyes are as doves apart from thy silence;* which both sees spiritually, and knows that there is a time for keeping silence and a time for speaking; so that it may bring forth its word [1902A] in season, lest by unseasonableness of speaking it be able to incur sin.

## 4.

*Thine eyes are as doves.* Here the simplicity and grace of the dove, that is, of the Church, is revealed. See then the simplicity of the eye: for he who sees a just man and rejoices, himself also wishes to be just: for it is a beautiful thing that a man should be delighted in others by that *1580* which he wishes to keep in himself, if he can: for it is inborn in good men that the modest man should love the chaste, the wise man the prudent, with holy affection, the merciful man the generous, and that they should love their own virtues in others. For very many, indeed, the sight of a just man is an admonition to correction, but for the more perfect it is a joy. For if there is such power in natural things that a living creature, when seen, profits the jaundiced — so that even the body of that creature when dead is said to profit, if it be shown to those who have fallen into [1902B] this kind of ailment — can we doubt that the sight of a just man heals, whose eyes are as doves, simple and modest? Therefore a cheap and irrational animal has such power that it can heal a man in the tiny moment in which it is seen: will a just man, if only he be looked upon with faith by one who desires to receive benefit from him, confer nothing? How beautiful a thing it is, then, that thou shouldst be seen and do good!

## 5.

A just man, then, is a good thing. This, in short, is why the apostle Paul went up to Jerusalem, that he might see just men: and he stayed with Peter fifteen days, that he might gain something from living with him [n: (Gal. I, 18)]. For this reason Paul himself and Barnabas, when they were entering Jerusalem, were magnificently received by [1902C] the Church, and by the apostles, and by the elders: but when they wished to depart, they were begged not to go away, and, as we read afterwards concerning Paul, he was escorted on his way with tears. Do not the very rays of the eyes seem to pour a certain power into those who faithfully desire to see him?

## 6.

But just as a just man gladdens the heart of the innocent when he is seen, so the wicked are tormented by the knowledge of the just, because they are reproved even by the silent conduct of the saints. Chastity torments the incontinent, generosity avarice, faith impiety. Of this too let us take a like example from a cheap living creature. For as we have said that a dumb animal profits when it is seen, so we perceive that the wolf does harm, if he has got ahead of anyone in the seeing; for those whom the wolf [1902D] has seen first are reported to lose their voice. The basilisk also (this is a harmful serpent), if it should see any animal first, is reported to kill it: and it is alleged that a man is slain at once who has been able to be seen beforehand by a serpent of this kind. The serpent itself also is said to die, if it has been forestalled by the sight of a man. Therefore, if there is such power either in the eyes of a serpent or in the eyes of a man, that if the one sees the other first he can kill him: is there no power in the eyes of a just man, who is filled with the grace of virtue — especially since faith works so much that even she who touched the Lord's fringe was healed, and he upon whom the Lord Jesus looked drew at once [1903A] from his eyes the grace of health [n: (Matth. IX, 22)]?

## 7.

But he who sees a just man ought to know what he sees. He does not see him in the body, not in his clothing, not in his patrimony, not in his face: but he sees within. He does not, I say, see him, unless he has seen his mind, unless he has attended to his speech, unless he has been able to grasp his understanding, has been able to take up wisdom from his discourse. Then therefore will he rejoice, when he has looked upon these things, when he has come to know them. So then let us also, whenever we hear of a just man, hasten to see him, like that woman who heard that the Lord Jesus was reclining at table in the house of the Pharisee, and went in, and poured ointment over his feet [n: (Luc. VII, 37 et 38)]. Let us be imitators of her, for who would doubt that in that woman there is a figure of the Church? [1903B] Wherever, then, a just man may sit, wherever he may recline at table, let us hasten to see him. It is a precious thing to see a just man, that thou mayest see him according to the image of God. What is outside profits nothing: what is within heals. Certainly in him who is without we often behold him who is within: so that if we see a poor man, let us honour in the poor man him to whose likeness he was made, of whom he says: *You gave me to eat, because what you gave to one of these, you gave to me [n: (Matth. XXV, 35 et 40)]*.

## 8.

*Thy hair is as a flock of shorn ones, which have been revealed from mount Galaad.* [var: Vulg. Cant. IV, 1 *Capilli tui sicut greges caprarum quae ascenderunt de monte Galaad*] Therefore hair, because the power of all the senses is in the head: for *The eyes of the wise man are in his head [n: (Eccl. II)]*. Deep, then, is the prudence of teachers, which can reveal the things that are obscure, [1903C] and open up the heights of the senses.

## 9.

*[n: (Vers. 2.)] Thy teeth are as a flock of shorn ones, which have come up from the washing, all of which bring forth twins, and there is not a barren one among them.* [var: Vulg. Cant. IV, 2 *omnes gemellis fetibus, et sterilis non est inter eas*] What is said in the literal sense of she-goats is to be understood mystically of the flock of the Church. And let these animals not seem cheap to thee; thou seest that this flock feeds on the heights, bold upon the mountain. And so where for others there are precipices, there for the she-goats is no danger: where for others is danger, there is the food of that flock, there the sweeter *1581* fare, there the choicer fruit. They are watched by their shepherds as they hang from the bushy rock, where the inroads of wolves cannot reach; there fruitful trees supply whole fruit. One may see them swollen with abundant milk, anxious with a mother's love over their tender offspring. [1903D] Therefore the Holy Spirit chose them, to which he might compare the coming together of the venerable Church.

## 10.

And that thou mayest hear it mystically: the hair of the Word is the height, and a certain eminence, of just [sic: *animasum*]; since the understanding of the wise man is in his head. For it is certain that wisdom lies in the height of human thought. And just as she-goats are shorn, that they may lay aside what is superfluous: so also holy Church has a flock of shorn souls, that is, the virtues of many souls, in which flock thou couldst find nothing without feeling, nothing superfluous; since faith has made them wise, [1904A] and spiritual grace has cleansed them from every stain of superfluous things.

## 11.

Rightly, therefore, have the souls of the just been revealed, and revealed from mount Galaad, that is, from the passing over of testimony, in that the heavenly testimony passed over from the Synagogue to the Church. And so on this mountain grow the incense-gum, the resin, and the other perfumes which those Ishmaelite traders were carrying, as thou hast it in the first book of the Old Testament [n: (Gen. XXXVII, 25)]. And so, just as she-goats refreshed with good food and blooming in the warmth of the sun are washed in the river, and rise up out of the river exulting and clean: so the souls of the just come up from the spiritual washing.

## 12.

These are they who truly bring forth twins, in whom there is no barrenness of virtues, no sterility [1904B] of merits. Well do they bring forth twins, because they double their understandings; whence thou hast it written in Proverbs: *And do thou write these things for thyself in threefold fashion, unto counsel and unto knowledge [n: (Prov. XXII, 20)]*. [var: Vulg. Prov. XXII, 20 *Ecce descripsi eam tibi tripliciter, in cogitationibus et scientia*] He set a threefold writing before, and added two things, counsel and knowledge. But knowledge is twofold: one of incorporeal things, the other of corporeal; whence also in what follows thou hast: *Thy two breasts are as two twin fawns of a roe, which feed among the lilies, until the day break and the shadows retire [n: (Vers. 5)]*.

## 13.

We have spoken of fruitfulness; let us speak of the teeth. For very many who sail, and those hastening on a journey by land, when they have seen some beautiful place, linger there for the sake of the delight, they feed their eyes and lift up [1904C] their mind. Nor is any of this reckoned a delay in their travelling, but a favour. So it is our heart's desire also to consider the most beautiful teeth of just souls; for Scripture has taught that the teeth of the just are most beautiful, speaking indeed according to the letter of the patriarch Judah, but spiritually of Christ: *His eyes are merry from wine, and his teeth are as milk [n: (Genes. XLIX, 12)]*. [var: Vulg. Gen. XLIX, 12 *Pulchriores sunt oculi ejus vino, et dentes ejus lacte candidiores*] In which it proclaimed not the services of human flesh, but the gifts of divine grace. The example teaches us, therefore, that the teeth are not to be passed over, where we have discoursed of the eyes.



## 14.

What then are the teeth of just souls, but those which, receiving food shapeless and hard, or often cold, or hot beyond measure, now break it small, now warm it, now temper it, according as the quality of the nourishment has been? They break the hard things small, [1904D] lest the harshness of the letter in the Old Testament and the rigidity of a worldly understanding, if it be not dissolved by the spiritual tooth, should choke the vital parts themselves — the passages of the books of salvation being shut off — and choke a certain gullet of the soul, by a careless voracity. It is fitting, therefore, that thou first divide it, if the food which is taken seems solid to thee, and that thou distinguish it; and that, having softened it, thou pour it without any harm to the soul into all its members by a natural division; so that its whole body may feast upon the vital juice, and that thou take nothing corpse-like, nothing dead, into thy mouth, lest it be said: *Their throat is an open sepulchre [n: (Psalm. XIII, 3)]*. But mayest thou drink in the living word, [1905A] that it may be able to work in the inward parts of thy mind. These teeth are whiter than milk; because they are the teeth of the just.

## 15.

In short, although all our fathers were baptized in Moses in the cloud and in the sea, it is nevertheless not written idly: *That all ate the same spiritual food, and all drank the same spiritual drink [n: (I Cor. X, 3 et 4)]*; so that a certain greater brightness might come to these teeth of the saints, whom, after the passage of the Red Sea, we shall recognize to have been cleansed by the bitterness of the fountain of myrrh tempered through the grace of the wood *(Exodi XV, 25)*; then by the drink of the twelve fountains, and last by the watering of the rock that poured out a spiritual wave; *For the rock was Christ [n: (I Cor. X, 3 et 4)]*. *1582* Therefore they also ate manna [n: (Num. XX, 11)], that so often washed they might eat the bread, as it is written, [1905B] of angels [n: (Psalm. LXXVII, 25)]. Now too in the mysteries of the Gospel thou recognizest that the baptized, though washed in the whole body, are nevertheless afterwards cleansed by spiritual food and drink; for that is the true brightness of the teeth, where the tuneful confession of a mind well aware of itself resounds. That eye is clean which no beam of grave sins has weighed down, which no mote of light defilements has troubled.

## 16.

*Thy teeth are as a flock of shorn ones, which have come up from the washing, all of which bring forth twins, and there is not a barren one among them.* This is no ordinary praise: first, by the fitting comparison with shorn ones; for we know that she-goats both feed on the heights without danger, and take their food in safety upon sheer crags, and then, when they are shorn, are unburdened of what is superfluous. To their flock the Church is compared, having in herself many virtues of souls, which through the washing lay aside [1905C] their superfluous sins, which bring to Christ mystical faith and moral grace, which speak of the cross of the Lord Jesus. In these the Church is well-formed, whence God the Word says to her: *Behold, thou art well-formed, my neighbor: behold, thou art well-formed; thine eyes are as doves. Thou art wholly well-formed, my neighbor, and there is no reproof in thee;* [var: Vulg. Cant. IV, 1 and 7 *amica mea* … *macula non est in te*] because guilt has been drowned. *Come hither from Lebanon, O bride, come hither from Lebanon, and thou shalt pass through from the beginning of faith;* [var: Vulg. Cant. IV, 8 *veni de Libano … coronaberis de capite Amana*] in that, renouncing the world, she has passed beyond the age, and has passed over to Christ.

## 17.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] Thy lips are as a scarlet cord.* For in scarlet the appearance of fire, and the blood of the Lord's cross, glows red. The lips of the Lord are scarlet, which spoke his own passion. In short, in Exodus scarlet is set in the place of fire [n: (Exod. XXV, 4)]. For the world does not consist [1905D] of scarlet, but of four elements. But in scarlet the figure of fire is expressed, and unless its heat penetrated heaven and the air, the seas and the lands, all things would be dissolved as though their powers were spent. By the cord, therefore, we recognize the bond of persuasion; by the scarlet, either the ardour of desire which sparkles in the minds of the hearers, or the token of the passion.

## 18.

*[n: (Vers. 4.)] Thy neck is as the tower of David, which is built in Thalphioth; a thousand shields hang upon it, all the darts of the mighty.* For the neck is upright toward God, and fit for the yoke of Christ, and is bent by no bending down to the earth [1906A] of enticements; as the royal tower of Christ is, upon which Nebuchadnezzar cannot lay his yoke. For David, that man mighty of hand, founded that tower, and built it above the heights of the walls; that it might be both for defence and for adornment: for defence, because it foresees the enemy and drives him off: for adornment, because it stands out not only among lowly things, but even among lofty ones. Yet it is so for defence or for adornment only if it has in itself the teachings of the word as certain necklaces of trappings: if it has also the darts of the mighty prophets, which are aimed by certain arms of faith against every height that exalts itself.

## 19.

Do not, therefore, bring down into the dust of death thy soul, to which the Lord has granted also a natural height and vigour, by which it may rouse itself and rise up. [1906B] Whence also what is said of this conjunction of soul and body is opportunely taken, in that in the natural mystery of our life the soul, bound to the body, creeps as it were upon the ground and clings to the pavement, partly on account of this earthly dwelling, partly because this body is of earth. Both the lodging of the region, therefore, and the very matter of our body agree with this judgment. And therefore the Apostle beautifully desires to be freed from the body of this death; because we are shut in a certain prison, and, set in a certain hovel of wantonness, we are wrapped in the darkness of our offenses.

## 20.

Let us therefore walk according to the will of God, that we may be said to cleave to God. For he who lives according to the appetite of the body is flesh: he who lives according to the precepts of God is spirit. Let not our soul then become flesh, that is, so that we should be called flesh; like those also who perished in [1906C] the flood, of whom it was said: *That they are flesh [n: (Genes. VI, 3)]*. But rather let our flesh, obeying the governance of the soul, become soul, and deserve to be called by this name; as the household of the patriarch Jacob was called, and the holy posterity of his generation; for so it is written: *These are the sons of Bala, whom Laban gave to Rachel his daughter: she bore these to Jacob, seven souls.* And in the same place: *And all the souls which entered with Jacob into [sic: Aeyyptum], which came out of his thighs, besides the wives of his sons, sixty-six [n: (Genes. XLVI, 25 et 26)]*. And at the beginning of Exodus we read: *And all the souls from Jacob were seventy-five [n: (Exod. I, 5)]*. [var: Vulg. Exod. I, 5 *septuaginta*, seventy] *1583*

## 21.

Therefore those who dwelt with Joseph and came out of Egypt are souls: but they who, [1906D] though they were angels of God — that is, imitating by the uprightness of their life the grace of the angels ( *For they who [n: (Matth. XXII, 30)] do not take wives, and they who do not marry, shall be as the angels of God in heaven* ) [var: Vulg. Matth. XXII, 30 *neque nubent neque nubentur, sed erunt sicut angeli Dei in caelo*] — they, then, who, though they seemed to be angels, were captivated by womanly beauty, these are flesh, as the Lord God said: *My spirit shall not remain in men for ever, because they are flesh [n: (Genes. VI, 3)]*. [var: Vulg. Gen. VI, 3 *in homine … quia caro est*, in the singular] And rightly are those who have no part in lust compared to angels, for they are not flesh, because they are not in the flesh, but in the spirit: such were those who followed the Apostle as their teacher, to whom he says: *But you [1907A] are not in the flesh, but in the Spirit [n: (Rom. VIII, 9)]*. But those who are captivated by the bodily lust of women are flesh: and would that they were only flesh, and not neighing horses as well! *For,* he says, *they neighed after the wife of his neighbor [n: (Jerem. V, 8)]*.

## 22.

*[n: (Vers. 7.)] Thou art wholly well-formed, my neighbor, and there is no reproof in thee.* [var: Vulg. Cant. IV, 7 *Tota pulchra es, amica mea, et macula non est in te*] But who can reckon any beauty greater than the comeliness of her who is loved by the king, approved by the judge, dedicated to the Lord, consecrated to God. Always a bride, always unwed; so that neither may love have an end, nor modesty a loss. This assuredly is the true beauty, to which nothing is lacking, which alone deserves to hear from the Lord: *Thou art wholly well-formed, my neighbor, and there is no reproof in thee.*

## 23.

*[n: (Vers. 8.)] Come hither from Lebanon, come hither from Lebanon: [1907B] thou shalt pass over and pass through from the beginning of faith, from the head of Sanir and Hermon, from the lairs of lions, from the mountains of leopards.* By these tokens it is shown that the perfect and irreproachable beauty of the virgin soul, consecrated at the divine altars, not bent by mortal things amid the encounters and the lairs of spiritual beasts, but intent upon the mysteries of God, has deserved her beloved, whose breasts are full of gladness; for wine gladdens the heart of man.

## 24.

*And the odour,* he says, *of thy garments is as the odour of Lebanon. Come from Lebanon, O bride, come from Lebanon: thou shalt pass over and pass through from the beginning of faith:* thou shalt pass over into the world to do battle, thou shalt pass through to Christ to triumph over the world. Thou hast heard that he has separated thee from the assaults of lions and leopards, that is, of spiritual wickednesses: [1907C] thou hast heard that the beauty of thy virtues pleases him.



## 25.

*Come hither from Lebanon, bride, come hither from Lebanon: thou shalt pass over, and thou shalt pass through, from the beginning of faith, from the head of Sanir, and Hermoniim, from the caves of lions, from the mountains of leopards;* [var: Vulg. *Veni de Libano… coronaberis de capite Amana, de vertice Sanir et Hermon, de cubilibus leonum*, "come from Lebanon… thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana"] that is, go forth out of the body, and [sic: *exne*] thyself wholly. For thou canst not be present to me unless thou first be a pilgrim away from the body; since they who are in the flesh are pilgrims away from the kingdom of God. *Come hither,* he says, *come hither.* Well did he repeat it, because whether present or absent thou oughtest to be present to and to please the Lord thy God: be present when present, be present when absent, even if thou art still in the body; for all are present to me whose faith is with me. He is present to me who has gone out from the world: he is present to me who thinks of me, who gazes on me, who hopes from me, to whom I am the portion: he is present to me who [1907D] has been absent from himself: he is with me who is not within himself; since he who is in the flesh is not in the spirit: he is with me who goes forth out of his own self: he is beside me who has been outside himself: he is whole to me who for my sake has lost his own soul; and therefore, *Come hither, come hither, bride; thou shalt pass over and pass through from the beginning of faith.* She passes over and passes beyond the rest who has come through to Christ: she passes over by the merit of faith and by the brightness of works, she who shines like Sanir and Hermon, that is, the way of the lamp: she passes over when the temptations of the world are conquered and the spiritual [1908A] wickednesses overcome, seeking the crown of a lawful contest.

## 26.

*[n: (Vers. 9.)] Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, bride, thou hast wounded my heart with one of thine eyes, and with one hair of thy neck.* For these eyes are the eyes of the mind, namely of the inner man, not these eyes which discharge the service of looking. For there is an eye both of the mind and of the flesh, but that eye is blind which does not see the things that are divine, which is puffed up in vain by the mind of the flesh. There is also another eye, the sense of Christ, by which the Church sees Christ, as he himself says to the Bride: *Thou hast taken us in heart by one of thine eyes.* [var: Vulg. *Vulnerasti cor meum in uno oculorum tuorum*, "thou hast wounded my heart with one of thine eyes"] Rightly is Christ seen with one eye, because he is not seen with the eye of the flesh, or because the Church, having two eyes, the moral and the mystical, sees Christ more with the eye of faith; [1908B] for the mystical eye is the keener, the moral the sweeter.

## 27.

And perhaps these are the eyes with which Paul saw eternal things, when he began not to see temporal things. In short, he who did not see Christ before he lost his eyes, saw *1584* him after he had lost the sight of his eyes; for he saw who said: *Who art thou, Lord [n: (Act. IX, 5)]*? Certainly he saw Christ, whom he also confessed as Lord. And below, *Lord,* he says, *what wilt thou have me to do?* Did he not then see him whose command he was awaiting? With what eyes therefore did Paul begin to see more, save with those which he himself showed us, saying: *I will pray with the spirit, I will pray also with the mind [n: (I Cor. XIV, 15)]*? In short, that thou mayest know that he saw while praying: *It came to pass,* he says, *when I was returning to Jerusalem, while I was praying in the [1908C] temple, I had a dread, I saw him saying to me: Make haste, go out more quickly from Jerusalem; for they will not receive thy testimony concerning me [n: (Act. XXII, 17 et 18)]*. [var: Vulg. *fieri me in stupore… exi velociter ex Jerusalem: quoniam non recipient*, "that I was in a trance… go out speedily from Jerusalem"]

## 28.

These eyes therefore fail for the word of God, and say: *When wilt thou comfort me?* With these eyes the prophets were called seers, because through revelation they discerned with the mind the things that were hidden. But because the eye of the mind and the eye of the flesh are one eye, and man is then upheld by consolation when the flesh and the mind do not desire diverse things, but desire one thing and seek one thing; therefore they are intent upon him who says: *I and the Father are one [n: (Joan. X, 30)]*. These eyes too confess that they are one; because they discharge one and the same desire and office.

## 29.

*[n: (Vers. 10.)] Thy breasts are more beautiful than wine, [1908D] and the odor of thine ointments above all spices.* And below: *The odor of thy garments is as of Lebanon.* [var: Vulg. *sicut odor thuris*, "as the odor of frankincense"] See what advance thou grantest us, virgin! For thy first odor is above all the spices which were sent to the Saviour's burial, and it breathes out that the motions of the body are dead and that the delights of the members have perished. Thy second odor, as the odor of Lebanon, breathes forth the integrity of the Lord's body, the flower of virginal chastity. Let thy works therefore compose a honeycomb of honey. The odor of the garments also is the odor of our acts. [1909A] Bring thy hands therefore to thy nostrils, and with unwearied and ever-watchful eagerness of mind search out the odor of thine acts. The odor of thy right hand will soothe thee, and thy members will breathe out the fragrance of the resurrection, thy fingers will sweat myrrh, that is, thy spiritual deeds will breathe out the grace of true faith. Thou takest therefore, virgin, delight from thine inner body, and thou art sweet to thyself, thou art pleasant to thyself, and thou dost not begin to be displeasing to thyself (which often happens to those who sin).

## 30.

*[n: (Vers. 11.)] A dropping honeycomb, thy lips, bride.* Let thy works therefore compose a honeycomb of honey; for virginity is worthy to be compared to bees, so laborious, so chaste, so continent: the bee feeds on dew, knows no coupling, composes honey. Dew too, for the virgin, is the divine word; because the words of God come down like dew. [1909B] The virgin's modesty is an unviolated nature, the virgin's offspring is the fruit of the lips, without bitterness, fertile in sweetness: the labor is for the common good, the fruit is common. How I would wish thee, daughter, to be an imitator of this little bee, whose food is the flower, whose offspring is gathered with the mouth, composed with the mouth. Do thou imitate her, daughter; let thy words hold out no veil of guile, let them have no wrapping of fraud; that they may both have purity and be full of gravity. Let an eternal posterity of thy merits also be brought forth for thee by thy mouth, and gather not for thyself alone, but for many also for what dost thou know when thy soul shall be required of thee; lest, leaving behind the receptacles of thy granaries heaped up with grain, which will serve neither the use of thy life nor thy merits, thou be snatched away thither, whither thou canst not carry thy treasure. Be rich therefore, [1909C] but for the poor man, that they who share thy nature may share thy means also.

## 31.

*A dropping honeycomb, thy lips, bride.* What the honeycomb is, teach us, Solomon. For thou hast said: *Good words are a honeycomb of honey [n: (Prov. XVI, 24)]*. [var: Vulg. *Favus mellis composita verba*, "well-ordered words are a honeycomb of honey"] And truly a good honeycomb, which the Church eats, redolent of honey gathered by the spiritual richness of many prophets as of bees. This is the honey of which he says: *I have eaten my bread with my honey [n: (Cant. V, 1)]*. [var: Vulg. *comedi favum cum melle meo*, "I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey"] The lips of the preacher drop honey, when the limbs of a fallen soul, bruised by hard mischances or downfalls, are revived. *Thy lips drop a honeycomb, bride, honey and milk are under thy tongue;* [var: Vulg. *Favus distillans labia tua*, "thy lips are a dropping honeycomb"] for the mouth of the just distils wisdom: from the mouth of the just proceed the honeys of sweetness and of mercy: in the mouth of the just there is no guile, no deceit, no bitterness [1909D] of sin. The Church hears the words of the just, the people of God hears the words of the wise man, is delighted by the sweetness of the address, is soothed by the pleasantness of moral discourse, saying: *How sweet are thy words to my throat, above honey and the honeycomb to my mouth [n: (Psalm. CXVIII, 103)]*! [var: Vulg. *Quam dulcia faucibus meis eloquia tua, super mel ori meo*, "how sweet are thy words to my throat, above honey to my mouth" — without *et favum*] For the honey of bees delights for an hour, but its savor quickly *1585* vanishes, and for the most part corrupt bowels are hurt: but the honeys of moral words, though they sting, do not hurt.

## 32.

Know however to whom those things are entrusted; for it is written: *Say nothing into the ear of a fool; lest at any time [1910A] he mock thy prudent words [n: (Prov. XXIII, 9)]*. [var: Vulg. *In auribus insipientium ne loquaris, quia despicient doctrinam eloquii tui*, "speak not in the ears of fools, for they will despise the instruction of thy speech"] For the fool will vomit out and cast back thy word, who cannot perceive its sweetness. How can the words of God be sweet in thy throat, in which is the bitterness of wickedness? How is there *honey and milk under thy tongue,* when thy tongue frames guile; so that thou conceivest one thing in thy breast, and puttest forward another in public speech, that thou mayest deceive the unwary? Since Peter the apostle says to thee that thou shouldest lay aside all malice, and cast off the paint of dissimulation, saying: *As infants born a while ago, reasonable and without guile, desire ye milk [n: (I Pet. II, 2)]*. [var: Vulg. *sicut modo geniti infantes, rationabile, sine dolo lac concupiscite*, "as newborn infants desire the reasonable milk without guile"] For showing us what it is to have honey and milk under the tongue, he teaches that we should know not to curse those who curse, but to render blessing: let us not know how to hate save the way of iniquity, that with a resisting [1910B] affection we may beware of doing what we execrate.



## 33.

*[n: (Vers. 12.)] A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed. Thy shoots,* etc. Because in gardens of this kind the image of God, impressed by the seals, shines back in the wave of the unmixed fountain, nor are the streams, bespattered with mire, disturbed by the wallowing-places of spiritual beasts. Hence that modesty, hedged about by a wall-like spirit, is shut in, lest it lie open to plunder. And so, like a garden inaccessible to thieves, it is redolent of the vine, it breathes the olive, it shines with the rose; that in the vine religion, in the olive peace, in the rose the modesty of consecrated virginity may grow. This is the odor which Jacob the patriarch breathed, when he deserved to hear: *Behold the odor of my son, as the odor of a full field [n: (Genes. XXVII, 27)]*. For although the field of the holy patriarch was full of almost all fruits, yet he brought forth crops by a greater [1910C] labor of virtue, this one flowers. Gird thyself therefore, virgin, and if thou wishest such a garden to breathe upon thee, shut it with the prophetic precepts: *Set a guard to thy mouth, and a door of enclosure to thy lips [n: (Psal. CXL, 3)]*. [var: Vulg. *Pone, Domine, custodiam ori meo, et ostium circumstantiae labiis meis*, "set, O Lord, a guard to my mouth"]

## 34.

*A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed; thy shoots a paradise of pomegranates, with the fruit of the apples of Cyprus.* [var: Vulg. *cum pomorum fructibus, cypri cum nardo*, "with the fruits of apples, cypress with spikenard"] The Bride is praised in that she is a garden, having in herself the odor of that full field, of which Isaac says: *The odor of my son, as the odor of a full field [n: (Genes. XXVII, 27)]*. A good soul therefore breathes out the odors of justice. And perhaps the field is the patriarch, the garden is the soul of some lesser one, as it were a portion of the field: and *a garden enclosed,* lest it be assailed by beasts: and *a fountain sealed,* she who by the integrity of the seal [1910D] and by the perseverance of faith has washed away her own sins. For he who has received from the Church; has that which he may refer to the grace of virginity; in that, placed in the paradise of delight, she takes spiritual fruits without labor, so that the souls of the patriarchs confer their own fruits upon her by a certain rustic labor of their own soul, whereby she may be able to take perpetual sweetness: who is rightly called a fountain sealed, in that the image of the invisible God is expressed in her.

## 35.

*A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed.* All the teachers agree [1911A] upon paradise, and upon the tree of life rooted there and the tree of knowledge, by which one may discern good and evil: and the other trees full of vigor, full of quickening, breathing and rational. From which it is gathered that paradise itself can be seen as earthly, not in any soil, but in our ruling part, by which the soul is animated and quickened with virtues and with the infusion of the Holy Spirit. In short Solomon in the spirit plainly declared that paradise is in man. And because he expresses the mysteries either of the soul and the Word, or of Christ and the Church; therefore he says of the virgin soul or the Church, whom he wished to assign as a chaste virgin to Christ: *A paradise enclosed, my sister, bride, a paradise enclosed, a fountain sealed.* Paradise in Greek is in Latin called a garden. In short Susanna was in a paradise, and so it is read in Latin: and Adam was in [1911B] paradise, and so we read. Therefore let it not disturb thee that some Latin codices have *hortum*, others *paradisum*. There then is the chaste wife, where the virgin also is. Although the virgin, set apart, has her own enclosures and seals, yet both are in paradise; that against the heats of the body and the burning of the flesh she may be cooled by the shades of the virtues.

## 36.

Therefore paradise is in our ruling part, growing wild with the nurseries of very many opinions, in which God chiefly set the tree of life, that is, the root of piety. For this is the substance of our life, if we render to the Lord our God *1586* the worship due to him. He set also a seed-plot of the knowledge of good and evil; for man alone among the other earthly living creatures has the knowledge of good and evil. There too are other [1911C] divers nurseries, whose fruits are the virtues. But because the affection of man, capable of knowledge, was known to God, that it would sooner incline to cunning than to the height of prudence; nor could the quality of his own work lie hidden from him, the judge who set fixed bounds in our soul: he willed to cast out cunning from paradise, as the provident author of our salvation, and to pour in the pursuit of life and the discipline of piety. And therefore he commanded man that of every tree which is in paradise he should taste, but that of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil he should not taste. But because every creature is liable to passion, therefore delight crept into human affections as by a serpent's gliding.

## 37.

And so not without reason did holy Moses figure delight under the likeness of a serpent; for it is prone [1911D] upon its belly like a serpent, not walking upon feet, nor raised upon any legs: but slippery, as it were, with the winding bend of its whole body. Earth is its food, as it is the serpent's, since it knows no heavenly meat; for it feeds upon bodily things, and is changed into various kinds of desires, and is bent into tortuous windings. It has venom in its teeth, with which every luxurious man disembowels himself, the glutton slays himself, the squanderer destroys himself. How many have wines burst, drunkenness undone, indigestion distended! Now I understand for what cause the Lord God breathed into the face of man; for there are all the senses, there the seat and the enticement of delight, in the eyes, the ears, the nostrils, and in the mouth; that he might make our senses stronger [1912A] against delight. This then poured cunning into us as the serpent did; for it is not delight, but labor and long meditation with the grace of God, that gives perfect prudence. Yet because the inheritance of the human race is wrapped in the deceits of the serpent, let us in this follow the serpent's cunning, that we cast not our head into dangers, but keep it whole before all else; and our head is Christ. Let this remain unharmed, that the serpent's venoms may not be able to hurt us: for wisdom is good with an inheritance, that is, with faith; since there is an inheritance of those who believe in the Lord.

## 38.

But if the first man, who spoke with God, set in paradise, could fall so easily, created from virgin earth, which had been formed and created at the word of God in a fresh springing forth, that earth not yet clotted with parricidal [1912B] blood and the blood of the rest, not yet polluted with crimes and disgrace, our flesh not yet condemned by the curse of a liable inheritance: how much more easily afterwards, the road to sinning being slippery, did it bring a greater downfall upon the human race, when the worse succeeded the more tolerable through the turns of generation! For if the magnet stone has such force of nature that it draws iron to itself, and pours itself over into its likeness; so that, when many who wished to make trial have brought several iron rings to that stone, it holds them all in like manner: then if to that ring to which the stone has clung thou bring another ring, and again set one under each in order; although the force of nature from that stone penetrates them all in order, yet it binds the earlier ones with a more vehement bond, the later with a slacker: [1912C] how much more has the condition and nature of the human race fallen from a purer state into a worse, the more wicked the state it has reached! For if nature is diminished through those kinds of things which are not capable of guilt: how much more is its strength blunted through minds and limbs polluted with the stain of crimes! Whence, because when malice prevailed innocence had been abolished, there was none that did goodness, there was not so much as one. The Lord therefore came, to reform the grace of nature, nay rather to increase it; that where sin had superabounded, grace might superabound.

## 39.

It is clear therefore that God is the author of man, and that there is one God, not many gods, but one who wrought the world, and one world, not many worlds, as the philosophers say. First then he created the world, [1912D] then the inhabitant of the world, to whom the whole world should be a fatherland: for if today the wise man goes anywhere, everywhere he is a citizen, everywhere he understands what is his own, nowhere does he judge himself a stranger, nowhere a guest: how much more was that first man an inhabitant of the whole world, and, as it is said in Greek, *cosmopolites*, a fresh *1587* converser with God, an assiduous citizen among the saints! planted about with virtues, set over all living creatures of the land, of the sea, of the air, he counted the whole world his own possession, which the Lord watched over as his own work, nor did he, as a good parent and author, desert it. In short he so cherished the creature he had made, that he redeemed him when disowned, received him back when cast out, raised him up when dead by the passion of his only-begotten Son. God therefore is the author of man, [1913A] and as a good workman he loves his own work, nor does the good father forsake him whom, like a rich householder, he has redeemed at the cost of his own inheritance.



## 40.

Let us beware therefore lest that woman, that is, the passion of our senses, herself deceived and mocked by delight, make effeminate that man, that is, the νοῦν, and draw him, circumscribed, into her own laws and judgment. Let us flee delight as a serpent, it has many arts and chiefly in man. For other living creatures are caught by greediness for food; man, since he has more senses of the eyes and of the ears, has by so much the greater dangers. And so beware lest the vigor of thy mind, bent by a certain coupling with bodily pleasure, be softened, and be dissolved into all its embraces, and open its fountain, which ought to be closed and hedged about by earnestness of purpose [1913B] and by the consideration of reason: *For a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed.* For when the sense of the mind is unstrung, bodily delights pour themselves forth, exceedingly destructive, and flowing on into an appetite full of grave danger; and if the considered guard of a living mind had remained, it would have reined them in.

## 41.

*[n: (Vers. 3, 4.)] Thy shoots,* he says, *are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruit of apples, cypress with spikenard, spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon with all the trees of Lebanon, myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments.* The gifts of the soul are praised which were sent by the Bridegroom, with which she came dowered (and to God the good odors are those of a devout soul), myrrh, aloes, saffron and the rest, with which the grace of the gardens breathes, and the stench of sins is done away. And so, made secure by so great a proclamation, [1913C] she asks that the north wind, that heavy wind, be still, lest it shake off the flowers: that the south wind blow, that is, that the winter as it were pass by, and that the mildness of a softer breath come as spring. She invites the Bridegroom into her garden, the bridegroom comes down, and delighted by the diversity of its fruits, he rejoices that he has found a stronger food, and has found a sweeter also. For there is as it were a certain bread of the Word and a honey, one discourse more vehement, another persuasive. There is also one faith more fervent, like wine; another clearer, as is the juice of milk. This food Christ feasts upon in us, this cup he drinks, and by the drunkenness of his draught he provokes us to make a passing over from lower things to better and best.

## 42.

The soul hearing these things drank in the drunkenness of the heavenly mysteries, and as though lulled to sleep by wine, and set as it were in [1913D] ecstasy or stupor, says: *I sleep, and my heart waketh [n: (Cant. V, 2)]*. Then, struck by the light of the Word present, she is roused by the Word. And this is the fourth advance of the soul. For first, impatient of love, and not bearing the Word's delays, she asked that she might merit kisses, and merited to see him whom she desired. Secondly, brought into the king's chamber, while she exchanged mutual speech, she rested in his shadow, and suddenly the Word departed from her in mid-discourse; yet he was not long absent from her as she sought him, but came leaping upon the mountains and skipping over the hills. And not long after, like [1914A] a roe or a young hart of the stags, while he speaks to his beloved, he leapt away and left her. Thirdly, when in her bed and by night, in the city and the market place, in the streets, she did not find him whom she sought, and at length grace by her prayers called back the one who was seeking, so far even that she was called nearer by the bridegroom. Fourthly, she herself, now sleeping, is roused by him, although she was waking in heart; so that at once she heard the voice of him that knocked: but having suffered delay, while she rises (because she could not grasp the swiftness of the Word), while she opens the door, the Word passed by, and she herself went out at his word: and having sought him through wounds, but the wounds of love, at last she scarcely found him, and held him, that she might not afterwards lose him. In sum I have touched upon these things in a compendious discourse, now let us examine them one by one.

## 43.

*[n: (Vers. 15.)] A fountain of gardens, a well of living [1914B] waters, which flow with force from Lebanon.* Thou hast these things in Solomon, since his Proverbs are moral, Ecclesiastes natural, in which he as it were despises the vanities of this world: mystical is his Song *1588* of Songs. Thou hast it also in the prophet: *Sow for yourselves unto justice, gather the vintage unto the fruit of life, light for yourselves the light of knowledge [n: (Ose. X, 12)]*; [var: Vulg. *Seminate vobis in justitia, et metite in ore misericordiae; innovate vobis novale*, "sow for yourselves in justice, and reap in the mouth of mercy; break up your fallow ground"] for this is the light of knowledge, to have the perfection of charity. And therefore it is said: *Fear not [n: (Joan. XII, 15)]*; [var: Vulg. *Noli timere, filia Sion*, "fear not, daughter of Sion"] for charity casteth fear out of doors.

## 44.

But that we may know that Solomon so interpreted these three wells, which Jacob dug, as to refer them to moral and natural and mystical teaching, in each of his books, which he wrote concerning moral and natural or mystical things, he set these wells. [1914C] For in the Proverbs too, when he said that the appearance of worldly enticement must be turned aside, he says: *Drink water out of thine own vessels, and out of the fountains of thine own wells, and let the waters overflow for thee out of thine own fountain [n: (Prov. V, 15)]*. [var: Vulg. *Bibe aquam de cisterna tua, et fluenta putei tui*, "drink water out of thy own cistern, and the streams of thy own well"] And below: *Let the fountain of thy water be thine own, and rejoice with the wife who is from thy youth [n: (Ibid., 18)]*; [var: Vulg. *Sit vena tua benedicta, et laetare cum muliere adolescentiae tuae*, "let thy vein be blessed, and rejoice with the wife of thy youth"] in that true wisdom is a remedy for us against the temptations of the world. Moral teaching also, which may wash away with its own watering the image of worldly pleasure smeared with certain harlot's paints, and wipe it off with the stream of its own fountain.

## 45.

Of natural things too thou hast the same in Ecclesiastes: *I made myself pools of waters, to water from them a sprouting wood [n: (Eccles. II, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *Exstruxi mihi piscinas aquarum, ut irrigarem silvam lignorum germinantium*, "I made myself pools of water, to water the wood of sprouting trees"] And let it not move thee that he put pools for a well, since Moses too spoke of a well of breadth; because he is loosed from all anxiety [1914D] and from straits who has passed beyond this world with a devout mind. Not without reason therefore has Ecclesiastes pools, who saw that there is no abundance under the sun: but if any wish to abound, let him abound in Christ.

## 46.

Of mystical things there remains for us also the well, which we find in the Song of Songs itself, Scripture saying: *A fountain of gardens, a well of living water, and coming down with force from Lebanon.* For if thou pursue the depth of the mysteries, it seems to thee a well, as though mystical wisdom were set in the deep: but if thou wouldst draw the affluence of charity, which is greater and richer [1915A] than faith and hope, then it is a fountain for thee. For charity overflows, so that thou canst both draw it close at hand, and water with its affluence thy garden, abounding in spiritual fruits. And because he himself is a well of breadth who has charity; therefore he said that where charity is, there a great force comes down from Lebanon.

## 47.

But lest anything move thee, that he called the same thing both a well and a fountain; let the Gospel also instruct thee, in which it is written: *That Jesus came into a city of Samaria, which is called Sichar, near the estate which Jacob gave to Joseph his son [n: (Joan. IV, 5 et 6)]*. And the fountain of Jacob was there: *And being wearied,* he says, *he sat thus upon the well.* Whence we recognize there also that this well is referred to mystical teaching, because there that Samaritan woman, that is, a keeper (and a keeper of the heavenly [1915B] precepts) drew from that well the divine mysteries, knowing that God is a spirit, and is not adored in a place, but in spirit: and that the Messias comes, who is Christ. Having heard these things, that woman, who bears the figure of the Church, knew the sacraments of the Law and believed.

## 48.

*A fountain of gardens, a well of living waters, and a force coming down from Lebanon.* These forces the Church has drawn down from Lebanon, by this force sins are washed away, by this force of the pure fountain of the Holy Spirit the Bride came from Lebanon, and from the beginning of faith she passed over the world, and passed through to the kingdom. To some spiritual grace is a fountain, to others a well, according to our capacity: to some a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed, to others a fountain of gardens, which is numbered in the Church's dowry: to others a force coming down from Lebanon, [1915C] and a great force which never fails. For the breasts fail not from the rock, nor the snow from Lebanon, nor the water which is borne by a strong wind to the virgin Jerusalem.



## 49.

The force came down from Lebanon, when, the apostles and many believers being gathered into one, there was suddenly a sound from heaven, as though a spirit were borne with great violence, and they were all filled with the Holy Spirit, who granted diversities of tongues [n: (Act. II, 1, 3)]. A good force, which knows not how to hurt, but knows how to fill. If anyone therefore wishes to merit this force of grace coming upon him from heaven, let him also himself go down with his eyes into the runnings of waters. He who has first poured out this force will merit that one. She went down with her eyes into these channels of waters, she who in the Gospel watered the Lord's feet with tears [1915D] [n: (Luc. VII, 38)], and therefore by the price of her faith bought health for her soul and for her body, flowing now no longer with blood, but with spiritual grace.

*1589.* 50. David the prophet therefore went down, and therefore he brought back grace out of sin. He went down into the channels of waters, that is, he filled them, and heaped up the streams of the running waters with his own tears, or filled the empty and void channels with weeping alone. Or, because he shows certain senses of moral utterance, he went down into the channels of waters, he passed over them. And we might say, he transcended [1916A] them, and passed above them: but the force of the utterance is lessened, whereby a greater force of the affluence coming down than of that going up is expressed. See, I pray, what the words have from usage, that the prophetic discourse may not lose its own force, although usage itself has been accustomed, with greater fitness, to serve the sense of the writers.

## 51.

*A well of living waters;* for a well, if thou draw nothing from it, is easily corrupted by inert idleness and degenerate mould: but when it is used it grows bright to the sight, sweet to the drinking. So too a heap of riches, sandy in its pile, is comely in use, but in idleness is held useless. Draw off therefore something from this well: *For water quencheth a burning fire, and alms resisteth sins [n: (Eccli. III, 33)]*. But standing water quickly breeds worms: let not thy treasure stand, lest thy fire stand. [1916B] It will stand in thee, unless thou turn it away by the works of thy mercy.

## 52.

*[n: (Vers. 16.)] Arise, north wind, and come, south wind, breathe upon my garden, and my spices flow forth.* [var: Vulg. *Surge, aquilo, et veni, auster; perfla hortum meum, et fluant aromata illius*, "arise, north wind… and let the spices thereof flow"] Thou hast heard, bride of Christ, that the beauty of thy virtues pleases him; thou hast heard that he has preferred the spices of thy garments, that is, the good odor of integrity, above all odors. Thou hast heard that thou art a garden enclosed, filled with the produce of sweet fruit-trees. Ask therefore that the Holy Spirit may breathe upon thee, may breathe above thy bed, and may heap up the odor of a devout mind and of spiritual grace. Rouse the Holy Spirit, saying: *Arise, north wind, and come, south wind; breathe upon my garden, and let my spices flow forth. Let my kinsman come down into his garden, and eat the fruit of his fruit-trees.* [1916C] The garden of the Word is the affection of the soul in its spring, and in the fruit-trees is the fruit of virtue. He comes therefore, and whether thou eat or drink, if thou callest upon Christ, he is present, saying: *Come, eat my loaves, and drink my wine [n: (Prov. IX, 5)]*. [var: Vulg. *Venite, comedite panem meum, et bibite vinum quod miscui vobis*, "come, eat my bread, and drink the wine which I have mingled for you"]

## 53.

*Arise,* he says, *north wind, and come, south wind; breathe upon my garden, and let my spices flow forth.* For from all parts of the world the odor of holy religion has grown, with which the limbs of the beloved virgin have been fragrant. Whence the Church also, keeping the loftiness of the heavenly mysteries, casts from herself the heavier storms of the wind, and invites the sweetness of springlike grace; and knowing that her garden cannot displease Christ, she calls her Bridegroom: *Arise,* he says, *north wind, and come, south wind.* Very many take these words thus, as though the north wind were cast out and the south wind invited. [1916D] But if they so take it, the icy harshness of unbelief is driven out from the Church, lest our flight be in winter or on the sabbath, and the southern springtime mildness is invited.

## 54.

Or certainly: *Arise, north wind,* that is, *rise, thou that sleepest, and arise from the dead [n: (Ephes. V, 14)]*. Thou people of the nations, who hast long slept before, awake at last, and Christ shall shine upon thee. Lastly all are invited to the Church, both the people of the Synagogue and that of the Gentiles: but the Synagogue's first, because the apostles from among the Jews believed first [1917A] and through them afterwards the peoples of the nations were gathered. See therefore our sun coming to the south, afterwards wheeling round to the north. *Jerusalem, Jerusalem:* he came indeed to her, whom he even deigned to call: but this Jerusalem is the earthly one, which slays [sic: *eccidit*] the prophets, that is, the Synagogue of the Jews: *How often,* he says, *would I have gathered thy children, as a hen her chickens, and thou wouldest not?
