Behold your house shall be left desolate [n: (Matth. XXIII, 37 et 38)]*. He wheeled therefore to the nations: and in wheeling the Spirit of God wheeled, and was turned into his own circles, that God might become all in all.

## CHAPTER FIVE.



## 1.

*[n: (Vers. 1.)] Let my beloved come into my garden, and eat the fruit of his apples.* [var: Vulg. *Veniat dilectus meus in hortum suum*, "let my beloved come into HIS garden"] Hence Plato framed for himself that garden [1917B] which he called in one place the garden of Jove, in another the garden of the mind; for he called Jove both God and mind. Into this, he says, the soul entered, which he names Venus, that she might fill herself with the abundance and riches of the garden, in which, filled with drink, she might lie, with a drink that poured out nectar. This therefore he framed out of the book of the Songs; in that the soul cleaving to God has entered into the garden of the mind, in which there was an abundance *1590* of divers virtues, and the flowers of discourses. And who does not know that out of that paradise, which we read in Genesis to have the tree of life and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil [n: (Gen. II, 8)], and the other trees, he thought an abundance of virtues was to be transferred, and to be planted in the garden of the mind?

## 2.

Which garden in the Song of Songs signifies the soul, or the soul herself; for so it is written: [1917C] *A garden enclosed, bride, my sister, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed.* And below the soul says: *Arise, north wind, and come, south wind; blow through my garden, and let thine ointments flow down, let my brother come down into his garden [n: (Cant. IV, 12 et 13)]*. How much more beautiful is this, that the soul adorned with the flowers of the virtues should be a garden; or should have in herself a sprouting paradise, into which garden she invites the Word of God to come down; that that soul, watered by the shower of the heavenly Word and by his abundance, may bear fruit! But the Word of God is fed with the virtues of the soul, as often as he has found the soul obedient to him and rich: and he plucks her fruits, and is delighted with them. And when the Word of God has come down into her, the ointments of healthful words flow down from her, and the breathings of divers [1917D] graces glow, redolent far and wide. Whence the Bridegroom says (and the Bridegroom of the soul is God the Word, to whom the soul is joined by a certain lawful covenant of wedlock):

## 3.

*[n: (Vers. 2.)] I have entered into my garden, my sister, bride; I have gathered my myrrh with my ointments, I have eaten my bread with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk. Eat, my neighbors, and drink and be drunken, my brethren. I sleep, and my heart waketh.* [var: Vulg. *Veni in hortum meum… messui myrrham meam cum aromatibus meis; comedi favum cum melle meo… Comedite, amici… carissimi*, "I came into my garden… I reaped my myrrh with my spices; I ate the honeycomb… eat, friends… dearest ones"] Let us learn what fruits or foods God feasts upon, or in what he is delighted. He is delighted in this, if any [1918A] mortify his own sin, blot out his own fault, bury and abolish his iniquities; for myrrh is the burial of the dead, and dead are the sins which cannot have the sweetness of life. And certain wounds of offenses are bathed with the ointments of the divine word, and are healed with the stronger food of the word as with bread, and with sweeter discourse as with honey. And Solomon teaches elsewhere too that words are foods, saying: *Good words are honeycombs of honey [n: (Prov. XVI, 24)]*. [var: Vulg. *Favus mellis composita verba*, "well-ordered words are a honeycomb of honey"] In that garden therefore there are good words, one that may restrain fault, another that may rebuke iniquity, another that may make insolence die, and as it were bury it, when one who has been rebuked renounces his errors.

## 4.

There is also a stronger word, which strengthens the heart of man [1918B] with the more powerful nourishments of the heavenly Scripture. *I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk.* For there is a persuasive word, sweet as honey, and yet in its very sweetness pricking the conscience of the sinner. There is also a word of more fervent spirit, which makes drunken like wine, and gladdens the heart of man. There is finally a milky word, pure and white. These foods of sweet and useful words the Bridegroom bids his neighbors feast upon: *Eat, my neighbors, and drink, and be drunken, my brethren.* And the neighbors are those who follow him, and are present at his marriage. With which food and drink (for every man drinks [n: (Prov. V, 15)] water out of his own vessels, and out of the fountains of his own wells) the soul, filled and made drunken, was sleeping to the world, was waking to God. And therefore, as what follows teaches, God the Word demanded that her door be opened to him, that he might fill her with his entering. [1918C]



## 5.

Hence therefore those Platonic feasters, hence that nectar out of the prophetic wine and honey, hence that sleep was transferred, hence that perpetual life which he said his gods feast upon, because Christ is life. And therefore with the seeds of such words the belly of his soul was filled, and she herself went out at the Word: and the soul which goes out from that servitude, and lifts herself up from the body, follows the Word. Otherwise, that we may go back somewhat to what is above [n: (Sup., cap. 4)]: *A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed.* By which he signifies that the mystery ought to remain sealed with thee, lest it be violated by the works of an evil life, and by the adultery of chastity: lest it be divulged to those to whom it is not fitting, lest it be scattered among the faithless by garrulous talkativeness.

## 6.

[1918D] Good therefore ought the guard of thy faith to be, that the undefiled integrity of life and of silence may persevere. Whence the Church also, keeping the loftiness of the heavenly mysteries, casts from herself the heavier storms of the wind, and invites the sweetness of springlike grace. And knowing that her garden cannot displease Christ, she calls the bridegroom himself, saying: *Arise, north wind, and come, south wind: blow through my garden, and let my ointments flow [n: (Cant. IV, 10)]*: *1591* *let my brother come down into his garden, and eat the fruit of his fruit-trees [n: (Cant. V, 1)]*; for she has good and fruitful trees, and her roots [1919A] which she had dipped in the watering of the sacred fountain, and they have sprouted into good fruits with the bud of a new fecundity; so that they may no longer be cut down by the prophetic axe, but be made fruitful with evangelical richness. In short the Lord, delighted by their fertility, answers:

## 7.

*I have entered into my garden, my sister, bride: I have gathered myrrh with my ointments, I have eaten my bread with my honey: I have drunk my drink with my milk.* Why I have said bread and wine, the faithful understand. But this is not doubtful, that he himself eats and drinks in us, as thou hast read in our case, that he said he was in prison [n: (Matth. XXV, 36)]. Whence the Church also, seeing so great a grace, exhorts her sons, exhorts her neighbors, to run together to the sacraments, saying: *Eat, my neighbors, and drink, and be drunken, my [1919B] brethren.* What we should eat, what we should drink, the Holy Spirit has expressed to thee elsewhere through the prophet, saying: *Taste, and see that the Lord is sweet; blessed is the man that hopeth in him [n: (Psalm. XXXIII, 9)]*. In that sacrament is Christ, because it is the body of Christ: it is therefore not bodily food, but spiritual. Whence the Apostle also says concerning its type: *That our fathers ate spiritual food, and drank spiritual drink [n: (I Cor. X, 3)]*. For the body of God is a spiritual body, the body of Christ is the body of the divine Spirit; because Christ is spirit, as we read: *The Spirit before our face, Christ the Lord [n: (Thren. IV, 20)]*. [var: Vulg. *Spiritus oris nostri, christus Dominus*, "the breath of our mouth, Christ the Lord"] And in Peter's epistle we have: *Christ died for us [n: (I Pet. II, 21)]*. [var: Vulg. *Christus passus est pro nobis*, "Christ suffered for us"] In short this food strengthens our heart, and this drink gladdens the heart of man, as the prophet recorded [1919C] [n: (Psal. CIII, 25)].

## 8.

*A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed;* let her not easily open her mouth, nor unseal it with common speech; for it is not fitting that thou answer even concerning divine things themselves, unless questioned by the word of God. What hast thou to do with the rest? Speak to Christ alone, converse with Christ alone. For if it is written that women should keep silence in the Church [n: (I Cor. XIV, 34)]: how much more is it unfitting that a virgin's door lie open, unfitting that a widow's doors lie open! Quickly the plotter against modesty creeps in, quickly he catches up a word which thou wouldst wish to call back. If Eve's door had been closed, neither would Adam have been deceived, nor would she, being questioned, have answered the serpent. Death entered through the window, that is, through Eve's door. Death enters through thy door, if thou speak falsehood, if basely, if [1919D] shamelessly, lastly if thou speak where it is not fitting. Let the doors of thy lips therefore be closed, and let the vestibule of thy voice remain barred, then perhaps to be unbarred, when thou shalt have heard the voice of God, when thou shalt have heard the word of God. Then myrrh shall sweat for thee, then the grace of baptism shall breathe upon thee, that thou mayest die together with Christ from the elements of the world, and rise again with Christ. *Why yet,* he says, *as though living, do ye decree concerning this world? Touch not, handle not, taste not, the things which are unto corruption by the very use [n: (Coloss. II, 20 et seq.)]*. [var: Vulg. *Ne tetigeritis, neque gustaveritis, neque contrectaveritis: quae sunt omnia in interitum ipso usu*, "touch not, taste not, handle not: which are all unto destruction by the very use"]

## 9.

*Let my brother come down into his garden, that he may eat [1920A] the fruit of his fruit-trees.* What are these fruit-trees? Thou wast made a dry tree in Adam, but now through the grace of Christ thou hast sprouted as a fruit-bearing tree. Gladly did the Lord Jesus receive it, and with heavenly condescension answered his Church: *I have come down,* he says, *into my garden, I have gathered myrrh with my ointments, I have eaten my bread with my honey, and I have drunk my wine with my milk.*

## 10.

Eat, he says, my brethren, and be drunken: *I have gathered myrrh with my ointments [n: (Psalm. LXXIX, 9)]*. What is this vintage? Know the vineyard, and ye shall recognize the vintage. *A vineyard,* he says, *thou hast brought out of Egypt,* that is, the people of God. Ye are the vineyard, ye are the vintage: as a vineyard ye were planted, as a vintage ye have given fruit. *I have gathered myrrh with [1920B] my ointments,* that is, unto the odor which ye have received.

## 11.

I have eaten my bread with my honey. Thou seest that in this bread there is no bitterness, but that all is sweetness. *I have drunk my wine with my milk,* dost thou see that such is the gladness, which is polluted by the filth of no sin? For as often as thou drinkest, thou receivest remission of sins, and art made drunken with the spirit. Whence the Apostle also says: *Be not drunk with wine . . . . . but be filled with the Holy Spirit [n: (Ephes. V, 18)]*. For he who is made drunken with wine staggers and totters: he who is made drunken with the spirit is rooted in Christ, and therefore that drunkenness is excellent which works soberness of mind.

*1592.* 12. *Eat,* he says, *my friends, and drink, and be drunken.* A good drunkenness, which makes a certain ecstasy of the mind toward better and pleasant [1920C] things, so that our mind, forgetful of its anxieties, may be gladdened with the wine of pleasantness. Good is the drunkenness of the spiritual table; in short, *how excellent is the inebriating cup [n: (Psalm. XXII, 5)]*! [var: Vulg. *calix meus inebrians quam praeclarus est*, "how excellent is my inebriating chalice"] And elsewhere thou hast: *Make drunk her streams, multiply her generations [n: (Psalm. LXIV, 11)]*; [var: Vulg. *multiplica genimina ejus*, "multiply her fruits"] in that the drunkenness of the earth, when it has been poured in by a heavenly shower, is wont to raise up seeds and to multiply fruits. And so, when the Word, which came down from heaven like rain, has made drunken with divine preaching the veins of our earth, that is, of the soul and of the mind, the pursuits of divers virtues are aroused, and the fruit of faith and of chaste devotion grows up, and rightly is it said to him: *Thou hast visited the earth, and hast made it drunken [n: (Psalm. LXIV, 10)]*. For by the assumption of a body he visited, that he might heal the sick: he made drunken with spiritual grace, that he might soothe the anxious [1920D] with pleasantness.

## 13.

*I sleep, and my heart waketh.* There is also a working sleep of the saints, according to what is written: *I sleep, and my heart waketh.* And according as holy Jacob, sleeping, saw the divine mysteries which waking he had not seen; from heaven to the lands the air going before the saints, the Lord looking back, and promising the possession of that land. And so, sleeping in a short sleep, he obtained what afterwards he acquired with great labor, for his inheritance is the sleep of the saints, keeping holiday from all pleasures of the body. [1921A] from every disturbance of the mind, bringing tranquillity to the mind, placidity to the soul; that, as though loosed from the bond of the body, it may lift itself up and cleave to Christ. This is the sleep of the life of the saints.

## 14.

*The voice of my beloved knocking, open to me, my sister.* Even if thou sleep, if only Christ has known the devotion of thy soul, he comes, and knocks at her door, and says: *Open to me, my sister.* Well *sister,* because the marriage of the Word and of the soul is spiritual; for souls know not the covenants of wedlock, and the practice of bodily union, but are as the angels of God in heaven. *Open,* he says, *to me,* but close to strangers. Close to the age, close to the world. Neither go thou forth outside to those material things, nor, leaving thine own light, seek another's; because material light pours in a dark [1921B] gloom, so that the light of true glory is not seen. *Open therefore to me,* be not straitened, but enlarge thyself, and I will fill thee. And because, having traversed the world, I have found more of troubles and of offense, and have not easily had where I might rest; therefore do thou open, that in thee the son of man may lay his head, for whom there is no rest save upon the humble and the meek.



## 15.

*Open to me, my sister; arise, my neighbor, my dove, my perfect one.* [var: Vulg. has *amica mea* and *immaculata mea*, "my friend… my spotless one," and prints no *surge*] Neighbor by charity, a dove by simplicity, perfect by virtue. *For my head is full of dew;* for as the dew of heaven removes the drynesses of the night, so the dew of our Lord Jesus Christ has distilled the moisture of eternal life upon the nightly and worldly darkness. This is the head which knew not how to grow parched by the heat of the world, whence he says: *For if they do these things in the moist, [1921C] what shall they do in the dry [n: (Luc. XXIII, 31)]*? [var: Vulg. *in viridi ligno… quid fiet*, "in the green wood… what shall be done"] This head therefore bedews others, and for itself abounds. And well does the head of Christ abound; because thy head is Christ, who is always full, and is not drained dry by his own liberalities, nor fails through long-continued largess. Upon this head no iron comes up, which is the instrument of war, the badge of discord. Now look, and see of what sort that dew is, namely, not of common moisture.

## 16.

*But my locks are of the drops of the night.* [var: Vulg. *cincinni mei guttis noctium*, ablative and with "nights" plural] Do not, my neighbor, take these locks to be locks of bodily hairs: those are not ornaments, but crimes; the pandering of beauty, not the precepts of virtue. The Nazirite has other locks, upon which no iron comes up, which no one cuts away: locks not arranged with the curling-iron or ordered by art, but which shone forth curling with the manifold grace of gleaming virtues. [1921D] Learn in the history what manner of locks the Nazirite has, which, so long as Samson kept them untouched, he could never be overcome. He lost his locks, and lost the merit of his virtue [n: (Jud. XVI, 17)].

## 17.

[n: (Vers. 3.)] Therefore, when the voice of the Word has been heard, seek not how thou mayest put on the tunic which thou hadst put off by night; for it is shown, and frequently offered through spiritual wickedness, how — how, I say, thou mayest put it on: forget it and know it not; as though the Lord were already standing by, do thou, free of bodily bonds, rise up troubled, [1922A] and prepare the inward mind by prayers while thou risest; that from lowly things thou mayest strive toward the things above, and be zealous to open the doors of thy heart. While *1593* thou stretchest out thy hands to Christ, thy deeds shall breathe a faithful odor.

## 18.

*Open to me, my sister.* When thou desirest to meet one of the foremost men, dost thou not first draw near to his house: afterwards thou seekest to be informed and instructed, that thou mayest learn the mind of the master of the house: then, that thou mayest enter his house, thou entreatest that no one cast thee off and shut thee out. Knock thou therefore also at that heavenly palace; knock not with the hand of the body, but with a certain right hand of thy prayer. Not the hand of the body alone knocks; the voice knocks also, for it is written: *The voice of my brother knocks at the door.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 5:2 *Vox dilecti mei pulsantis*, "the voice of my beloved knocking"] We knock also with the finger. In short, [1922B] Thomas too deserved with his finger to open the door of the resurrection: and Jesus says to thee: *Put thy finger hither, and put it into my hands and my side, and be not unbelieving, but faithful [n: (Joan. XX, 27)]*. [var: Vulg. *et vide manus meas, et affer manum tuam, et mitte in latus meum*, "and see my hands, and bring thy hand, and put it into my side"] Knock therefore with thy finger, if thou canst not with the whole hand; knock at the door. Christ is the door, who says: *By me if any man shall enter in, he shall be saved [n: (Joan. X, 9)]*.

## 19.

When therefore thou hast knocked at this door, see how thou enterest; lest perchance, even having entered, thou be outside the sight of the king. Many enter palaces, and do not straightway see that king of the earth, but often keep watch, that at some time they may deserve to see him. Nor do they presume upon the opportunity of seeing him, but are presented at his bidding, and pour out a petition; that they may be received with good will, being careful of the first approaches of their speech; [1922C] that nothing stumble, that nothing give offense: how much more must God be entreated, that our prayer may enter the door of his mercy!

## 20.

*The voice of my brother knocks at the door.* Learn thou also, O bride of Christ, to bar thy door in the times of night; lest anyone easily find it lying open. The Bridegroom himself wills it to be shut when he knocks. Our door is our mouth; it ought to be opened to Christ almost alone, nor let it be opened unless the Word of God has first knocked. *The voice of my brother knocks at the door.* Hear him knocking, hear him desiring to come in.

## 21.

*[n: (Vers. 2.)] Open to me, my sister, my bride, my dove, my perfect one; for my head is full of dew, and my hair of the drops of the night.* Consider when God the Word most of all knocks at thy door: when [1922D] his head has been filled with the nightly dew. For he deigns to visit those set in tribulation and temptations; lest anyone perchance succumb, overcome by hardships. His head therefore is filled with dew or with drops, when his body labors. Then therefore must we keep watch, lest, when the Bridegroom has come, he depart shut out. For if thou sleep, and thy heart do not keep watch, he departs before he knocks: if thy heart keep watch, he knocks, and demands that the door be opened to him.

## 22.

We have therefore a door of our soul; we have also gates, of which it is said: *Lift up your gates, ye princes, [1923A] and be ye lifted up, ye everlasting gates, and the King of glory shall enter in [n: (Psal. XXIII, 7)]*. There is therefore also a heaven in those in whom are the everlasting gates. If thou wilt raise up these gates of thy faith, the King of glory shall enter in to thee, bearing the triumph of his own passion. For justice has gates, for of these too we read it written, the Lord Jesus saying through his prophet: *Open to me the gates of justice [n: (Psal. CXVII, 19)]*. And below the prophet David says: *Praise the Lord, O Jerusalem; praise thy God, O Sion; for he hath strengthened the bolts of thy gates [n: (Psal. CXLVII, 12)]*.

## 23.

There is therefore a soul which has a door, there is one which has gates. To this door Christ comes, and knocks: he knocks also at the gates. Open therefore to him; he wills to enter, he wills to find the Bride keeping watch. Do not therefore [1923B] make delays for a good lover: he quickly withdraws; and thou by the sleep of thy body shalt be seen to have shut out him who knocks. For thou dost shut him out when thou art slothful, when lazy, when drowsy. By these bars Christ is shut out: if thou be chaste and sober, beware lest thou be negligent. He does Christ the greater injury who repels him at his coming.

## 24.

*Open to me, my sister, my neighbor:* he knocks at the door even when thou sleepest: if only, being roused, thou awake, or, being called, thou open the door of thy breast, he will enter in. But if thou flee the prophetic reading, if thou read not at home, if thou be unwilling to hear in the Church — art thou not like him who winks with averted gaze, that he may not see what he could see, and shuts his eyes lest he look, though the power of seeing is his: or as many in a rage have cast their hands upon their own eyes; so thou too at first turnest away [1923C] rather by a winking dissimulation than by an abrupt refusal. For when thou comest to the Church and assertest that thou art *1594* a Christian, thou seemest whole, thou openest the eyes with which thou couldst see: but while thou dissemblest to hear the things that are read, thou closest them lest thou see for thyself, even if to others thou seem to see. Thou castest also certain hands of faithlessness and intemperance upon the eyes of thy soul, and bringest upon thy heart a blindness (which is the graver thing) that is voluntary; so that seeing thou seest not, hearing thou hearest not.

## 25.

Dost thou think that thou art alone when thou committest fornication, and dost not remember that the eyes of the Lord behold the round earth? Dost thou not hear him who says: *Behold, the hour comes that ye be scattered every man to his own, and leave me alone: but I am not alone, for the Father is with me [n: (Joan. XVI, 32)]*. The Father therefore is present, the Son of God also is present, the ministers are present, [1923D] the Cherubim and Seraphim are present, who say. *Holy, holy, holy, the earth is full of thy majesty [n: (Esai. VI)]*. [var: Vulg. *plena est omnis terra gloria ejus*, "the whole earth is full of his glory"] For the world is full of virtues, because it is full of wickednesses: the round earth is full of remedies, because it is full of snares.

## 26.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on?* [var: Vulg. *Expoliavi me tunica mea: quomodo induar illa?*, passive, "how shall I be clothed with it?"] For indeed in this night of the world the garment of bodily life must first be put off by thee; for the Lord put off flesh from himself, that for thee he might triumph over the dominations and powers of this world. *How shall I put it on?* See, O soul devoted to God, what she says. [1924A] So has she put off the acts of the body and earthly manners, that she knows not how, even if she should wish it, she could put them on again. *How shall I put it on?* That is: with what shame, with what modesty, with what memory at the last? For the habit of good has lost the use of the old depravity.

## 27.

The soul, hearing *Open to me,* having a head full of dew — that is, of worldly temptations — suddenly troubled, and as one about to rise who was bidden to rise, says, while she gave off the scent of aloes and myrrh, the badge of burial: *I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on: I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?* For she fears lest she rise again into temptations, lest she return again into fault and sin, and begin to defile her goings-out and the advances of her virtues with earthly footprints. [1924B]



## 28.

Assuredly in this way also she declares the perfection of her virtue, in that she has deserved so great a charity of Christ; that he comes to her, and knocks at her door, and comes with the Father, and sups with that same soul, and she with him, as John the apostle said in the Apocalypse [n: (Apoc. III, 20)]. For indeed, when she had heard in the passages above: *Be here from Lebanon;* [var: Vulg. *Veni de Libano, sponsa, veni de Libano*, "Come from Lebanon, my bride, come from Lebanon"] and when she perceived that she could not be present to Christ in the flesh, but was then present if she were present in the spirit, conforming herself to his will, that she too might be conformed to the image of Christ; now she does not feel the spoils of the flesh, now, as it were a spirit stripping itself of the conjunction of the body, now as one forgetful, and one who, if she wished, could not remember that coupling, she says: *I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on?* For I have put off that garment of skins which Adam and Eve received after [1924C] their fault, the tunic of corruption, the tunic of the passions; *How shall I put it on?* She does not ask to put it on: but so signifies it cast away, that now it could not be a garment for her.

## 29.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?* That is: I have washed my footsteps, while I was going out, and lifting myself from the fellowship of the body, from that connection and familiarity of the carnal embrace: how shall I defile them, so as to return into the enclosure of the body and that dark prison of its passions? So do we bury the passions, if we do not loose the cross of this body; if we do not write again, upon the cross of Christ, the bond of sin which has been blotted out; if we do not put on the garment of the old man which we have put off. For it is written in the Canticles: *I have put off my tunic, [1924D] how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?* The members of the body therefore have been mortified: why do its vices sprout up? Therefore the law did not prevail, because it did not mortify the flesh: therefore it passed by like a shadow, because it did not discolor: and therefore it overshadowed us from the sun of justice, because it heaped up crimes.

## 30.

Sometimes to be naked is a sign of virtue, because it is written: *I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?* What tunic therefore she has put off, and how she has not found in what manner [1925A] to put it on, let us disclose from the Scriptures if we can. For there is a certain bodily tunic, there are certain woven veils of desires; and therefore it is sometimes better to be naked in body than veiled in heart. Whence Paul too admonishes us that it is *1595* much better to be stripped than to be clothed over, saying elsewhere: *Stripping yourselves of the old man with his acts, put on the new, who is renewed unto knowledge, according to the image of him who created him.* [var: Vulg. *et induentes novum*, a participle, "and putting on the new"] Therefore she who has been stripped, and has her feet washed, knows not how she may not be defiled; for by grace she forgets what she had drawn in by nature.

## 31.

*I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?* Thou hast learned in the Gospel that to wash the feet is a mystery of faith, a badge of humility, according to what is written: [1925B] *If I, the Lord and master, have washed your feet, how much more ought you to wash one another's feet [n: (Joan. XIII, 14)]*! [var: Vulg. *et vos debetis alter alterius lavare pedes*, "you also ought to wash one another's feet"] But this pertains to humility. As to the mystery, however, he ought to wash his feet who wishes to have part with Christ: *For if I shall not wash,* he says, *thy feet, thou shalt not have part with me [n: (Ibid. 8)]*. [var: Vulg. *Si non lavero te*, "If I wash thee not"] When this is said to Peter, what is judged of us? He therefore who has washed his feet has no need to wash again; and therefore let him beware lest he defile them.

## 32.

And well does holy Church say: *I have washed my feet;* she does not say, how shall I wash them again, but *how shall I defile them again?* As one forgetful of the old stain, forgetful of the contagion, she admonishes how in a bodily ministry we ought to wash away the spiritual trace of our acts. And so, when once thou hast washed thy feet [1925C] with the watering of the eternal fountain, and hast cleansed them by the sacrament of the mystery, beware again of the filth of bodily desire, lest thy miry acts be defiled with earthly squalor. These are the feet which David washed in the spirit, who teaches thee how thou canst not defile them, saying: *Our feet were standing in thy courts, O Jerusalem [n: (Psal. CXXI, 2)]*. Assuredly understand here the feet not of the body but of the mind; for how should an earthly man have the feet of the body in heaven? Jerusalem, as Paul has taught thee, is in heaven: and the same has taught thee how thou canst stand in heaven, when he says: *But our conversation is in heaven [n: (Philip. III, 20)]*. The conversation of manners, the conversation of deeds, the conversation of faith.

## 33.

*I have done judgment and justice, deliver me not [1925D] to them that slander me.* Where justice is, there is mercy. Mercy frees from sin. How therefore am I delivered to sinners? Like to this is that in the Canticles: *I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?* I have put off the tunic of the sinner, and the veil of the earthly man; why am I adjudged as though a sinner and an earthly man? I have washed my feet, that no lot of sin might cleave to my footstep; why dost thou give power over me to them that offend?

## 34.

Jeremiah in his Lamentations says: *Thy iniquity hath failed, [1926A] O daughter of Sion; he shall no more add to drive thee out: he hath visited thine iniquities, O daughter of Edom, he hath revealed upon thy sins [n: (Thren. IV, 21)]*. [var: Vulg. *Completa est iniquitas tua… discooperuit peccata tua*, "Thy iniquity is accomplished… he hath uncovered thy sins"] Dost thou observe that iniquity could not fail without God's visitation, nor the correction be full, save through the grace of the Lord and Savior? But how iniquity fails, hear the Church saying: *I have put off my tunic, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?* The garment of the old man, therefore, woven through with the vices of error, laid down in the regeneration of the laver, she knows not how she could put on; for by the zeal of correction a forgetfulness of sins had grown up in her. So great is the force of a completed amendment, that she returns to a certain age of spiritual childhood, which knows not the ways of error; even if she wished, she could not admit a crime, because use has grown unaccustomed [1926B] to knowing how to sin.

## 35.

*[n: (Vers. 4.)] My brother put his hand in from the lookout, and my belly was troubled over him. I myself rose up to open to my brother. My hands dropped myrrh, my fingers were full of myrrh.* [var: Vulg. *Dilectus meus misit manum suam per foramen, et venter meus intremuit ad tactum ejus*, "My beloved put his hand through the opening, and my belly trembled at his touch"] First therefore he puts in his hand as though from a lookout, when God is estimated from his works, whence he says: *If ye believe not me, believe the works [n: (Joan. X, 38)]*. [var: Vulg. *et si mihi non vultis credere*, "and if you will not believe me"] Then love is increased, and, conceived in the innermost bowels, grows up. Thereupon, his seeds having been poured into the intelligible womb in which is the receptacle of the Word, our soul desires to drink in his fullness dwelling bodily; for the womb of women is not troubled, save the wombs of those who have been heavy in the belly. The soul therefore rises to open to the Word of God, but while she spreads herself out and [1926C] opens, that she may mortify the works of this world by the reception of the Word, like him who says: *We carry about the mortification of the Lord Jesus in our body [n: (II Cor. IV, 19)]*. Therefore, while she opens, the Bridegroom passes by; for he wills always to be sought, and more frequently to be found. And if he shall have found the door shut, he knocks; and if through delay *1596* he has been shut out, he withdraws; but quickly he returns, to him he knocks again, that at least afterwards he may find his Bride prepared.

## 36.

It can indeed be taken thus also, *My brother passed by:* as we read that he penetrated the inmost marrow of the beloved, in the manner in which it was said to Mary: *And a sword shall pierce through thy own soul, that the thoughts of many hearts may be revealed [n: (Luc. II, 35)]*. [var: Vulg. *ex multis cordibus*, "out of many hearts"] In short, the Bride added that her soul had gone out at his word, which happens when the soul is a pilgrim from [1926D] the body and is present to God. Hearing these things the Bridegroom sent his operation, the Word, as through a cave, not yet face to face: he sent it as it were a hand, *And my belly,* she says, *was troubled over him.* Whosoever so lives can say:

## 37.

*My brother put his hand through the lookout, and my belly was troubled at him: I rose to open to my brother.* It is good that at the coming of the Lord the inward parts be troubled. If Mary was troubled at the coming of the angel, how much more are we troubled at the coming [1927A] of Christ! For when the things of God flow in, bodily affection goes into exile, and that habit of the outward man grows out of use. Do thou also be troubled, do thou make haste. Those men are bidden to eat the lamb in haste. Rise, open: Christ is at the door, he knocks at the porches of thy house: if thou shalt open, he will enter in, and will enter in with the Father. Nor only when he has entered does he leave a reward: but even before he enters he sends the reward on ahead. Still the soul is troubled, still she gropes along the walls of her house, still she seeks the door where Christ is, still she looses the bond of the flesh and the enclosure of the body, still Christ knocks outside.



## 38.

What is our window, but that through which we see the works of Christ, namely the eye of the soul and the gaze of the mind? And therefore, O virgin, let Christ come in through thy window, [1927B] let Christ put in his hand: let the love of the Word come to thee through the window, not the love of the body. If therefore the Word of God puts in his hand through thy window, see how thou preparest thy windows, see how thou oughtest to wipe them clean from every dust of offenses. Let the windows of a virgin have nothing foul, nothing adulterate. Far off be the antimony and the other trifles of an affected comeliness, far off the enticements of an adulterate love. Like to this is the closing of the ears, on which no burdens are to be hung, no wounds to be fixed, but there is one adornment: to hear what profits.

## 39.

*[n: (Vers. 6.)] I opened,* she says, *to my brother; my brother passed by.* How he passed by: that is, he penetrated the inward parts of the mind, as it was said to Mary: *And a sword shall pierce through thy own soul.* For the living Word of God, [1927C] like a sharp sword, penetrating the bars of bodily thoughts, searches out the inward parts of the heart. Set therefore in thy chamber and in the time of night, meditate always upon Christ, and hope for his coming at every moment. If he seems to thee to delay, rise up. He seems to delay when thou sleepest long: he seems to delay when thou art idle from prayer: he seems to delay when thou dost not lift up thy voice in psalms. Dedicate to Christ the firstfruits of thy watchings, offer to Christ the firstfruits of thy acts.

## 40.

*[n: (Vers. 5.)] I myself rose up to open to my brother; my hands dropped myrrh, my fingers are full of myrrh upon the hands of the fastening.* [var: Vulg. *digiti mei pleni myrrha probatissima*, "my fingers full of the choicest myrrh"] Let us consider what this signifies. First, as I have said, God the Word is seen in his own works as through a cave, not fully [1927D] and perfectly: then love is increased and the conception grows, and out of his seeds, which the soul has received in a certain intelligible womb, she desires to see the whole fullness of his divinity dwelling in him bodily, as we read. She rose that she might see that Word of God more closely, and in this very thing her advance is signified, that she rose by vigor and strength; for the presence of the Word drew forth the strength of the soul, as the presence of Mary, when she was heavy with the womb, instructed John set within the womb: so far that he leapt out of the womb and exulted, recognizing the presence of the Lord. [1928A]

## 41.

She rose to open, and her works and deeds were mortified to the world. For such ought the soul to be which is to receive the Word: that she die to the world, and be buried together in Christ; for thus is Christ found, and such a lodging does he seek for himself. Then the very ministries of her operations, that is, the hands and the fingers by which Christ is grasped, are mortified: which fingers we may reckon as the eminent works *1597* of our deeds. And so, as it were out of her own embrace, when she was already stretching out her intelligible hands and fingers to grasp the Word, the devout soul says that it passed by her, yet not that it had as yet passed through. And this advance it has, when the Word of God passes by and passes through the soul, because it is written: *And a sword shall pierce through thy own soul, that the thoughts of many [1928B] hearts may be revealed [n: (Luc. II, 35)]*. Hence there is as yet a passing by, not a passing through; as in Mary, in the later things, there is perhaps a passing through, where the Lord Jesus is set as a seal in the midst of her.

## 42.

In short, straightway there is another advance of the Word passing through, in that the soul went out at his Word; that is, following his Word she went out of the body, lifting herself out of its habitation, and making herself a pilgrim to it, that she might be present to God, and be a fellow-citizen of the saints. For we cannot at once be of the household both of the flesh and of God. Therefore in this place the soul is signified to go out, as I have said, when she withdraws herself from the pleasures of the body. In short, it is written: *Go out of Babylon, fleeing from the Chaldeans [n: (Esai. XLVIII, 20)]*. [var: Vulg. *Egredimini de Babylone, fugite a Chaldaeis*, both plural imperatives] Not indeed that the Hebrew should flee the regions of Babylon, but its manners, as he is admonished by the prophetic [1928C] address: since indeed there are those who are Hebrews in Babylon, and teach by their manners that they have gone out of Babylon. For those too of whom the prophet says that *they sat upon the rivers of Babylon [n: (Ps. CXXXVI, 1)]* sat indeed in the region of Babylon, but were not in its vices and confusion. For how were they in that confusion of vices, who wept, doing penance, because they had fallen away from the ark of devotion and faith, and from the merits of their fathers' virtue? But the soul which goes out at the word seeks the Word; whence she also, while she was seeking, fell in with the guards who go about the city.

*[n: (Vers. 7.)] The guards who go about the city found me, they struck me, and wounded me, and the guards of the walls took away my cloak from me.* Well indeed did she come as a bride with a cloak, with which she might veil [1928D] her head when the bridegroom should meet her; as Rebecca, who, having learned that Isaac was coming to meet her, came down from the camel and covered herself with a cloak: so this soul too sent before her the badges of the nuptial garment, lest perchance she be cast out as not having a wedding garment; or that she might veil her head because of the angels. But they struck her, that she might be the more tested; for souls are exercised by temptations. They took her cloak from her, seeking whether she brought the true beauty of naked virtue, or because one ought to enter that heavenly city without a covering, bringing with him no coverings [1929A] of paint. There are also those who search out whether any soul carries with her the spoils of carnal enticement and bodily concupiscence. She is stripped of her cloak when her conscience is made manifest. But there is one who is well stripped, to whom it is permitted to imitate him who says: *For the prince of this world comes, and in me he finds nothing [n: (Joan. XIV, 30)]*; [var: Vulg. *et in me non habet quidquam*, "and in me he hath not anything"] for assuredly he found nothing in him alone who did no sin.

## 44.

Blessed also is she in whom he finds nothing grievous or manifold, but finds in her the garment of faith and the discipline of wisdom. And so without loss to herself (because even if one should wish it, he cannot take away true wisdom), though the adversary clamor; yet where the true integrity of a harmless conversation shines out, she has passed the guards, and, mingled with the daughters of that heavenly city, seeks the Word; [1929B] and by seeking she stirs up his charity within herself, and learns where she may seek the Word. It is not only an injury not to have found him whom thou seekest, but very often to have sought where one ought not is itself a wound: to have sought in the houses of men who falsely take to themselves the name of teachers, to have sought more boldly than modestly. Therefore let us beware, by her example, lest the guards who go about the city find us.

## 45.

*They found me,* she says, *the guards who go about the city: they struck and wounded me, and the guards of the walls took away my cloak from me.* Not in herself, O daughters, but in us is the Church wounded. Let us therefore beware lest our fall become the Church's wound, lest anyone take from us the cloak, that is, the garment of prudence, the badge of patience, by which the ambition of a softer [1929C] garment is put off: for *They who are clothed in softer garments are in the houses of kings [n: (Matth. XI, 8)]*. [var: Vulg. *qui mollibus vestiuntur*, "they who are clothed in soft things"] But to us Christ has given the cloak with which he himself clothed his apostles and his own body, which in short he bids thee give, if any man asks thy tunic, that thou leave him the cloak also [n: (Matth. V, 40)]; that is, that thou hand over the badge of thy philosophy, and clothe him about, as with the garment of thy prudence, *1598* who before was naked.

## 46.

There therefore, O daughters, let us seek Christ where the Church seeks him, on the mountains of good odor, which by the sublimity of their lofty deeds breathe out the sweet odor of life according to the summits of their merits. She who seeks Christ ought not to be common, ought not to be in the market or in the streets, querulous of voice, slippery of step, easy of approach, cheap of aspect. The Apostle denies thee earthly fellowship, and teaches that beyond the bounds, almost, of nature [1929D] we must fly up to heaven on spiritual wings. *Mind the things that are above,* he says, *not the things that are upon the earth [n: (Colos. III, 2)]*. But because this was impossible for those shut up in this enclosure, as it were, of the body, and because when bodies are dead the soul is said to fly back to the things above, being bound by a certain law of our nature while we live, therefore he added: *For you are dead, and your life is hidden with Christ in God [n: (Ibid., 3)]*. If it is hidden with Christ in God, let it not appear to the world, for Christ is dead to the world, and lives to God. See now how Christ loves to be desired, and loves not [1930A] idle talk. That virgin opened her doors to the Word of God: but *he passed by,* she says, *and my soul went out at his word [n: (Vers. 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *Anima mea liquefacta est, ut locutus est*, "My soul melted when he spoke"] She went out from the world, she went out from the age, she remained in Christ. *I sought him,* she says, *and I found him not.* For Christ loves to be sought long.



## 47.

The guards of the walls found her. Perhaps there are also other guards, whom we ought rather to understand. For there is a city which does not have the gates of its walls shut, of which it is said: *And the gates thereof shall not be shut by day [n: (Apoc. XXI, 25)]*; for night shall be no more in it, the nations shall bring glory and honor into it. That therefore is the city Jerusalem which is in heaven, within which thou art now kept as one perfect and unspotted; for no common thing enters into it. Chastity which is written in the book of life is not common, modesty [1930B] is not common.

## 48.

If therefore we have found the city, let us enter into it, let us see its light, let us see the walls, let us see the foundations of the walls, let us see also the guards of the walls. But how shall we enter into it? In this city is life, and there is one way which leads to life. The way therefore is Christ; let us therefore follow Christ. But that city is itself in heaven; how then shall we ascend to heaven? The Evangelist teaches, who says: *And he led me forth in the spirit into a mountain great and high, and showed me the holy city Jerusalem coming down from heaven [n: (Apoc. 21, 10)]*. [var: Vulg. *sustulit me in spiritu*, "he took me up in the spirit"] Let us therefore ascend in the spirit, because the flesh cannot ascend to it. Let us ascend meanwhile into heaven, that afterwards it may come down from heaven to us: in which the light is like [1930C] a most precious stone, and as of the jasper stone and of crystal it has a wall great and high.

## 49.

Thou hast learned of the light, thou hast learned of the wall; learn of the gates, learn of the guards. *It has,* he says, *twelve gates, and in the gates twelve angels, upon which the names of the twelve tribes of the children of Israel are written above [n: (Ibid., 12)]*. In the gates the names of the patriarchs are contained, in the wall those of the apostles; for the foundations of the city are the apostles, and the corner-stone is Christ, in whom the whole structure rises up. God is without, God is within, God is everywhere: *For the city has,* he says, *the majesty of God [n: (Ibid., 11)]*. [var: Vulg. *habentem claritatem Dei*, "having the brightness of God"] Therefore you also, holy virgins, and whosoever of you are just, and bear the unspotted chastity of the soul, are fellow-citizens of the saints and of the household of God. But then shall you possess that nobility of your fatherland, if you seek Christ within the enclosures of that city, having entered [1930D] by faith and by precious acts, made bright by the light of the patriarchs, founded upon the apostles, dwelling among the angels.

## 50.

How therefore are those guards angels, who take away the cloak of a chaste soul? The cloak of virgins is one thing, that of a girl who haunts the streets another. She who seeks Christ in the market has lost even the cloak she had. For prudence is possessed not in the market, not in the streets, but in the Church. And perhaps, that we may come into favor with them also, and may teach that God is merciful to all — since they too [1931A] at some time find Christ, if only they seek continually — this cloak is the garment of the body. Therefore he who has sought Christ in his chamber, if only he has so sought as he who said: *So have I been mindful of thee upon my bed [n: (Ps. LXII, 7)]*; [var: Vulg. *Si memor fui tui*, "If I have been mindful of thee"] if he has sought him in the nights, according to what is written: *In the nights lift up your hands to the holy places [n: (Ps. CXXXII, 2)]*: if he has sought him in the city, in the market and in the streets, in the city of our God: in the market perhaps *1599* in which that judge of divine right sits, in the streets from which were gathered those who came together to the Lord's supper — he can, while he seeks long, meet with the angels who keep the city of God. Nay more, from the heavenly nature of the guards we can understand the city to be heavenly, and the market a heavenly market of everlasting justice, and the streets not cheap streets, but perhaps those in which [1931B] that fountain was wont to overflow, of which it is written: *Let thy waters overflow from thy fountain, and let thy waters be poured out in thy streets [n: (Prov. V, 16)]*. [var: Vulg. *Deriventur fontes tui foras, et in plateis aquas tuas divide*, "Let thy fountains be conveyed abroad, and divide thy waters in the streets"] He therefore who so seeks Christ comes to the angels.

## 51.

But if it is by good merits that one comes to the angels, why is he who has come wounded? There is also a good sword, and the wound of that sword is good. The Word of God wounds, but does not ulcerate. There is a wound of good love, there are wounds of charity; and therefore she said: *I am of wounded charity [n: (Cant. II, 5)]*. [var: Vulg. *quia amore langueo*, "for I languish with love"] She who is perfect is wounded with charity. Good therefore are the wounds of the Word, good are the wounds of the lover: *For more useful are the wounds of a friend than the willing kisses of an enemy [n: (Prov. XXVII, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. *Meliora sunt vulnera diligentis quam fraudulenta oscula odientis*, "Better are the wounds of one that loves than the deceitful kisses of one that hates"] Of wounded charity is Rebecca, who, leaving her parents, journeyed to her bridegroom. Of wounded charity [1931C] is Rachel, who was jealous of her sister and loved her husband; for she, still barren, envied her sister in that she abounded in sons, because she bore the type of the Church, to whom it is said: *Rejoice, thou barren that bearest not, break forth and cry, thou that travailest not [n: (Esai. 54, 1)]*. [var: Vulg. *Lauda, sterilis, quae non paris; decanta laudem, et hinni, quae non pariebas*, "Praise, O thou barren that bearest not; sing forth praise and cry out, thou that didst not bear"]

## 52.

The guards therefore found her and wounded her, and took her cloak from her: that is, they carried off the wrapping of bodily action, that the naked simplicity of the mind might seek Christ; because no one clothed with the garment of philosophy, that is, in the habit of worldly wisdom, can see Christ. And well is the garment of philosophy taken from her, lest anyone despoil her through philosophy. Well is the cloak taken from her who draws near to Christ, that, being about to see God, she may walk with a clean heart: for *Blessed are the clean of heart, for [1931D] they shall see God [n: (Matth. V, 8)]*. In short, when she had cleansed her heart, she found the Word, she saw God. Seek him therefore, O virgin — nay rather, let us all seek him; for the soul has no sex. But perhaps it received a feminine name for this reason, that a more violent heat of the body drives it: while it soothes the onset of the flesh and of its own love by a certain soft and gentle reasoning. Therefore by prayers and supplications we ought to invite God, that he may deign to breathe upon us like a good south wind. And let him breathe upon us the breeze of the heavenly Word: which is wont to stir the fruit-bearing trees not with a heavy wind, but with a light breathing and a soft blast. [1932A]

## 53.

*They took away my cloak from me.* A good garment is the eternity of life; this cloak of the Bride the guards of the walls wished to take away, of which the first man had been stripped; but the soul devoted to God, by holding fast the long-sought and not letting go her beloved, clothed herself with the precious wrapping of divine charity. Blessed therefore are they who are clothed with such a cloak, and have deserved a garment of this kind by the observance of the law; because they have not been forgetful of the law, but have done the things that were of the law.

## 54.

*[n: (Vers. 8.)] I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my beloved, that you announce to him that I languish with love.* [var: Vulg. *ut nuntietis ei*, "that you tell him"] But what is this languor of the soul? The moral sense makes it needful that we remember the old history, which is of holy Moses, who asked God to show himself to him, and that he might see him face to face. Assuredly [1932B] the holy seer of the Lord knew that he could not see the invisible God face to face. But holy devotion oversteps measure, and he thought that even this was possible to God, that he should cause that which is incorporeal to be comprehended by bodily eyes. This error is not blameworthy, but rather a pleasing and insatiable desire, in that he longed to hold his Lord as it were with the hand, and to see him with the gaze of the eyes. For he knew that man had been made to the image and likeness of God; for when, chosen by the Lord God to free the people, he was filled with the spirit of wisdom, he had seen that angel, and his face in glory; in short, he was made afraid by his shining light, and saw the bush burning and not burnt up. By sight he took in the brightness, and marvelling at it he drew near; provoked by desire and by grace, that he might more diligently [1932C] look upon that brightness in the bush. And so, if he received so great an ardor and underwent so great a desire when he had seen the angel in the flame of fire out of the bush, that, though seized with dread he dared not look, yet he wished to look: how much more did he desire to see the countenance of the Lord bodily, saying within himself: That countenance full of light, *1600* full of grace, full of virtue, full of divinity — what shall I say it is? I can say or feel no more of God; for when a man has finished, then he begins; and when he has made an end, then shall he be at a loss, as it is written, because *the eternal majesty of God is incomprehensible [n: (Eccles. XVIII, 6)]*.



## 55.

Vain indeed was the effect of his petition, but the affection of the servant was worthy of approval; because, having advanced by devotion beyond his own nature, he estimated from the Lord's angels [1932D] how much more he might desire of the Lord's countenance, whereby he longed to fill up his desire. He knew that in man himself there would be after death one glory and another brightness: *For as star differs from star in brightness, so also is the resurrection of the dead; which, even if it is sown in corruption, yet shall rise in incorruption, shall rise in glory, shall rise in power, shall rise a spiritual body [n: (I Cor. XV, 41-44)].* He therefore who could know these things rightly presumed to desire to see the countenance of the Lord, since he would see it when the death of the body had been undergone. Such a form assuredly had he taken on, and such did the Lord already approve, that the heavenly minister and executor of the oracle stood no distance from the angels. And therefore, [1933A] since he knew *That the angels of the little ones daily see the face of the Father who is in heaven [n: (Matth. XVIII, 10)]*, [var: Vulg. *angeli eorum in caelis semper vident faciem Patris mei*, "their angels in heaven always see the face of my Father"] he thought that he could and ought already to see it, as one forgetful of the body and having laid the flesh aside; like him who, being still set in the body, when he was caught up into paradise, said that he knew no further whether he had been caught up to the third heaven in the body or outside the body [n: (II Cor. XII, 3)]. For the saints, even when they bear a body, are nevertheless not in the flesh but in the spirit; since they who are in carnal desire and conversation cannot please God. But the saints are not in the flesh by their conversation, but in heaven.

## 56.

From these things therefore let us comprehend the affection of the languishing Bride. She desired to see the countenance of God as one innocent, as it were with a certain countenance of conscience, and she longed to open [1933B] to him the face of her inward mind, and to be more fully known. Impatient love, knocking day and night at harlots' doors, if the desires of possessing are put off longer, itself faints in the waiting while it hopes: in which assuredly there is not an end of love, but an increase. And whatever is desirable, if it does not fall to him who desires it, he faints toward it, and he who desires lays down, as it were, his very soul; yet if a nearer hope rises up, the hope close at hand gives strength; but if there be an absence of the beloved, by that very thing whereby she desires him absent, she who has longed suffers a fainting of her own soul. And so, the further off that is which is desired, the more does he faint who desires it. This therefore is to faint: that every man passes over with all his zeal into that which he loves; he thinks it, he cleaves to it, he sounds it forth, that which he has received to be loved; into it he is poured over by a certain [1933C] fainting of the soul. As if a mother should await the presence of a son, in the way that the wife of Tobias awaited her travelling son; fainting from desire, and set in straits, she seemed as one undone [n: (Tob. X, 3 et seq.)]. For what else do her words signify but a certain fainting? But the more the affection is wearied, the more abundantly does love grow; and the longer he who desires is absent, the more do the desires of her who waits take fire with a certain greater force of love. The flesh faints, but the desire is nourished and increased.

## 57.

And so the holy soul knows not how to desire anything other than the Bridegroom, who is Christ Jesus: him she longs for, him she desires, toward him she strains with all her powers, him she cherishes in the bosom of her mind, to him she opens and pours out herself, and this alone she fears, that she might lose him. [1933D] And so, the greater the desire with which the soul has been roused, longing to cleave to God her Savior, so much the more does she faint. Therefore this fainting works indeed a diminution of frailty, but a taking-on of strength. She longed, she desired, she fainted, she was dissolved in her whole affection, that she might become wholly his whom she desired, as David himself also says: *I pour out my prayer in his sight, and I declare my tribulation before him, in the failing of my spirit [1934A] from me [n: (Ps. CXLI, 3 et 4)]*. For his spirit failed; nay rather, the spirit failed from him who denies himself, that he may cleave to Christ. And so, as one impatient of love, she ran about, and sought everywhere the Word of God, because she was wounded, because she was naked; and she sought the support of someone by whom Christ might be asked to come. See her burning, see her desiring: *I adjure you,* she says, *O daughters of Jerusalem,* etc.; then, mingled with the daughters of that heavenly city, she seeks the Word, and by seeking and by contemplating his beauty, she stirs up his charity within herself. *1601*

## 58.

*[n: (Vers. 10.)] My brother,* she says, *is white and red, chosen out of thousands.* [var: Vulg. *Dilectus meus candidus et rubicundus*, "My beloved is white and ruddy"] For it is fitting, O virgin, that thou shouldst fully know whom thou lovest, and shouldst acknowledge in him every mystery both of his unbegotten divinity and of the incorporation [1934B] he assumed. He is therefore white by right, because he is the splendor of the Father: red, because he is the offspring of a virgin. The color of each nature shines and glows red in him. Yet remember that the badges of divinity in him are more ancient than the mysteries of the body; because he did not begin from the Virgin, but he who was came into the Virgin.

## 59.

*My brother is white and red.* White with eternal brightness, red with the appearance of human color, which he took on by the mystery of the incarnation. And rightly does that very thing which is red smell sweet; because the flesh of Christ is without sin, which the faithless, handling it, defiled their own hands: while the saints, venerating it, burned with the odor of piety.

## 60.

*Chosen out of thousands. Ten thousands,* says Jacob, *of Ephraim and thousands of Manasseh;* that is, let him have dominion over both the Jews [1934C] and the Gentiles, and out of both peoples acquire for himself the fullness of the Church. And therefore holy Jacob laid his right hand upon Ephraim; because thou readest, the Church saying in the Song of Songs: *My brother is white and red, chosen out of ten thousands.* [var: Vet. Lat. — Migne prints this same lemma as *ex millibus*, "out of thousands," at §§58–59 above, and *ex decem millibus* here; the plate is inconsistent with itself and is not harmonized] In short, they proclaimed even David — the author of the young Mary, from whose succession Christ was born by the Virgin's childbearing — in ten thousands: but Saul in thousands, though they ought rather to have set the favor of reverence toward the king first.

## 61.

*[n: (Vers. 11.)] His head is gold Cephas.* [var: Vulg. *aurum optimum*, "the finest gold"] Which signifies a steadfast and eminent wisdom. The body of Christ is the Church, and the head of this is as gold, because the wisdom of the saints is precious, that is, men just and prudent. [1934D]

## 62.

*His hairs are as fir trees, black as the raven.* [var: Vulg. *comae ejus sicut elatae palmarum, nigrae quasi corvus*, "his locks as the branches of palm trees, black as a crow"] Of this he said elsewhere, *Thy head of hair is as a flock of shorn ones.* Therefore a head of hair, because the power of all the senses is in the head: for *The eyes of the wise man are in his head [n: (Eccle. II, 14)]*. The deep prudence, therefore, of the teachers, which can reveal the things that are obscure and open the heights of the senses. And disputants of this kind are the hairs of the Church, like the young of the ravens to whom the Lord gives food: as he gave to holy Jacob, and fed him [1935A] from his youth. These others, and deep ones, the Lord feeds with heavenly mysteries in the richness of doctrine.

## 63.

*[n: (Vers. 12.)] His eyes* also *are as doves.* The eyes are men, that is to say, adorned with spiritual senses, who are keen for the seeing of mysteries, and ready for penetrating the secrets of divine Scripture, shining with rational milk, in whom there is no spotted confusion of guile, but the unspotted sincerity of a simple affection. Therefore he made mention of these doves as washed in milk amid an abundance of waters, saying:

## 64.

*His eyes are as doves upon the abundance of waters, washed in milk, of one sitting upon fullness,* [var: Vulg. *super rivulos aquarum, quae lacte sunt lotae, et resident juxta fluenta plenissima*, "upon the little brooks of waters, which are washed with milk, and sit beside the plentiful streams"] The Lord baptizes in milk, that is, in sincerity. And these are they who are truly baptized in milk, who believe without guile, and bring a pure faith, that they may put on an unspotted [1935B] grace. Therefore the Bride ascends white to Christ, because she has been baptized in milk; therefore the powers marvel at her, saying: *Who is this that comes up made white [n: (Cant. VIII, 7)]*? [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:5 *Quae est ista quae ascendit de deserto, deliciis affluens*, "Who is this that comes up from the desert, flowing with delights"] A little while before she was saying, *I am black [n: (Cant. I, )]*: now she is seen made white, and ascends to heaven, and, leaning upon the Word of God, now penetrates the heights.



## 65.

And not undeservedly is there an abundance of waters there where Christ is, from whom the human mind desires to be filled. These are the waters the stag thirsts for, which, when he has drunk them, he cannot thirst. These waters the Prophet thirsted for when he said: *My soul hath thirsted for thee [n: (Psalm. XLI, 3)]*. [var: Vulg. Ps 41:3 *Sitivit anima mea ad Deum fontem vivum*, "My soul hath thirsted after God, the living fountain"; the printed wording is that of Ps 62:2] Christ therefore sits upon the abundance of waters, and upon the fullness, and therefore he who baptizes in water says: *And we all have received of his fullness [n: (Joan I, 16)]*. Whence the eye of punishments also is not alien to the Church; because [1935C] even if John was baptizing in Aenon, he was baptizing near Salim, where there was an abundance of waters, and twelve fountains, and seventy palm trees. These fountains the Church has, that is, in the Old Testament the twelve patriarchs, in the New the twelve apostles; and therefore it was said: *In the churches bless ye the Lord God from the fountains of Israel [n: (Psalm. LXVII, 27)]*. With these fountains is he first washed over, whosoever attains the most sacred mysteries; for these fountains, flowing out from the eternal fountain, have streamed over the whole world. Where these fountains are, there is the ascent of souls. In short, they have interpreted Salim as "the very one ascending"; for he truly ascends who lays down his own sins. By this word, therefore, *1602* the use of a purifying sanctification is expressed.

## 66.

*[n: (Vers. 13.)] His cheeks are as phials of spice, [1935D] bringing forth perfumeries.* [var: Vulg. *Genae illius sicut areolae aromatum, consitae a pigmentariis*, "his cheeks are as beds of spices set by the perfumers"] Now we understand the cheeks and the rest to be the virtues of the soul, and the diversities of teachers: who either administer spiritual nourishment to the mind by a diligent stewardship, or bind the hearer, by the preaching of the Lord's cross, as it were with a certain line of the Word, or are most pleasing by the modesty of a bashful nature and by the bloom of youth. And even if they are called back from touch by shame, yet let the odor of Christ be fragrant in them: and as upon the cheeks the ointment comes down as it were from the priestly head, so also let the beauty of doctrine shine out in them.

## 67.

*His lips distilling full myrrh.* [var: Vulg. *Labia ejus lilia distillantia myrrham primam*, "his lips are lilies distilling the first myrrh"] For by [1936A] myrrh is declared the ointment of the passion and the grace of the resurrection, which pours the revived odor of life into the raised-up inward parts of the dead. Let the doors of thy lips therefore be shut, and let the porch of thy voice remain barred, then perhaps to be unbolted when thou hast heard the Word of God. Then shall myrrh sweat forth for thee, then shall the grace of baptism breathe upon thee; that thou mayest die together with the elements of the world, and rise again with Christ.

## 68.

*His lips distilling full myrrh.* But we think ourselves idle if we seem to devote ourselves to the word only: and we reckon of more account those who work than those who exercise the pursuit of knowing the truth; for very many say: Behold the man and his works — as though he who devotes himself to the word does not work; whereas [1936B] this is a work rather than the others. For if justice is a work, if temperance is a work, if fortitude is a work, then assuredly wisdom also is a work; for these four are held to be the principal virtues. For if Christ works according to that which is justice, assuredly he works according to that which is the Word. And he was working when he was in the beginning with the Father. In short, through him all things were made, that thou mayest know that he is the worker of all things, and that our work is Christ Jesus. For indeed, according to that which he is the Word, the Word to those who inquire, the Word is a great work. Whence, when Martha was hastening about the service, but Mary was hearing the word of the Lord, she who was hearing deserved to be set before her who was serving; for when Martha said: *Lord, hast thou no care that my sister hath left me to serve alone? Bid her therefore [1936C] that she help me,* the Lord answered and said: *Martha, Martha, Mary hath chosen a good part, which shall not be taken away from her [n: (Luc. VII, 40-42)]*. [var: Vulg. Luke 10:42 *Maria optimam partem elegit, quae non auferetur ab ea*, "Mary hath chosen the best part"] Thus it is determined by the authority of a divine sentence that to know the Word is a greater work than to serve.

## 69.

But perhaps someone may say that it has been said by the Apostle: *That the kingdom of God is not in speech, but in power [n: (I Cor. IV, 20)]*. I do not deny that it is written, but I recognize in what speech; namely, that which a puffed-up man has poured out, a speech which cannot profit the hearers, which is without the showing of the Spirit and of power. This speech Paul does not deign to recognize; for he wishes rather to commend the power of speech. In short, the Apostle wished his own speech to be such as came in weakness, that it might make others stronger: in fear [1936D] and trembling, that those who feared might dread nothing but the Lord Jesus; that, trembling, they might keep his peace and tranquillity. Hear then what manner of speech the Apostle had: *And my speech,* he says, *and my preaching were not in the persuasion of the words of human wisdom, but in the showing of the Spirit and of power [n: (II Cor. II, 4)]*; [var: Vulg. 1 Cor 2:4 *non in persuasibilibus humanae sapientiae verbis*, "not in the persuasive words of human wisdom"] because faith is established not in the forensic speech of wisdom, but in the showing of the Spirit, but in the power of God.

## 70.

Therefore in the speech of the saints there is power, but in that forensic and philosophical speech the vanity of the world. And that there is power in the speech of the saints, [1937A] let this prophet also teach thee, who says: *The Lord will give the word to them that preach the gospel with much power [n: (Psal. LXVII, 12)]*; that is, that they may be able to preach the Gospel with much power. It is proved therefore that in the preaching of the Gospel there is power, and the preaching of the Gospel is the speech of the saints; so there remains no doubt that in holy speech there is power. And this is the myrrh distilling from the lips of the Bridegroom, namely that by which vices are mortified.

## 71.

*[n: (Vers. 14.)] His hands are turned on the lathe, golden, full of hyacinths.* See what hands made man, and what a man they made! Him assuredly *Whom we have put on according to Christ, despoiling the old man with his acts, and putting on the new who is renewed unto the knowledge according to the image of him who created him: where there is no bondman and free, but all things and [1937B] Christ in all [n: (Colloss., III, 9-11)]*. We have put on Christ therefore, as it is said also elsewhere: *Ye have put on Christ [n: (Galat. III, 27)]*. We have received the Holy Spirit, who not only forgives our sins; *1603* but even makes us his priests, to forgive sins to others. Therefore the Prophet says: *Thou hast fashioned me and hast laid thy hand upon me [n: (Psal. CXXXVIII, 5)]*: [var: Vulg. *Tu formasti me*, "Thou hast formed me"] he fashioned by the clay, he laid on his hand by spiritual grace; although very many take this psalm to be spoken in the person of the Savior. Hear that the hand of the Lord is also called Spirit: Job cries out, *The divine Spirit who made me [n: (Job. XXXIII, 4)]*. [var: Vulg. *Spiritus Dei fecit me*, "The Spirit of God made me"] These therefore are the hands which prepared man, Christ and the Spirit.

## 72.

Therefore the Lord Jesus is also the author of our body, who first made man unto the image; [1937C] and afterwards fashioned him from clay, and willed to save what he had made, to save what he had molded, that he might make the whole man safe. These are the hands of God turned on the lathe, that is, perfect: golden on account of the wisdom which is Christ, full of hyacinths on account of the Holy Spirit, and the fullness of his graces.

## 73.

*[n: (Vers. 14.)] His belly is of ivory, set apart with sapphires.* The Apostle says, *And if he was crucified out of weakness* — behold the belly, that is, the weakness of the flesh — *yet he lives out of the power of God [n: (II Cor. XIII, 4)]*; behold the distinction of the sapphire. For the sapphire, in that it bears the appearance of a serene sky, signifies the works which are of the divinity. But [sic: *Onir*], on account of chastity and brightness and perpetuity, signifies that flesh of the Savior, of which the same Apostle says: *That although we have known Christ according to [1937D] the flesh, yet now we know him no more [n: (II Cor. V, 16)]*.

## 74.

*[n: (Vers. 15.)] His legs are pillars of marble, founded upon golden bases.* They signify that the Apostles are the pillars of the Church, who were founded in holy fear. For as Peter, James, John and Barnabas seemed to be pillars of the Church, so also whosoever has overcome this world becomes a pillar of God, whom he strengthens who says: *I have strengthened its pillars [n: (Psal. LXXIV, 4)]*. So too the golden base is fear full of discipline, [1938A] because it is the beginning of wisdom. In the fear therefore of the wise, apostolic preaching is made firm as a pillar of Christ upon a golden base. Accordingly, to speech and to the apostolic word the tribunal is the fear of the just man, and the golden base full of prudence: but the good image, as a likeness of the truth, is the speech of the saints.

## 75.

And see that the fear of the saints is as it were a golden base. Read Isaiah: see how many things he set fear under, that he might make fear blameless and good: *The Spirit,* he says, *of wisdom and understanding, the Spirit of counsel and of power, the Spirit of knowledge and of piety, the Spirit of the fear of the Lord [n: (Esai. XI, 2)]*; [var: Vulg. *spiritus consilii et fortitudinis, spiritus scientiae et pietatis*, "the spirit of counsel and of fortitude, the spirit of science and of godliness"] how many things did he set fear under, that it might have what it could follow! It is formed by wisdom, it is instructed by understanding, it is directed by counsel, it is made firm by power, it is ruled by knowledge, [1938B] it is adorned by piety. Take these things away from the fear of the Lord, and it is an irrational and unwise fear, one of those: *Without, fightings; within, fears [n: (II Cor. VII, 5)]*; with which Paul too would have been afflicted, had he not had the Lord consoling him.



## 76.

*[n: (Vers. 15.)] His appearance is as of Lebanon, choice as the cedars.* He dwells in the heights, and looks upon lowly things, because his appearance is like the cedar of Lebanon, which sets its foliage among the clouds and its root in the earth; for its beginning is from heaven, and its hinder parts in the earth have brought forth fruits nearest to heaven. This kind of tree never loses its greenness, in winter as in summer it feeds its foliage, nor is it changed with a different color: this tree alone the wind never despoils of its honor: this alone is never stripped of its old covering, nor clothed with a new bloom. So apostolic grace too knows no failing, and [1938C] blossoms with a perpetual comeliness of its own. The soul therefore knows not how to be corrupted, which is vigorous with flourishing members, and always upholds with patient greatness of heart the summits of justice and of the other virtues; and therefore it neither flows away nor fails, because in it there is nothing ruinous or slack, nothing shifting, nothing slippery, nothing which could be poured out of it by a fault of speech.

## 77.

*[n: (Vers. 16.)] His throat is most sweet, and he is wholly desire. The judgments of the Lord are true, justified in themselves, to be desired above gold and much precious stone [n: (Psal. XVIII, 10 et 11)]*. [var: Vulg. Cant 5:16 *et totus desiderabilis*, "and he is wholly desirable"] What is severe in others, in Christ is sweet, in Christ is pleasant; because he himself is pleasant: *In short, taste and see that the Lord is sweet [n: (Psal. XXIII, 9)]*. Sweet are the judgments to him who confesses, because he himself says: *I am he, I am he, who blot out [1938D] thine iniquities, and I will not be mindful of them: but be thou mindful, and let us be judged. Tell thine iniquities, that thou mayest be justified [n: (Esai. XLIII, 25 et 26)]*. [var: Vulg. *Ego sum, ego sum ipse qui deleo iniquitates tuas propter me, et peccatorum tuorum non recordabor. Reduc me in memoriam, et judicemur simul: narra si quid habes ut justificeris*] Sweet are the judgments to him who does penance, because he himself said: *There shall be joy over one sinner doing penance, than over ninety and nine just who need not penance [n: (Luc. XV, 7)]*. If therefore the judgments of the Lord are sweet, let us labor to receive the fruit of his *1604* sweetness. Wilt thou know how sweet the judgments of the Lord are? *He who believes in him is not judged [n: (Joan. III, 18)]*. Sweet therefore are the judgments to them that believe: but they who [1939A] believe not have been condemned not by the judgment of Christ, who came not to judge the world but to save and redeem it; rather they have undergone the judgment of their own impiety, who would not believe unto the remission of sins. For they cannot belong to the benefit of him whom they have refused to acknowledge. Therefore they who have not believed in him are seen to be unworthy of his judgment. For learn what his judgment is, since he himself says: *And this is the judgment, whereby the light came into this world [n: (Ibid. 19)]*. Sweet therefore is that which is light, sweet is the judgment which mercy goes before; for so it is written, *Mercy and judgment will I sing to thee, O Lord [n: (Psal. C, 1)]*. Sweet also and most pleasant is the throat, whence this mercy goes before and this judgment.

## 78.

*[n: (Vers. 16.)] And he is wholly desirable. Shapely in form [1939B] above the sons of men [n: (Psal. XLIV, 3)]*. He was indeed man according to the flesh, but beyond man, according to his divine working. In short, when he touched the leper, he was seen to be man: but beyond man, when he cleansed him.
