And when he wept for Lazarus dead, he wept as man: but he was above man, when he bade the dead man come forth with his feet bound. He was seen to be man, when he hung upon the cross: but above man, when, the tombs being unbarred, he raised the dead. Nor let the venom of Apollinaris flatter itself, because it is read thus: *And in appearance found as a man [n: (Philip. II, 7)]*; [var: Vulg. *et habitu inventus ut homo*, "and in habit found as a man"] for Jesus was not denied to be man, since elsewhere Paul himself says of him: *The mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus [n: (I Tim. II, 5)]*; but he was confirmed. For it is the usage and custom of Scripture so to signify, as we read also in the Gospel: [1939C] *And we saw his glory, the glory as of the only-begotten of the Father [n: (Joan. I)]*. As therefore he is called "as the only-begotten," and yet the only-begotten Son of God is not denied; so also he is called man, and the perfection of man in him is not denied.

## 79.

Since therefore he was in the form of a servant, humbled even unto death, he was nevertheless in the glory of God. What then did servitude harm him? But he is read to have been made a servant, because he is read to have been made of a Virgin, and created in the flesh; for every creature serves, as the prophet says: *For all things serve thee.* This is the glory of Christ, that he took servitude upon himself in his own body, that he might restore liberty to all: he bore our sins, that he might take away the sin of the world: he was made a servant, sin, a curse, that thou mightest cease to be the servant of sin, and that he might absolve thee [1939D] from the curse of the divine sentence. He therefore took upon himself thy curse: for *Cursed is every one that hangeth upon a tree:* he was made a curse upon the cross, that thou mightest be blessed in the kingdom of God: he was dishonored and cheapened, and accounted as nothing: he said, *I have labored in vain;* through whom Paul deserved to say, *I have not labored in vain [n: (Philip. II, 16)]*; that he might confer upon his little servants the fruit of good work, and the glory of evangelical preaching, whereby all men might be released from the burden of labor.

## 80.

*[n: (Vers. 17.)] Such is my beloved, and my friend, O daughters of Jerusalem.* So the Church, or the [1940A] holy soul, mingled with the daughters of that heavenly city, seeks the Word, and by seeking stirs up his charity within herself: while they indeed ask and say: *Whither is thy beloved gone, O most beautiful of women?* Whither has thy beloved turned aside, that we may seek him with thee?

## CAPUT SEXTUM.



## 1.

*[n: (Vers. 1.)] My beloved,* she says, *is gone down into his garden, to the little bed of spice, that he may feed in the gardens, and gather lilies.* She recognizes where she should seek the Word: she knows that he tarries among the prayers of the saints, and that he cleaves to them, and she understands that he feeds his Church, or the souls of his just ones, among the lilies. This mystery he showed thee in the Gospel, when on the sabbath he led his disciples through the sown fields. [1940B] Moses led the people of the Jews through the desert, Christ leads through the sown fields, Christ leads through the lilies; because through his passion the desert flowers as a lily. Let us follow then, that on the day of that great sabbath, in which is the great rest, we may gather fruits. And fear not that the Pharisees may accuse those who gather from the sown fields: even if they accuse, Christ excuses, and makes the souls which he wills, following him, like to that David who ate the loaves of proposition above the Law, already then conceiving in mind the prophetic sacraments of the new grace.

## 2.

*[n: (Vers. 2.)] I am my beloved's, and my beloved is mine, who feeds among* *1605* *the lilies.* The utterance seems easy and common, but it belongs to few. For rare enough is he who can say: *I am my beloved's, and my beloved is mine.* What is it to say, I am thine? For he says it who cleaves to [1940C] God with all his senses, who knows not how to think of anything else; he uses this utterance who can say to the Lord: *Show us the Father, and it sufficeth us [n: (Joan. XIV, 8)]*. Does the man greedy of money, of honor, of power use this utterance? For many it is not enough to know God — indeed for very many. So many peoples, so many nations, so many rich men think it poverty to serve the Lord; and he who is above all is to them a small and narrow thing: for them the Son of God, in whom are all things, is not enough. In short, that rich man in the Gospel to whom it was said: *If thou wilt be perfect, sell all that thou hast, and give to the poor [n: (Matth. XIX, 21)]*, [var: Vulg. *vade, vende quae habes*, "go, sell what thou hast" — without *omnia*] judged that God was not sufficient for him. In short, he was also made sorrowful, as though he were bidden to leave what was of more worth, and to choose what was of less. He therefore says, I am thine, who can say: *Behold, we have left [1940D] all things, and have followed thee [n: (Ibid., 27)]*.



## 3.

This is the voice of the apostles, and yet not of all the apostles; for Judas too was an apostle, and reclined among the apostles at the banquet of Christ. He too was saying, I am thine; but with the voice, not with the heart. Satan came and entered into him, and began to say: He is not thine, Jesus, but he is mine. In short, he thinks of the things which are mine, he turns over in his breast the things which are mine: he banquets with thee, and feeds with me: from thee he received bread, from me money: with thee he drinks, and to me he sells thy blood: he is thine as apostle, and mine as hireling. The worldly man cannot say, I am thine; for he has [1941A] many masters. Lust comes and says: Thou art mine; because thou lustest after the things which I am, the things of the body: in the love of that young girl thou hast sold thyself to me: in the embrace of that harlot I have counted out the price for thee. Avarice comes and says: The silver and gold which thou hast is the price of thy servitude: the possession thou holdest is the purchase of thy right, the sale of thy liberty. Luxury comes and says: Thou art mine; the banquet of a single day is the price of thy life: that outlay of feasts is the bidding for thy head, it is the sum of thy contract. And what is worse, thou hast been bought at a dear price, thou art cheaper than thy food: thy table for one day is more precious than the life of thy whole time: among cups I have redeemed thee, among feasts I have acquired thee. Ambition comes and says to thee: Plainly thou art mine. Knowest thou not that I therefore made thee command others, that thou thyself [1941B] mightest serve me? Knowest thou not that I therefore conferred power upon thee, that I might subject thee to my power? Or art thou ignorant that it was said to the Lord Savior himself by the prince of this world, when he had shown him all the kingdoms of the world: *All these will I give thee, if falling down thou wilt adore me [n: (Matth. IV, 9)]*? Before all, then, he himself is made subject who wishes to have others subject. All the vices come, and each says: Thou art mine. He whom so many claim, how cheap a chattel is he! How therefore canst thou, who art of this sort, say to Christ: I am thine? He will answer thee: *Not whosoever saith to me, Lord, Lord, shall enter into the kingdom of heaven [n: (Matth. VII, 21)]*: [var: Vulg. *Non omnis qui dicit mihi*, "Not every one that saith to me"] not whosoever says to me, I am thine, is mine.

## 4.

Plainly thou art mine, if conscience does not refute the voice, if thy mind or thy works do not disprove thy speech. [1941C] I do not deny him to be mine who does not deny himself, or at any rate if for my sake he denies his very self to himself. I will not have a little servant who serves very many masters. For how is he mine, if he says to me in word: I am thine; and denies it by his works, and by his deeds adjudges and binds himself to the devil? He is not mine whom lust inflames, because chastity is mine: he is not mine whom the care of despoiling lesser men drives about, because bounty is mine: he is not mine whom the breeze of fickleness disquiets, because tranquillity is mine. He is not mine who is drunken with the surfeit of wine, unclean with the filth of ambition, greedy of worldly glory, drunk unto peril, who cannot keep the track of sober moderation. I am peace, I know not how to quarrel: to what end is he mine, concerning whom the devil may come and say, He is mine; for to me he has bowed his neck, [1941D] I find more of my own in him: he claims thy name for himself, and my gift. He is not Christ's, therefore, save he who is a stranger to crime: he is not Christ's, save he who can always show himself Christ's little servant.

## 5.

*I am my beloved's, and my beloved is mine, who feeds among the lilies.* She seems to say, among the beloved things of the beloved: *My secret to me, my secret to me.* For she had understood, in the ecstasy of her love, certain hidden mysteries of the faith, which she judged were not to be published even to the daughters of Jerusalem themselves. *It is good to hide the mystery* *1606* *of the king;* for he sins against God who thinks that the mysteries committed [1942A] to him are to be divulged to the unworthy. [var: Vulg. Tob 12:7 *Sacramentum regis abscondere bonum est*, "It is good to hide the sacrament of the king"] And so it is a danger not only to speak false things, but even true ones, if a man insinuates them to those to whom he ought not. Which fault is of four parts, either of flattery, or of avarice, or of boasting, or of incautious talkativeness; because while a man wishes to flatter the one to whom he speaks, he pours out the mystery: some too, from zeal for gain, pursue the wage of betrayal, so as to sell by speaking what ought to be covered with silence: others, that they may seem to know more, and may vaunt their own knowledge, open what they ought to conceal: very many, while they speak without judgment, let go a word which they cannot call back. Therefore a serious man is praised, and not an idle one. There was a man hiding his own words, as the apostle Paul, who knew what he should speak to individuals, and when he ought to speak anything. [1942B]

## 6.

*[n: (Vers. 3.)] Thou art well-pleasing, my sister, shapely as Jerusalem, a wonder as one set in order.* [var: Vulg. *Pulchra es, amica mea; suavis, et decora sicut Jerusalem; terribilis ut castrorum acies ordinata*, "Thou art beautiful, my love; sweet and comely as Jerusalem; terrible as an army set in array"] The truth of love is proved by its perpetuity. And so the Bride is praised by the Bridegroom, because she has sought him so well and so constantly. And therefore she is now not only called *sister*, but is even named *well-pleasing*, as being pleasing to him; because she has pleased the Father: and, *shapely as Jerusalem, a wonder as one set in order;* because she has all the mysteries of the eternal city: and is a wonder to all who see her; because she is full as equity is, and perfect: and, having borrowed her brightness from the light of the Word, while she ever strains toward it, she is even terrible, being advanced by a certain order to the summit of the virtues.

## 7.

*Thou art beautiful,* he says, *my love, sweet and comely,* etc. And truly beautiful is she who strains toward him of whom [1942C] it is said: *Shapely in form above the sons of men [n: (Psal. XLIV, 3)]*. Whence let us too strain our mind toward him as much as we can, and be in him. Let us hold in mind that which is beautiful, comely, good; that by his illumination and brightness our soul may become shapely, and our mind clear. For if our eyes, when they are overspread with any dimness, are fed with the greenness of the fields, and by the appearance of a grove or a shady hill drive off all the unevenness of a sickening gaze, and the very pupils and orbs seem to be colored with a certain healthful appearance: how much more does this eye of the mind, when it beholds that highest good, and is engaged in it, and is fed by it, grow bright and shine forth. *Beautiful as Jerusalem.* [var: Vulg. *decora sicut Jerusalem*, "comely as Jerusalem"] This therefore is not the beauty of a perishable body, to perish either by disease or by old age, but a beauty subject to no [1942D] mischances, never to die in the esteem of good deserts; since it is worthy to be compared now not with human things, but with heavenly, whose life she lives on earth. And therefore the Bridegroom says to the Bride, as to one already perfect:

## 8.

*[n: (Vers. 4.)] Turn away thine eyes from me, because they have made me fly away.* Strain not against me with too great devotion and faith. Thou hast passed beyond the possibility of thy nature and of thine own condition, because to gaze straight upon the inaccessible light is a heavy thing. *Turn away,* he says, *thine eyes from me:* for this reason, that she cannot bear the fullness of his divinity, and the splendor of the true light. [1943A]

## 9.

Yet we can also take it thus: *Turn away thine eyes from me;* even if thou art perfect, there are other souls still to be redeemed by me, others to be propped up. For thou liftest me up by seeing me: but I came down for this, that I might lift up all. Even if I have risen, and have the seat of the Father, yet I will not leave you orphans, destitute as of a father's protection, but by my presence I will strengthen you. Thou hast this written in the Gospel: *Behold, I am with you even to the consummation of the world [n: (Matth. XXVIII, 20)]*. Turn away then thine eyes from me, because thou liftest me up. For the more a man strains toward the Lord, so much the more does he lift up the Lord, and is himself lifted up. Whence he too says: *I will exalt thee, O Lord, because thou hast upheld me [n: (Psalm. XXIX, 1)]*. For the holy man exalts the Lord, the sinner humbles him. He wishes therefore that she turn away her eyes, lest, [1943B] beholding her, because she can now follow to higher things, he be lifted up and leave the other souls behind. Therefore in the Gospel also he showed his glory not to all the disciples, but to the more perfect [n: (Matth. XVII, 1)]. Set before thyself now some teacher who wishes to open an obscure matter to his hearers: how, even if he himself be powerful in speech and knowledge, he yet condescends to the ignorance of those who do not understand, and uses a simple and plainer and more familiar speech, that he may be able to be understood. If therefore there be among the hearers one quicker in sense, who can easily follow, he lifts him up and rouses him. Seeing this, the teacher calls him back, that he may rather allow the teacher to dwell upon lowlier *1607* and plainer things; whereby the rest also may be able to follow.



## 10.

[n: (Vers. 5-7.)] The Bride is praised also because she is faithful, [1943C] because she is powerful in word, because she is fruitful with diverse fruits, because she is one dove, having the unity of the Spirit, in which is peace, which makes both one: and because she is not composed of the diverse elements of a distinct and warring nature. For what is so diverse as fire and water, air and earth, out of which the creature of our body consists? But every blessed soul is simple, which imitates him who says: *That all may be one, as thou, Father, in me, and I in thee, and that they themselves may be one in us [n: (Joan. XVII, 21)]*; for this is the consummation and the perfection, whence he added also: *That they may be one, as we also are one: and I in these, and thou in me, that they may be consummated in one [n: (Ibid., 23)]*. This therefore is the one and perfect dove, which, being simple and spiritual, is not disturbed by the passions of this body, in which [1943D] there are fightings without, fears within. In short, Scripture teaches us that by this word of unity concord and peace are signified, saying: *And the multitude of them that believed had one heart and one soul, and there was no separation at all among them [n: (Act. IV, 32)]*. [var: Vulg. *Multitudinis autem credentium erat cor unum et anima una*, without the final clause]

## 11.

Nor is the soul praised idly for its fruitfulness, not only because it is fruitful in virtues, but also because it has nothing of evil in itself; for that is comely and shapely in which there is nothing of evil. For that is comely which is good; but that which is uncomely, this is evil. Shapely is the fruitfulness of good works, contrary [1944A] therefore to shapeliness is barrenness; since in him who is deprived of shapeliness or comeliness there is every evil: but that which is evil, this is barren and unfruitful. And of this matter what plainer sign is there than the earth? For the earth which is good is fertile and fruitful: but that which is bad is meager and barren; but that which is fertile, this is also comely. For what is more beautiful than a full field, when the crop waves, when the apples redden, or when the garlands of grapes hang down, or the olive, laden with berries, bends over, or when the tops of the mountains and the low places of the valleys are clothed with green herbage?

## 12.

For God is the author of all good things: and the things which are his are assuredly all good, and there is no evil there; and if our mind abide in him, it knows no evil. The soul therefore which does not abide in God is [1944B] to itself the author of evils, and so it sins: but *the soul that sinneth, the same shall die;* for being loosed from the golden chains of the virtues, it is borne headlong down the precipice, and slips to lower things. But blessed is the soul which no adverse battles of the body storm. This soul, like a sparrow when the snare is broken, flies away. For the baits of evils are the pleasures of the body: whosoever strains after these entangles his own soul in the snare. But he who abstains from its baits, and comes out of its darkness, his soul shines as the dawn, of which it is said:

## 13.

*[n: (Vers. 9.)] Who is this that looks forth as the dawn, beautiful as the moon?* [var: Vulg. *Quae est ista quae progreditur quasi aurora consurgens, pulchra ut luna*, "Who is she that cometh forth as the morning rising, fair as the moon"] For she looks forth as from a free house, nor does she say: *Darkness covers me, and walls surround me, and who knows whether the Most High sees [n: (Eccli. XXIII, 26)]*? [var: Vulg. *Tenebrae circumdant me, et parietes cooperiunt me, et nemo circumspicit me: quem vereor?*, with the verbs the other way about and a different close] But she rather seeks the light [1944C] as in the upper parts of her house, that is, of her body: and, set above the world, she gazes upon divine things and lifts herself to eternal things, that she may stand near to God; now carrying the light of her works round her own orb, as the moon does, over the whole world.

## 14.

*[n: (Vers. 10.)] I went down into the garden of the nut tree, to see in the birth of the torrent, and to look whether the vine had flowered, and the pomegranates had budded.* [var: Vulg. *Descendi in hortum nucum, ut viderem poma convallium*, "I went down into the garden of nuts, to see the fruits of the valleys"] The Word of God is invited, in short, into the garden of the nut tree, in which is the fruit of prophetic reading, and the priestly grace, which is bitter in temptations, hard in labors, fruitful in the inward virtues. Whence Aaron's rod of nut-wood also flowered, not now by its own nature, but by a hidden power. Let him come down therefore into the garden, that he may gather the vintage of faith, take in the odors, find the heavenly fodder, feast upon the sweetness [1944D] of our honey.

## 15.

For while she is praised by the Bridegroom, modestly shrinking from being praised to her face; then, called back by the love of the Bridegroom, she says: *I went down into the garden of the nut tree to see in the birth of the torrent.* For where is the Church, save where the priestly rod and grace flowers? There she is frequently, that she may be tried in bitternesses and in temptations. By the nut we understand bitternesses, by the torrent temptations, yet tolerable ones, because it is written: *Our soul hath swum through the torrent [n: (Psalm. CXXIII, 5)]*. [var: Vulg. *torrentem pertransivit anima nostra*, "our soul hath passed through the torrent"] And so she went down into the place of bitterness, where the vine flowers, [1945A] and the fruit various and manifold in the appearance of pomegranates, which as it were under one covering of the whole body is fortified by faith and charity. *1608*

## 16.

In that bitterness, therefore, the soul did not know itself, *For the corruptible body weighs down the soul, and the earthly dwelling is quickly bowed down [n: (Sap. IX)]*. [var: Vulg. *Corpus enim quod corrumpitur aggravat animam, et terrena inhabitatio deprimit sensum multa cogitantem*] But it ought always to know itself. Yet Peter was tempted, and Peter too did not know himself; for if he had known himself, he would not have denied his author. But Christ knew him. In short, he knew him, because he also looked back at him; for the Lord knows who are his: and, as a good ruler, by the reins of his mercy he called back his own from the fall: our ruler therefore is Christ. Whence there follows also:

## 17.

*[n: (Vers. 11.)] The chariot of Aminadab has set me:* [var: Vulg. *Nescivi: anima mea conturbavit me propter quadrigas Aminadab*, "I knew not: my soul troubled me because of the four-horse chariots of Aminadab"] in that the soul is joined to the body, and, like a certain [1945B] chariot of raging horses, seeks a charioteer to rule it. For Aminadab was the father of Naason, as we read in Numbers [n: (Num. I, 7)], who was prince of the people of Juda, whose figure is referred to Christ, who, the true prince of the people, mounting the soul of the just man as a chariot, guides it as driver with the reins of the Word, lest by the fury of violent horses it be swept headlong and over precipices. For his four horses, as it were, are the four affections: anger, desire, pleasure, fear; and when it has begun to be driven by these in their raging, it in no way knows itself. For the corruptible body weighs down the soul, and, like a chariot of irrational animals, sweeps it away unwilling, rushing forward as by a certain onset while cares roll on, until the aforesaid passions of the body grow mild by the power of the Word. [1945C]

## 18.

This is the foresight of the Word as of a good driver: that the mortal body joined to that soul which is not in itself subject to death may not make the driving of it difficult. First therefore let him tame these swift motions of the body, and bridle them with the bond of reason: then let him beware lest, by unequal motion, they entangle themselves like horses; so that either the wicked one discolors the good, or the slow one hinders it, or the turbulent one disquiets it. For the horse of malice snorts, and by tossing itself injures the chariot, and weighs down its yoke-fellow. This the good charioteer soothes, and sends forth into the field of truth, and turns aside from the windings of deceit. Safe is the course to higher things, perilous the descent to lower. Thence, as though well discharged from service, they who have borne the yoke of the Word are led even to the Lord's manger, in which the food is not hay, but the bread which came down from heaven. Of this [1945D] chariot there are wheels, of which the Prophet said: *And the spirit of life was in the wheels [n: (Ezech. I, 20)]*; in that the chariot of the soul, smooth and round, rolls on without any stumbling.

## 19.

*The chariot of Aminadab has set me.* The soul therefore is a chariot, which bears a good ruler. If the soul is a chariot, it has horses either good or bad. The good horses are the virtues of the soul: the bad horses are the passions of the body. The good ruler therefore restrains and calls back the bad horses, and urges on the good. The good horses are four: prudence, temperance, fortitude, justice: the bad horses are wrathfulness, concupiscence, fear, iniquity. Sometimes the horses themselves disagree among themselves, and either wrathfulness stretches forward, or [1946A] fear: and they hinder one another, and slow the course. But indeed the good horses fly, and raise themselves from the earth to higher things, and lift up the soul; especially if they have the sweet yoke and light burden of him who says: *Take my yoke upon you; for my yoke is sweet, and my burden light [n: (Matth. XI, 29)]*.

## 20.

He himself is the ruler, who knows how to govern his own horses, so that the course of all may be even: he slows the swifter ones by prudence, justice admonishes the more sluggish with its own lash; temperance makes them gentler, fortitude makes them harder. He knows how to couple the discordant, lest perchance they shatter his chariot. And so it is possible to see, by an intelligible spectacle, every soul caught up to heaven with the highest striving, the horses hastening to come first to the prize of Christ, upon whose necks the palm may first be laid. [1946B] These are the horses subjected to the yoke of faith, bound fast by the chain of charity, by the reins of justice, by the halters of sobriety.



## 21.

Beautifully therefore she says: *The chariot of Aminadab has set me,* that is, the father of the people: but he himself who is the father of the people is likewise the father of Naason, that is, of serpents. Now do thou call to mind who hung upon the cross like a serpent for the salvation of all men, and thou wilt understand that soul to be peaceable whose president is God the Father, and whose driver is Christ; because this name too is written in our books: *Father, father, driver of Israel [n: (IV Reg. XI, 12)]*. [var: Vulg. 4 Kings 2:12 *Pater mi, pater mi, currus Israel et auriga ejus*, "My father, my father, the chariot of Israel and its charioteer"] This driver therefore says:

## 22.

*[n: (Vers. 12.)] Turn, O Sunamite, turn.* [var: Vulg. *Revertere, revertere, Sulamitis*, "Return, return, O Sulamitess"] Well does he say it as a driver, and as one speaking to a chariot: *Turn, O Sunamite,* that is, O peaceable one; for the soul which is peaceable turns and corrects itself quickly, even if it has sinned before: and the more does *1609* Christ mount it, [1946C] and deign to rule it — he to whom it is said: *Mount upon thy horses, and thy riding is health.* And elsewhere: *Thou hast sent thy horses of Tharsis [n: (Habac. III, 8)]*. [var: Vulg. Hab 3:8 *qui ascendes super equos tuos, et quadrigae tuae salvatio*, "who wilt mount upon thy horses, and thy four-horse chariots are salvation"] These are the horses of Christ. Christ therefore mounts his own horses: the Word of God mounts holy souls. When our soul has become peaceable, so that it is said to it: *Turn; turn, O Sunamite,* which signifies "peaceable," then it will receive into itself Christ as a seal, that is, the image of God, that it may be unto the image; since *As is the heavenly, such are they also that are heavenly, and we must bear the image of the heavenly [n: (I Cor. XV, 48)]*, that is, peace. And that we may know it to be true, thou hast in the same Song of Songs, at the very end, this said to the soul now made perfect, which may the Lord Jesus say to me also: *Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon thy arm [n: (Cant. VIII, 6)]*; that peace may shine in my heart, and Christ in my works, [1946D] that wisdom, and justice, and redemption may be formed in me.

## CAPUT SEPTIMUM.



## 1.

*[n: (Vers. 1.)] How shapely are thy steps made in sandals, O daughter of Aminadab! The measures of thy thighs are like collars, the work of a craftsman.* [var: Vulg. *Quam pulchri sunt gressus tui in calceamentis, filia principis! Juncturae femorum tuorum sicut monilia quae fabricata sunt manu artificis*, "How beautiful are thy steps in shoes, O prince's daughter! the joints of thy thighs are like jewels"] There is no doubt that here too the steps of the Church, or the advance of the soul, are signified. *How shapely are the feet of them that preach the gospel of peace, of them that preach good things [n: (Esai. LII, 7)]*! [var: Vulg. Isa 52:7 *Quam pulchri super montes pedes annuntiantis et praedicantis pacem*, "How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that announceth and preacheth peace"] Assuredly he calls shapely the advances of evangelical preaching and disputation, as it is said elsewhere: *Pass over the rivers [n: (Esai. XLVII, 2)]*, that is, run across the streams and slippery places of this world with a steadfast [1947A] gait of the mind. That this is said of the going of the soul, David plainly shows, when he brings forward that his soul had passed over the torrent of iniquities [n: (Psal. CCXIII, 5)].

## 2.

But what does he mean by saying that the steps of the Church are shapely in sandals? We read it said to Moses: *Loose the sandal of thy feet (Exodi III, 5)*: whereby he seems to be admonished not to be held bound by bodily chains. Therefore in the Song of Songs he signifies the shapely loveliness of the soul, which uses the flesh as a sandal, and in that very sandal suffers no hindrance, but excels in the comeliness of its going. Let the soul therefore shoe itself with ecclesiastical grace, that it may pass over the course and passage of this life with comeliness. Which comes about if it does not befoul its own sandal with bodily mud, does not plunge it into the abyss of vices: if it chastise [1947B] its own flesh, lest it delay in the course, and be weighed down with the burden of fat grease. The good sandal of the soul is modesty, the good step is in the track of chastity. But wisdom is the mantle of the soul, whence it is written: *Honor her, and she will embrace thee [n: (Prov. IV, 8)]*. [var: Vulg. *Arripe illam, et exaltabit te; glorificaberis ab ea cum eam fueris amplexatus*, "Take hold of her, and she shall exalt thee"] Let us use the body therefore as a sandal for the works of a lower virtue: for ministry, not for command: for compliance, not for delight: for obedience, not for dissension; and let us set our footstep in the way of wisdom, lest some force of a torrent shut in our steps.

## 3.

Therefore it was said to Moses: *Loose thy sandal (Exodi III, 5)*. It was said to Jesus son of Nave also [n: (Josue V, 16)]. But of Christ it was not said, but rather it is written, the Baptist saying: *After me there comes a man, [1947C] whose sandals I am not worthy to bear [n: (Matth. III, 11)]*; [var: Vulg. Matt 3:11 *qui autem post me venturus est, fortior me est*, "he that shall come after me is mightier than I"; *post me venit vir* is the wording of John 1:30] because they are rightly admonished to loose their sandal who could not be without sin. But he not only did not loose his sandal, but even unlooses the sandals of others; because he not only kept his own body immune from sins, but also gave pardon of the sins of all.

## 4.

Therefore the Church, in imitation of Christ, is shapely, and in its sandals washed from every fault. And perhaps when it speaks wisdom among the perfect, it is shapely in its upper members: but when men even of a lower rank or of lower teaching follow the Word, do not forget the order of faith, keep the precepts of the priest, it is shapely in its sandals. Very often the clergy has erred, the priest has changed his sentence, [1947D] the rich have sided with the earthly king of this world, and the people has kept its own faith.

## 5.

Whence of the Lord Jesus also we can rightly say that even in those things which are bodily the Word has shapely steps, when there is disputing about moral matters. And the apostles are perhaps for this reason sent with bare feet, lest their disputation be overshadowed, but rather shine out. And so the Church is the daughter of Aminadab, that is, of the willing or the well-pleasing one; because a willing or well-pleasing one gathered her, and she is shapely in her sandals. And deservedly it is added:

[1948A] *1610* 6. *The measures of thy thighs are like collars, the work of the hands of a craftsman;* that the ornaments of the Church's posterity might be sung. For by the thigh we recognize the badge of generation, according to that word: *Gird thy sword upon thy thigh, O most mighty [n: (Psal. XLIV, 4)]*: whereby it is signified that the Son of God, when he had emptied himself, girt with the divinity of the Word, and shod with human generation, should come forth from the Virgin to give salvation to all. But "measures" are the precious ornaments which are wont to be hung upon the necks of matrons. So great therefore is the advance of the Church that is signified, that it is compared to the most precious ornaments, and to the collars of those who triumph; for these are the ornaments of warriors. Whence Symmachus also said *Epitrachelia*, that is, the things which are about the neck. Whether therefore the generation of Christ [1948B] from the Virgin, or the propagation of the Church, in appearance indeed as by the hand of a craftsman with adorned collars, but with the spiritual badges of true virtue, has adorned the necks of the faithful. In short, this whole description of the members of the Church is full of comeliness and of praise. For also:

## 7.

*[n: (Vers. 2.)] Her navel is as a turned goblet,* it is proclaimed, not lacking in the mixture; in that, being turned upon the lathe in every doctrine, in the fullness of knowledge, it does not lack the spiritual draught either. And her belly is not only fattened with the heap of wheat, that is, with the stronger foods of the heavenly mystery, but is also filled as with certain lilies with the sweetness of moral things. Whence she too, as one well deserving, is crowned as a queen with the blood of Christ, as it is written: *And the adornment of her head is as purple.* [var: Vulg. Cant 7:5 *comae capitis tui sicut purpura regis*, "the hairs of thy head as the purple of the king"] The blood of Christ is the purple, which dyes [1948C] the souls of the saints, resplendent not only in color, but also in power; because it makes them kings, and better kings, to whom it gives an eternal kingdom.

## 8.

*Thy navel is as a turned goblet, never wanting cups.* The navel of the soul and the belly of her who ascends to Christ is fine; and therefore she is praised by the voice of the Bridegroom saying: *Thy navel is as a turned goblet, not lacking in the mixture; thy belly a heap of fortified wheat among the lilies.* [var: Vulg. Cant 7:2 *numquam indigens poculis… venter tuus sicut acervus tritici vallatus liliis*; the first form Migne prints in this same paragraph agrees with the Vulgate, the second does not] For it is turned upon the lathe in every doctrine, and the spiritual draught is not lacking in fullness, and in the knowledge of heavenly secrets. The belly of the soul also is mystical, as the navel is; by which belly it takes not only the strong food by which hearts are made firm, but also the sweet and flowery food by which they are delighted.

## 9.

*[n: (Vers. 4.)] Thine eyes are pools in Hesebon, in the gates [1948D] of the daughter of many, Thy nostrils are as the tower of Lebanon.* [var: Vulg. Cant 7:4 *Oculi tui sicut piscinae in Hesebon quae sunt in porta filiae multitudinis. Nasus tuus sicut turris Libani*, "Thy eyes like the fishpools in Hesebon which are in the gate of the daughter of the multitude. Thy nose is as the tower of Libanus"] What are the pools in Hesebon, save an abundance in reasonable thoughts, which is in the gates of the Church, to which the multitude of doctrine is deservedly brought? For the daughter of many is the posterity and the effect of very many doctrines, whose nostrils are the odor of sacrifices, surpassing all flowers.

## 10.

*Thy nose is as the tower of Lebanon:* exceedingly lofty beyond measure, and therefore it looks forth upon the face of Damascus, smelling out, that is, the gentile people, and its faith, by whose grace it might wipe away the stench of its own faults. The face [1949A] therefore of Damascus is the faith of the Gentiles, overshadowed by no covering, hidden by no clothing, naked and free, more intent upon heaven than upon earth. This the nostrils of the Church watch and look forth upon, gathering what is sweet in it both of the breathings of odors and of grace.



## 11.

And well is it said, *Her nostrils are as the tower of Lebanon;* [var: Vulg. Cant. 7:4 *nasus tuus sicut turris Libani*, "thy nose is as the tower of Lebanon"] because in the sacrifices there is the sweet odor of the Church, in which sacrifices the remission of sins is a victim of good odor. Take therefore these nostrils, O man, that thou mayest sunder the flowery things from the rank-smelling, and then the Lord will quicken thee. For when he shall have marked what thou askest of him, that he may turn the eyes of thy mind away from vanity, he works together with thy soul, so that, if it is caught by some appearance, or is not bent either by hardness or by weakness, he may bend it with the yoke of the Word, and govern it with his own reins, that by the will of God it may be drawn away from vices, and may take the odor of everlasting [1949B] life.

## 12.

*[n: (Vers. 8.)] I will go up into the palm tree, and I will lay hold of its height.* [var: Vulg. *Dixi: Ascendam in palmam, et apprehendam fructus ejus*, "I said: I will go up into the palm tree, and will take hold of the fruit thereof"] Whence know thou that he has gone up even to this soul, and has led her through to the place of the palm, when he also says to her: *How beautiful and sweet art thou become, O charity, in thy delights! Thy stature is become like to a palm tree [n: (Cant. VII, 6)]*. [var: Vulg. Cant. 7:6–7 *Quam pulchra es, et quam decora, carissima, in deliciis… Statura tua assimilata est palmae*, "How beautiful art thou, and how comely, my dearest, in delights"] And she herself says: *I said, I will go up to the palm tree.* But charity itself also is a palm, for it is itself the fulness of victory; for the fulness of the Law is charity. Let us run therefore that we may lay hold, let us run that we may conquer: he who has conquered goes up into the palm, and eats the fruits thereof; he who has conquered runs no longer, but sits, as *1611* it is written: *He that shall overcome, I will give to him to sit with me in my seat, as I also have overcome, and have sat with my Father in his seat [n: (Apoc. III, 21)]*. Hence [1949C] the philosophers set forth in their books those race-course contests of souls, nor yet were they able to come through to the palm; because their souls knew not the sweetness and the height of the Word. *I will go up,* she says, *into the palm* by conquering the world; that I may hold the height of the Word, which this soul knew, in whom was the turning of the Word; for thus she says:

## 13.

*[n: (Vers. 10.)] I am my brother's, and his turning is upon me.* [var: Vulg. *Ego dilecto meo, et ad me conversio ejus*, "I to my beloved, and his turning is toward me"] This sense she has repeated a third time, and diversely, in the Song of Songs. At the beginning she says: *My brother is mine, and I am his: who feeds among the lilies until the day breathe, and the shadows incline [n: (Cant. II, 16)]*. [var: Vulg. Cant. 2:16–17 *Dilectus meus mihi, et ego illi, qui pascitur inter lilia, donec aspiret dies*, "My beloved to me, and I to him, who feedeth among the lilies, till the day break"] Then she says: *I am my brother's, and my brother is mine, who feeds among the lilies [n: (Cant. VI, 2)]*. At the end she says: *I am my brother's, and his turning is upon me.* The first belongs to the instruction [1949D] of the soul; and therefore she also set first: *My brother is mine;* for by his showing himself she took also the affection of cleaving to God. That which follows is according to progress: the third according to perfection? In the first, as in instruction, the soul still sees shadows, not yet stirred by the revelation of the Word drawing near; and therefore the day of the Gospel did not yet shine upon her: in the second, without the confusion of shadows, she gathers holy odors: in the third, being now perfect, she ministers rest to the Word within herself; that he may be turned upon her, and lay back his head and rest; and she invites him to her field, saying: [1950A]

## 14.

*[n: (Vers. 11.)] Come, my brother, let us go out into the field, let us rest in the strongholds.* [var: Vulg. *Veni, dilecte mi, egrediamur in agrum, commoremur in villis*, "Come, my beloved, let us go forth into the field, let us abide in the villages"] Above she was inviting him to the garden; here to a field having not only the grace of flowers, but also wheat and barley, that is, the firm foundation of solid virtues, that he may look upon its fruit. *Let us rest,* she says, *in the strongholds,* to which Adam, when he had been cast out of paradise, had been banished: in these he rested, but he tilled the earth.

## 15.

But for what reason she would have him go out into the field, the understanding of it is plain: that as a good shepherd he may feed his flock, may lift up the weary, may call back the wandering. For although this soul has kept both new things and old, yet there are still, as it were, lambs who must be nourished with the juice of milk. Therefore, as one perfect, she intercedes not for herself, but [1950B] for others; that he may go out from the bosom of the Father, that he may proceed forth as a bridegroom out of his chamber; that going out he may run his way, that he may win the weak; that he may not linger in that hidden throne of the Father, and in that light, whereto weak horses are not able to climb; but that he may be taken up and brought into the house and the secret place of the Bride. Let him be without for himself, that he may be within for us: let him be in our midst, even though he be not seen by us. Whence she says: *Who shall give thee to me, my brother, sucking the breasts of my mother? Finding thee without, I will kiss thee [n: (Cant. VIII, 1)]*. [var: Vulg. *Quis mihi det te fratrem meum sugentem ubera matris meae, ut inveniam te foris, et deosculer te*, "Who shall give thee to me for my brother, sucking the breasts of my mother, that I may find thee without, and kiss thee"] Good is the soul which is without, that the Word may be within: she outside the body, that the Word may dwell in us.

## 16.

*I will take thee up,* she says, *and I will bring thee in [n: (Ibid., 2)]*. [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:2 *Apprehendam te, et ducam in domum matris meae*, "I will take hold of thee, and bring thee into my mother's house"] Rightly is the Word of God taken up and brought in; because he knocks at the soul, that the door may be opened to him: and unless [1950C] he shall have found the door opened to him, he does not enter. But if any man shall have opened the gate, he enters and sups. Thus the Bride takes up the Word, that by taking him up she may be taught. Whence not undeservedly he goes up to her, to the higher dwellings, and she ever receives an advance of virtue.

## 17.

*Come, my brother, let us go out into the field, let us rest in the strongholds, let us rise at daybreak in the vineyards, let us see whether the vine has flowered.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 7:12 *Mane surgamus ad vineas; videamus si floruit vinea*, "Let us get up early to the vineyards; let us see if the vineyard flourish"] Many fields have fruits; but that one is better which abounds both with fruits and with flowers. The field of the Church, then, is fruitful with diverse plenties. Here thou mayest discern the buds of virginity blooming with flower; there, as woods in the plains, widowhood strong in gravity; elsewhere, as the rich produce of the Church, the harvest of wedlock filling the barns of the world, and, as it were with the offspring of a wedded vine, the winepresses of the Lord Jesus overflowing, [1950D] in which the fruit of faithful wedlock abounds.

## 18.

The Bride, having obtained the plenty of her desire, speaks to the Bridegroom: *Come, my brother, let us go out into the field, let us rest in the strongholds, let us rise at daybreak to the vineyards.* Thou seest how the Bride invites the Word of God, that he may come into the lands and take away the sins of the world. This field was before a desert, squalid with the briars of our offences, bristling with thorns. There was a stronghold in which Adam, bound fast, held the heirs of his posterity in perpetual exile. Thither, then, the Church leads Christ, [1951A] that he may set Adam free. Then, the exiles being loosed, the field of this world began to have fit tillers: and that which was before hungry was made fruitful by the planting *1612* of the eternal vine. Nor yet does she so range abroad, glad with spiritual shoots, but she calls Christ to these vineyards, where there are those who sing psalms and those who pray, where by nights and by days a harmless fruit endures.

## 19.

*[n: (Vers. 12.)] There,* she says, *will I give my breasts, there the mandrakes have given their odor.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 7:12–13 *ibi dabo tibi ubera mea. Mandragorae dederunt odorem*, "there will I give thee my breasts. The mandrakes have given a smell"] Very many distinguish a certain sex among mandrakes, so that they think there are both males and females, but that the females are of heavy odor. It signifies therefore that the nations, which before stank, since they were the weaker by a certain unmanned feebleness of unbelief, began to bear fruits of good odor after they believed unto the coming of the Lord. We read also that [1951B] holy Rachel received mandrakes from her sister Lia, that she might grant her to sleep that night with holy Jacob. Ruben the firstborn had brought the mandrakes to his mother Lia, who, with bleared eyes, took on the figure of the Synagogue; because she could not see the grace of Christ, the gaze of her feeble mind being dulled. By which it is declared that the fruits which the Synagogue had before received from the firstborn Son of God, she yielded up to the Church. But because, the lying-together of that night having been sought, Lia conceived and bore an heir of her posterity, she fulfilled the mystery; since, when the apostles believed, the remnant of the Jews were made safe through the election of grace, whence the Church says:

## 20.

*[n: (Vers. 13.)] New things and old, my brother, have I kept for thee. Who shall give thee to me for a brother, sucking the breasts of my mother? Finding thee without, I will kiss thee, and indeed they shall not [1951C] despise me. I will take thee up and bring thee into my mother's house, and into the secret place of her that conceived me: there thou shalt teach me.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 7:13–8:2 *omnia poma nova et vetera, dilecte mi, servavi tibi… et jam me nemo despiciat. Apprehendam te, et ducam in domum matris meae: ibi me docebis*, "all fruits new and old, my beloved, I have kept for thee… and now no man may despise me. I will take hold of thee, and bring thee into my mother's house: there thou shalt teach me"] Having therefore the shaping of the new and the old Scriptures, and not feeling herself contemptible, she holds the Word not only by prayers in the secret of the heart, but also with the voices of a psalm-singing choir she kisses him, as with certain kisses of grace.

## 21.

*New things and old, my brother, have I kept for thee;* this is to say, I hold all the commandments of the New and the Old Testament. The Church alone can say this: no other congregation says it, the Synagogue does not say it, holding neither the new things according to the letter, nor the old things according to the spirit. The Manichaean heresy does not say, *The old things have I kept for thee,* seeing that it does not receive the prophets. Deservedly is she called made white, who shines with the grace of either Testament. [1951D]

## 22.

*I have adjured you, O daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir up and stir up again love, until such time as it shall will.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:4 *Adjuro vos, filiae Jerusalem, ne suscitetis, neque evigilare faciatis dilectam, donec ipsa velit*, "I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir not up, nor make the beloved to awake, till she please"] Still she seeks one that stirs up, and demands that it be stirred up for her by the daughters of Jerusalem, for whose sake, that is, for the sake of faithful souls, she desires that the Bridegroom be provoked to a more abundant love of her. And so those powers speak, which guard the gates of heaven.

## CHAPTER THE EIGHTH.



## 1.

*[n: (Vers. 5.)] Who is she who this one comes up white, leaning upon her brother?* [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:5 *Quae est ista quae ascendit de deserto, deliciis affluens, innixa super dilectum suum?*, "Who is this that cometh up from the desert, flowing with delights, leaning upon her beloved?"] In what manner she who has set [1952A] her head upon the left hand of wisdom, that she might open her hand to the poor for the relieving of the needy; that she might use riches divided or left to her, not riches seized and plundered — riches which, desirous of glory, she acquired even by good works, and did not court by the empty boasting of worldly dignity: for this it is to set one's own head, and a certain ruling part of the senses, above the hand of wisdom. That soul, I say, ascends by whitening merits, out of the desert of this life, as very many hold it to be, unto that ever-flowering place of eternal gladness.

## 2.

These are the powers which also in the book of Isaiah say: *Who is this that comes from Edom, the redness of garments out of Bosor, so shapely in his robe [n: (Esai. LXIII, 1)]*? [var: Vulg. *Quis est iste, qui venit de Edom, tinctis vestibus de Bosra? iste formosus in stola sua*, "Who is this that cometh from Edom, with dyed garments from Bosra? this well-formed one in his robe"] These, I say, are they that marvel that out of this broken and rocky desert any soul is able to ascend [1952B] without the ruin of great vices; and therefore they give thanks that she has been found, who has not defiled the garments of natural innocence with the ink of worldly folly, but rather has cleansed them with the whiteness of spiritual wisdom and of grace. The daughters of Jerusalem marvel, the holy souls of the patriarchs and of the prophets and of the just of old, or the heavenly powers, saying: *Who is this that comes up made white, leaning upon her brother?* That is, with such solemnities the Church shines, and she who before was dark through the day now is splendid in the nights, and gives back her light. The Lord himself also, delighted with so great an offering of them that sing psalms, says:

## 3.

*[n: (Vers. 6.)] Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a signet upon thy arm; because charity is strong as death, hard* *1613* *as hell is thy zeal. [1952C] Its wings are wings of fire, and of flames. Much water shall not be able to shut out charity, and the floods shall not overwhelm it.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:6–7 *quia fortis est ut mors dilectio, dura sicut infernus aemulatio; lampades ejus lampades ignis atque flammarum. Aquae multae non potuerunt exstinguere caritatem, nec flumina obruent illam*, "because love is strong as death, jealousy hard as hell; the lamps thereof are lamps of fire and of flames. Many waters could not quench charity, neither shall the floods drown it"] Because the Church has seen so great a devotion, she thinks the peoples now fit, that we may be able to bear his seal in our heart and on our arm. For him the Father, God, has sealed, and he who has received his testimony has set his seal that God is true; and therefore they that work the food which endures unto life everlasting are sealed unto the image and likeness of Christ, who is the image of the invisible God. As therefore God is true, do thou also seal truth in thy thought and in thy work; that thy mouth speak not a lie, that thy hands work not the works of men, which are of this deceitful world, but those things which are of God: that they may give bountifully to the poor, may raise up the weak, may honor the dead with the mounds of burial. By these works [1952D] charity is sought, that no one may be able to be torn away from Christ even by the peril of death; whence he says: *Who shall separate us from Christ? Tribulation, or distress, or persecution [n: (Rom. VIII, 35)]*? [var: Vulg. *Quis ergo nos separabit a caritate Christi?*, "Who then shall separate us from the charity of Christ?"] And below: *For I am confident that neither death, nor life, nor angels [n: (Ibid.)]*. [var: Vulg. Rom. 8:38 *Certus sum enim*, "For I am sure"]

## 4.

In that same fashion also the zeal of charity is hard, and its wings are wings of fire. It has wings as a dove, for the feathers of the dove are silvered, wherewith he who loves flies forth, saying: *Behold, I have gone far off in flight, and I abode in the wilderness [n: (Psal. LIV, 8)]*. But the wings of charity are wings of fire, wherewith it kindles the ardor of love. [1953A] With this heat the Lord made his angels fervent spirits, and his ministers a fire that burns, but does not burn up. We read also of a wing of the temple, upon which the devil, tempting, made the Savior to go up, which was on the pinnacle of the temple [n: (Matth. IV, 3)]. There are therefore heights of piety, there are pinnacles of charity, which have been wont to kindle the vapor of grace in human breasts; so that much water is not able to quench nor to shut out charity, and no floods of worldly tempests shut it in. We have said that charity shuts out fear; we have not said that it shuts out every disturbance; for indeed to him who loves God there is a deep tranquillity of a mind confirmed.

## 5.

*[n: (Vers. 7.)] Many waters,* he says, *shall not be able [1953B] to shut out charity, and the floods shall not overflow it.* Much water is of diverse passions, and the floods of worldly desires stirred up by the motions of the body, which nevertheless cannot overthrow the wall of charity. And therefore one grounded in charity says: *Our soul has passed through the torrent [n: (Psal. CXXIII, 5)]*. Could the water of the sea shut out the charity of Moses? And that the series of the psalms may support thee according to the letter: assuredly he who loved God believed the way through the seas to be safe for him; but they who loved not God, being sunk in the waves, endured an end worthy of their sacrilege.

## 6.

*[n: (Vers. 5.)] Who is this that comes up, leaning upon her brother?* Above they said, *Who then is this that looks forth as the daybreak, shapely [1953C] as the moon, choice as the sun?* [var: Vulg. Cant. 6:9 *Quae est ista quae progreditur quasi aurora consurgens, pulchra ut luna, electa ut sol?*, "Who is she that cometh forth as the morning rising, beautiful as the moon, bright as the sun?"] Here something more was found to be added, in that she had ascended leaning upon the Word of God; for the more perfect recline upon Christ, as John also was reclining on the breast of Christ. So therefore this one also either was leaning upon Christ, or was laying herself back upon him: or assuredly, since we are speaking of a marriage, being now as it were delivered over into the right hand of Christ, she was being led into the chamber for the Bridegroom. And because there is now a bond of charity, the bridegroom caresses her, and says:

## 7.

*Under the apple tree I raised thee up; there thy mother travailed with thee, there she travailed with thee that bore thee.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:5 *Sub arbore malo suscitavi te; ibi corrupta est mater tua, ibi violata est genitrix tua*, "Under the apple tree I raised thee up: there thy mother was corrupted, there she was defloured that bore thee"] Good is the soul which rests under a fruitful tree, and one especially of good odor. For if Nathanael was good, in whom there was no guile, and was seen under a fig tree, surely good is the soul which under the apple tree was raised up by [1953D] her Bridegroom. For it is more to be raised up than to be seen: and more still to be raised up by the bridegroom. For although Nathanael was seen under the tree, yet his soul was not a bride; because he came secretly to Christ, because he feared the Jews. She was not shapely as the moon, choice as the sun, who was in the shadow; because a bride who weds by day confesses publicly. Therefore this one is under the apple tree, that one under the fig; because this one spread abroad more widely the odor of her confession, while that one had the sweetness of the purity of innocence, and had not the burning of the spirit.

## 8.

*There,* he says, *thy mother travailed with thee, there she travailed with thee that bore thee;* for there we are born, there we are born again. [1954A] But those are travailed with in whom the image of Christ is formed, whence *1614* Paul also says: *Little children, whom I travail with, until Christ be formed in you [n: (Galat. IV, 19)]*. [var: Vulg. *Filioli mei, quos iterum parturio*, "My little children, of whom I am in labour again"] For he has travailed who receives in the womb the spirit of salvation, and pours it into others. Whence, since Christ was now formed in her, he says:

## 9.

*[n: (Vers. 6.)] Set me as a seal in thy heart, as a signet on thy arm.* The seal of Christ is on the forehead, the seal is in the heart: on the forehead, that we may always confess; in the heart, that we may always love; the seal on the arm, that we may always work. Let his image therefore shine in our confession, let it shine in our reading, let it shine in our works and deeds; that, if it can be brought to pass, his whole appearance may be expressed in us. Let him be our head, because the head of the man is Christ; let him be [1954B] our eye, that through him we may see the Father; let him be our voice, whereby we may speak to the Father; let him be the right hand, whereby we may offer our sacrifice to God the Father. He also is our seal, which is the badge of charity and of perfection; because the Father, loving, sealed the Son, as we read: *Whom the Father, God, hath sealed [n: (Joan. VI, 27)]*. [var: Vulg. *Hunc enim Pater signavit Deus*, "For him hath God the Father sealed"] And so Christ is our charity. Good is the charity, when he offered himself to death for those he loved: good is the charity which forgave sin.

## 10.

And therefore let our soul put on charity, and charity of such a kind as is strong as death; because as death is the end of sins, so also is charity; since he who loves the Lord ceases to sin: for *charity thinks no evil, neither rejoices over [1954C] iniquity, but bears all things [n: (I Cor. XIII, 5)]*. For he who seeks not the things that are his own, how shall he seek the things of another? There is also that strong death through the laver, by which every sin is buried and the fault is remitted. Such was the charity which that woman of the Gospel bore, of whom the Lord says: *Her many sins are forgiven, because she has loved much [n: (Luc. VII, 47)]*. [var: Vulg. *Remittuntur ei peccata multa*, "Many sins are forgiven her"] There is also that strong death of the holy martyrs, which does away former guilt; and therefore is it strong, and charity is not unequal to it, charity which is made equal to the passion of the martyrs, that it may take away the desert of offences.



## 11.

Zeal also is as hell, since he who has the zeal of God spares not even his own for Christ. And so charity has death, and charity has zeal, and charity has wings of fire. In fine, Christ, loving [1954D] Moses, appeared to him in fire. And Jeremiah, having in himself the gift of divine charity, said: *And there was a fire flaming in my bones, and I am undone on every side, and I cannot bear it [n: (Jerem. XX, 9)]*. [var: Vulg. *et factus est in corde meo quasi ignis exaestuans, claususque in ossibus meis: et defeci, ferre non sustinens*, "and it became in my heart as a burning fire, shut up in my bones: and I fainted, not able to bear it"] Good therefore is charity, having the wings of burning fire, which flits through the breasts and the hearts of the saints, and burns up whatever is material and earthly; but whatever is unmixed it proves, and what it has touched it makes better with its own fire.

## 12.

This fire the Lord Jesus sent upon the earth, and faith shone out, devotion was kindled, charity was lit up, justice shone again. With this fire he inflamed the hearts of his apostles, as Cleophas testifies, [1955A] saying: *Was not our heart burning within us, when he opened the Scriptures [n: (Luc. XXIV, 32)]*? The wings of fire in the flame, therefore, are the divine Scriptures. In fine, he was opening the Scriptures, and fire was going forth, and was piercing the hearts of them that heard. And they are true wings of fire, because: *The words of the Lord are chaste words, silver tried by the fire [n: (Psalm. XI, 7)]*. When Paul also was taken up by Christ, he saw a light shine round about upon him, and upon those who were with him; he fell from terror, and rose up the more approved. In fine he was made an apostle, who had come as a [sic: *presecutor*]. The Holy Spirit also came down, and filled the whole house in which very many were sitting, and there appeared parted tongues as it were of fire. Good wings, wings of charity, truly wings which flitted through the mouths of the apostles: and wings of fire, because they spoke a purified [1955B] speech.

## 13.

With these wings Enoch flew forth, caught up to heaven; with these wings Elias flew forth, carried over to the things above by a fiery chariot and by fiery horses. With these wings the Lord God led the people of the fathers by a pillar of fire. These wings the Seraphim had, when it took coals of fire from the altar, and touched the mouth of the prophet, and took away his iniquities, and purged his sins. By the fire of these wings the sons of Levi were purged, and the peoples of the nations were baptized, as John testifies, saying of the Lord Jesus: *He shall baptize you in the spirit and in fire [n: (Matth. III, 11)]*. [var: Vulg. *in Spiritu Sancto et igni*, "in the Holy Spirit and fire"] Deservedly did David wish his reins and his heart to be burned, because he knew that the fiery wings of charity are not to be feared. Deservedly did the Hebrew children in the burning furnace not feel the burnings of the fire, because the flame of charity [1955C] cooled them. And that we may know more fully *1615* that perfect charity has wings, thou hast heard him saying a while ago: *How often would I have gathered thy children together, as a hen gathers her chickens under her wings [n: (Matth. XXIII, 27)]*.

## 14.

Let us take therefore these wings, which as flames direct us to the things above. Let every man strip his soul of its filthier wrappings, and, as gold by fire, let him approve it wiped clean of the mire. For thus the soul is purged, as the best gold. But the beauty of the soul is unmixed virtue and comeliness, a truer knowledge of the things above; that it may see that good upon which all things hang, while itself hangs upon none. By it, therefore, it lives, and receives understanding. For that highest good is the fountain of life, the desire of which is kindled within us, to draw near [1955D] to which and to be mingled with it is a delight: which to him who does not see it is a matter of desire, and to him who sees it is life; and therefore he despises all other things, and by this one alone is soothed and delighted. This is that which supplies all substance; but itself, remaining in itself, gives to others, and takes nothing into itself from others; of which the Prophet says: *I said to the Lord: Thou art my God, for thou hast no need of my goods [n: (Psal. XV, 2)]*.

## 15.

*Its wings are wings of fire.* All must be proved by fire, whosoever desire to return to paradise; for not idly is it written that, when [1956A] Adam and Eve had been cast out of the seat of paradise, God set at the outgoing of paradise a fiery sword that turned about. All must pass through the flames, whether it be John the evangelist, whom the Lord so loved that he said of him to Peter: *If I will that he remain, what is that to thee? Follow thou me [n: (Joan. XXI, 22)]*. [var: Vulg. *Sic eum volo manere donec veniam, quid ad te?*, "So I will have him to remain till I come, what is it to thee?"] Some have doubted of his death; of his passing through fire we cannot doubt, because he is in paradise, nor is he separated from Christ. Whether it be Peter, who received the keys of the kingdom of heaven, who walked upon the sea, he must say: *We have passed through fire and water, and thou hast brought us out into a refreshment [n: (Psalm. LXV, 12)]*. But for John the fiery sword will quickly turn aside, because there is not found in him iniquity, whom equity loved. If there was in him anything of human vice, divine charity boiled it away: *For its wings [1956B] are as wings of fire.* He who here shall have had the fire of charity, there will not be able to fear the fire of the sword. To Peter himself, who so often offered his own death for Christ, he says: Pass over, recline. But he says: *He has tried us by fire, as he himself has tried silver.*

## 16.

*Its wings are wings of fire.* Since therefore the power of flying has been given to us, let every man stir up in himself the grace of God, and, forgetting the things behind, seeking after the things before, let him press on toward what is appointed. Far from the honors of the soldiery, far from the heats of the world; lest, as the fables relate, the wax loosed by the heat of the sun, the feather slipping back, fail the [sic: *lcareos*] flights. For although the weightiness of their sayings be against it, yet with poetic salt they meant to declare that flights through heaven are safe for the maturity of the prudent; but that youthful levity, liable to the desires of the world, its feathers flowing back, [1956C] and the framework of its merits loosened through the forgettings of truth, slips back to the earth with the greater ruin.

## 17.

Flight is by no means easy for all; the course of human life is difficult also for the living creatures that are at discord upon the earth. But if the order of our action agree with thee, the prophet will see even in us that one wheel upon the earth, joined to the four living creatures. Ezechiel therefore will see again — for he still sees, and is in vigor, and will be in vigor [n: (Ezech. I, 15 et 16)] — he will see, I say, a wheel in the midst of a wheel, gliding upon the earth without stumbling. For the wheel upon the earth is the life of the body, fitted to the virtue of the soul, and formed with a coherent course to the precept of the Gospel. But the wheel in the midst of the wheel is as it were a life within a life, in that the life of the saints [1956D] is not out of tune with itself; but such as it was in the earlier age, such let it be also in that which follows: or that in this life of the body the practice of eternal life be turned round. When these things shall have agreed together, then let the divine voice sound back, then above the likeness of the throne a likeness as the appearance of a man will appear. This man is the Word, because *the Word was made flesh [n: (Joan. I, 14)]*: this man is the driver of our living creatures, the ruler of our manners, who, according to the measure of our merits, mounts now for the most part a chariot, now a mountain, now a ship.

## 18.

*Set me as a seal in thy heart, as a signet [1957A] on thy arm, thou who hast kept new things and old for me.* Thou art my seal, thou art unto my image and likeness. In thee shines the image of justice, the image of wisdom, the image of virtue. And because the image of God is in thy heart, let it be also in thy works: let the likeness of the Gospel be in thy deeds, that in thy manners thou mayest keep my precepts. The likeness of the Gospel will be in thee, *1616* if to him that strikes thy cheek thou offer the other, if thou love thine enemy, if thou take up thy cross and follow me. Therefore have I carried the cross for thee, lest thou shouldst hesitate to carry it for my sake. The daughters of Jerusalem heard this, that the Lord God was now joining the Church to himself: and because, considering the greatness of the Word, they esteemed themselves unequal to so great a marriage, lest perchance they might not be able to bear the weight of so great a union, they excuse themselves, saying: [1957B]

## 19.

*[n: (Vers. 8.)] Our sister is a little one, and she has no breasts.* [var: Vulg. *Soror nostra parva*, "Our sister is little" — without the diminutive] For thus they who wish to put off a marriage are wont to excuse it: that they allege the weakness of unripe age, and assert that she has no breasts, which signify the time of marriageable age. This token is wont to be common to all virgins about to wed: that when the breasts have begun to stand out, then they are judged fit for union. Troubled therefore, because in the zeal of love the Bridegroom urges on the marriage, they say:



## 20.

*What shall we do to our sister, in the day in which he shall speak in her,* or, as Symmachus has it, *in which he shall speak to her?* [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:8 *Quid faciemus sorori nostrae in die quando alloquenda est?*, "What shall we do to our sister in the day when she is to be spoken to?"] That is: in the solemnity of a betrothal a conference is wont to take place, and a confirmation of the marriage. What then shall we do, they say, being troubled, because the spiritual union presses upon us? They cannot excuse themselves from so great a marriage; for there is no one [1957C] who does not think the union either of the soul and the spirit, or of Christ and the Church, a blessed thing. But because the fulness of the Word and of the Holy Spirit quivers and flashes, and there is nothing that can be made equal to them, therefore they desire to defer it, that by that postponement either the soul or the Church may be able to be more perfect. They say therefore:

## 21.

*[n: (Vers. 9.)] If she is a wall, let us build upon her chambers of silver: and if she is a gate, let us carve upon her boards of cedar.* [var: Vulg. *Si murus est, aedificemus super eum propugnacula argentea; si ostium est, compingamus illud tabulis cedrinis*, "If she be a wall, let us build upon it bulwarks of silver: if she be a door, let us join it together with boards of cedar"] The soul of a holy man is a wall. The Church too has her walls, who, being now more perfect, says: *I am a fortified city [n: (Esai. XXVII, 3)]*. [var: Vulg. Isa. 27:3 *Ego Dominus qui servo eam*, "I am the Lord that keep it"; the reading here follows the Greek] This is the wall which has twelve apostolic gates, through which entrance into the Church lies open to the people of the nations. But a wall, although it enclose the circuit of the whole city, is then [1957D] the better fortified when it has had chambers made ready, in which the defenders of the city may have a safe resource for watching and for guarding. But because this city is rational, and all its hope is in the Word of God, not iron but silver bulwarks are required for it, accustomed to beat back the assaults of the enemy by heavenly sayings rather than by bodily pleasures.

## 22.

Propped by that defence, shining with that splendor, she is judged the fitter for the union with Christ. And because Christ is the gate, who says: *If any man enter in by me, he shall be saved (Joan. [1958A] X, 9)*; the church too is called a gate, because through her the approach to salvation lies open to the peoples. But lest she be corrupted by the moths or worms of the heretics, the daughters of Jerusalem, or the angels, or the souls of the just, say: *Let us build upon her boards of cedar,* that is, the good odor of a sublime faith. For the odor of this timber is sweet, and neither worm nor moth corrupts it. Therefore the use of this timber is chosen for raising the peaks of roofs, and for shaping the elements of letters, with which the age of boyhood is steeped in the study of liberal learning. This timber, then, is sublime for grace, light for burden, sweet for odor, useful as an instrument of knowledge, apt for the ministry of eternal knowledge. But even as Christ, loving his Bride, was urging her toward the solemnity of the spiritual union, so also the Church, now taken with the comeliness of the Word, was hastening [1958B] to the marriage. And so, impatient of the delays and of the postponement which the daughters of Jerusalem were eager to weave in, she says:

## 23.

*[n: (Vers. 10.)] I am a wall, and my breasts are towers.* [var: Vulg. *Ego murus, et ubera mea sicut turris*, "I am a wall, and my breasts are as a tower"] That is: do not doubt whether I am a wall; for they had said: *If she is a wall;* I, she says, am a wall, and I have not small breasts, but my breasts are as towers. How do you say that I have no breasts? I have senses as towers of wisdom, in which there is abundance, as it is written: *And abundance in thy towers [n: (Psalm. CXXI, 7)]*. By these breasts, that is, by her senses, she esteemed herself fit for so great a marriage. But the daughters of Jerusalem could not yet so esteem her, because they did not see the abundance of her senses. And she added: [1958C]

## 24.

*[n: (Vers. 11.)] I was in his eyes as one finding peace;* [var: Vulg. Cant. 8:10 *ex quo facta sum coram eo, quasi pacem reperiens*, "since I am become in his presence as one finding peace"] that is: my senses being deliberated upon, since I have found the peace of God, which surpasses all understanding, and keeps hearts and senses in Christ Jesus. Such, she says, was I in the eyes of the bridegroom, as one who has peace; for it is written: *They who rightly seek peace shall rejoice.* *1617*

## 25.

*[n: (Vers 12.)] I,* she says, *am a fortified city, I am a besieged city [n: (Esai. XXVII, 3)]*. By these walls she is fortified, by these, when besieged, she is defended. And truly the soul is a wall, which is stretched out before the camp. Whence she herself also says in the Song: *I am a wall, and my breasts are as towers [n: (Vers. 10)]*. A good wall is that which the Lord has painted, as he himself says: *In my hands I have painted thy walls, and thou art always in my sight [n: (Esai. XLIX, 16)]*. [var: Vulg. *Ecce in manibus meis descripsi te; muri tui coram oculis meis semper*, "Behold, I have graven thee in my hands: thy walls are always before my eyes"] Good is the soul which has [1958D] God as its watchman, and is in his hands; as the prophetic soul, which is commended into the hands of God as a spirit [n: (Psal. XXX, 6)], and which is in the sight of God: *For the eyes of the Lord are upon the just;* as he himself also says: *I was in his eyes as one finding peace.* She has good towers, who has both a word concerning things intelligible and a discipline concerning things moral. The beloved and the beloved one therefore hastening, the spiritual union was celebrated, sought by mutual consent. And so, singing as it were a marriage song, the Spirit exulted in the prophet, saying: [1959A]

## 26.

*[n: (Vers. 11.)] A vineyard was made for Solomon in Beelamon; he gave his vineyard to those who keep it.* [var: Vulg. *Vinea fuit pacifico in ea quae habet populos: tradidit eam custodibus*, "The peaceable had a vineyard in that which hath people: he let out the same to keepers"] The Spirit therefore cries out: A congregation of peoples has been planted, but founded upon the root of the eternal vine; and it has subjected spiritual hearts, with a meek heart, under the yoke of the Word. And it was planted in a multitude of nations. For that *Beelamon* is to be understood thus, Symmachus, Aquila, and the other translations have taught in the Greek tongue. The old union, which could not bring forth fruit, was repudiated; the vineyard was given to new and faithful tillers, who could not only make fruit, but also keep it. One sheep therefore went astray, but being called back it filled the spaces of the whole world. Error had led away one sheep, but the grace of the Lord gathered together a multitude of peoples. Man went astray, but in the Church he is now a wall, [1959B] and a strong wall. Adam went astray; David is a wall, who did not forget the commandments of God. This vineyard, therefore, being guarded and walled about with a spiritual fortification, gives a thousand fruits to Christ, but two hundred fruits to the keepers; and so the Church says:

## 27.

*[n: (Vers. 12.)] My vine is in my sight: a thousand for Solomon, and two hundred for those that keep the fruit.* [var: Vulg. *Vinea mea coram me est. Mille tui pacifici, et ducenti his qui custodiunt fructus ejus*, "My vineyard is before me. A thousand are for thee, the peaceable, and two hundred for them that keep the fruit thereof"] The perfection and fulness of Christ is the portion of little ones. Thou hast this mystery in Genesis, where Joseph assigned five portions to Benjamin the younger brother, and single portions to the remaining brethren [n: (Gen. XLIII, 34)]. To the Lord therefore is offered the portion and the prerogative of the five senses, which he himself whom he loves assuredly assigns to him, as he loved Paul also, to whom he gave the chief place of wisdom for the calling forth of the nations. Let fear depart here from the holy virgins, to whom the Church first assigns so great [1959C] defences, she who, anxious for the increase of her tender offspring, herself as a wall grows up with breasts abounding after the manner of towers: until, the assault of the hostile siege being broken up, she may win peace for a strong youth by the defence of a mother's strength. Whence the Prophet also says: *Let peace be in thy strength, and abundance in thy towers [n: (Psalm. CXXI, 7)]*.

## 28.

*[n: (Vers. 13.)] Thou that sittest in the gardens, the friends listening to thy voice, make thy voice known to me.* [var: Vulg. *Quae habitas in hortis, amici auscultant; fac me audire vocem tuam*, "Thou that dwellest in the gardens, the friends hearken; make me hear thy voice" — the Vulgate's addressee is feminine, Migne's masculine] And so this soul, having the grace of her own breasts, enters into the gardens, and finding there the Bridegroom sitting and disputing with his friends, says: *Thou that sittest in the gardens, make thy voice known to me:* to me, she says, not to the friends.

## 29.

*Thou that sittest in the gardens, the friends listening to thy voice, make thy voice known to me.* For she was delighted [1959D] that Christ was sitting in the gardens, and that the friends set in the gardens were listening to his voice: but because those friends were of the heavenly ones, Archangels or Dominations and Thrones (for men had been driven out of paradise on account of disobedience to the heavenly commandments, and therefore the Church could not yet hear his voice, which she desired to hear), therefore she says: *Make thy voice known to me.* Whence we also, if we wish him to sit within us, let us be gardens enclosed and fortified, let us bear the flowers of the virtues, the sweetness of grace, that we may be able to hear the Lord Jesus disputing [1960A] with the angels.
